of a symmetry group
G
and for any, possibly non-Gaussian, state
\hat{\rho}
with displacement vector
\boldsymbol{d}
, define the integer

f_{\mu}(\hat{\rho})\coloneqq{\rm rank}\left(T^{(\mu)}\left(\boldsymbol{d}\boldsymbol{d}^{\dagger}\right)\right)\,.

(65)

The monotonicity of this quantity under covariant Gaussian channels follows immediately from Eq.(
48
).
Since the support of
T^{(\mu)}(\boldsymbol{d}_{B}\boldsymbol{d}_{B}^{{\dagger}})
for the output state is contained in the support of
T^{(\mu)}(\boldsymbol{d}_{A}\boldsymbol{d}_{A}^{\dagger})
for the input state, its dimension cannot be larger.

It is also clear that
f_{\mu}
is an integer bounded by
d_{\mu}
, the dimension of irrep
\mu
.
In particular, for Abelian groups, it can only take values
0
or
1
, indicating whether or not the first moment of the state has a component in a mode that supports irrep
\mu
.

V.3
Type–2 asymmetry monotones

Here, we present a general recipe for constructing type–2 Gaussian asymmetry monotones, based on the notion of type–1 asymmetry–destroying maps.
One can define the type–1 asymmetry-destroying map
\mathcal{D}^{(1)}_{\boldsymbol{d}}:(\boldsymbol{d},\sigma)\mapsto(\boldsymbol{0},\sigma)
that removes the first moment
\boldsymbol{d}
of a state
\hat{\rho}
.
This map explicitly depends on input
\boldsymbol{d}
and can be realized as a displacement
\mathcal{D}^{(1)}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\hat{\rho})=\hat{D}_{-\boldsymbol{d}}\hat{\rho}\hat{D}_{\boldsymbol{d}}
.

Consider a Gaussian
G
–covariant channel
\mathcal{E}
whose action in phase space is described by
X,Y,\boldsymbol{\xi}
according to Eq.(
7
), such that
\mathcal{E}(\hat{\rho}_{1})=\hat{\rho}_{2}
for states
\hat{\rho}_{1},\hat{\rho}_{2}
with first moments
\boldsymbol{d}_{1},\boldsymbol{d}_{2}
.
We can fully remove the type–1 asymmetry in the input/output states by applying appropriate displacements, obtaining states
\mathcal{D}^{(1)}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{1}}(\hat{\rho}_{1})
and
\mathcal{D}^{(1)}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{2}}(\hat{\rho}_{2})
with vanishing first moments.
Then, the Gaussian
G
–covariant channel
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
described by
X,Y,\boldsymbol{0}
according to Eq.(
7
), maps
\mathcal{D}^{(1)}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{1}}(\hat{\rho}_{1})
to
\mathcal{D}^{(1)}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{2}}(\hat{\rho}_{2})
, as depicted
7
7

7

One can similarly define a type–2 asymmetry-destroying map that sends the covariance matrix
\sigma
of a state
\hat{\rho}
to a fixed covariance matrix that respects the symmetry such as the identity
I
for passive representations.
However, in general, one cannot guarantee the existence of a covariant channel
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
that maps the transformed input state to the corresponding transformed output state, as depicted in Fig.
4
(for instance, this is the case for 1-mode phase-insensitive amplifiers with
\operatorname{U}(1)
symmetry).
in Fig.
4
.

Given an asymmetry monotone
f
, we can construct a type–2 asymmetry monotone
f^{(2)}:\hat{\rho}\mapsto f(\mathcal{D}^{(1)}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\hat{\rho}))
, where
\mathcal{D}^{(1)}_{\boldsymbol{d}}
is the type–1 asymmetry-destroying map that depends on the first moment
\boldsymbol{d}
of input state
\hat{\rho}
.
Then, using the notation of Fig.
4
,

f(\mathcal{D}^{(1)}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{2}}(\mathcal{E}(\hat{\rho}_{1})))=f(\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(\mathcal{D}^{(1)}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{1}}(\hat{\rho}_{1})))\leq f(\mathcal{D}^{(1)}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{1}}(\hat{\rho}_{1}))\,,

(66)

because
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
is a
G
–covariant operation.
We proceed to give concrete examples of type–2 asymmetry monotones in the next section.

Figure 4:
Type–1 asymmetry
.
Suppose a
G
–covariant Gaussian channel
\mathcal{E}
sends an input state
\hat{\rho}_{1}
to an output state
\hat{\rho}_{2}
.
Then, there exists a
G
–covariant Gaussian channel
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
that maps
\hat{\rho}^{\prime}_{1}=\mathcal{D}^{(1)}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\hat{\rho}_{1})
to
\hat{\rho}^{\prime}_{2}=\mathcal{D}^{(1)}_{\boldsymbol{d}}(\hat{\rho}_{2})
.

V.4
Petz-Rényi relative entropy of asymmetry

Here, we give a recipe for deriving Gaussian asymmetry monotones and corresponding type–2 asymmetry monotones, providing some examples that take explicit closed forms for Gaussian states.

Given any divergence
D
and any group element
g\in G
, the quantity
f_{g}(\hat{\rho})\coloneqq D(\hat{\rho}\|\hat{S}(g)\hat{\rho}\hat{S}^{\dagger}(g))
is an asymmetry monotone for unitary representation
\hat{S}
of
G
.
Concretely, for any
\hat{\rho}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
and
G
–covariant
\mathcal{E}:\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})\rightarrow\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
,

\displaystyle f_{g}(\mathcal{E}(\hat{\rho}))

\displaystyle=D(\mathcal{E}(\hat{\rho})\|\mathcal{E}(\hat{S}(g)\hat{\rho}\hat{S}^{\dagger}(g)))

\displaystyle\leq D(\hat{\rho}\|\hat{S}(g)\hat{\rho}\hat{S}^{\dagger}(g))=f_{g}(\hat{\rho})\,.

(67)

where the first equality follows because
\mathcal{E}
is covariant and the inequality is the data-processing inequality that divergence
D
needs to satisfy.

Consider the Petz-Rényi
\alpha
–divergence
Petz 1986
,

D_{\alpha}(\hat{\rho}\|\hat{\tau})\coloneqq\frac{1}{\alpha-1}\log{\rm{tr}}[\hat{\rho}^{\alpha}\hat{\tau}^{1-\alpha}]\,,

(68)

defined for
\alpha\in(0,1)\cup(1,\infty)
.
This quantity has been calculated explicitly
Seshadreesan et al. 2018
in terms of the displacement vector and covariance matrix of any two Gaussian states
\hat{\rho},\hat{\tau}
, for
\alpha\in(0,1)
.
For our purposes, consider
f_{\alpha,g}\coloneqq D_{\alpha}(\hat{\rho}\|\hat{S}(g)\hat{\rho}\hat{S}(g)^{\dagger})
, where
\hat{\rho}
has first moment
\boldsymbol{d}
and covariance matrix
\sigma
.
We find in Appendix
C.6
that

\displaystyle f_{\alpha,g}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\left(f_{\alpha,g}^{(1)}(\hat{\rho})+f_{\alpha,g}^{(2)}(\hat{\rho})\right)\,,

(69)

\displaystyle f_{\alpha,g}^{(1)}(\hat{\rho})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\boldsymbol{d}_{g}^{\rm T}\left(\sigma_{\alpha}+S(g)\sigma_{1-\alpha}S(g)^{\rm T}\right)^{-1}\boldsymbol{d}_{g}\,,

(70)

\displaystyle f_{\alpha,g}^{(2)}(\hat{\rho})

\displaystyle\coloneqq-\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{{\rm{det}}[(\sigma_{\alpha}+\sigma_{1-\alpha})/2]}{{\rm{det}}\left[\left(\sigma_{\alpha}+S(g)\sigma_{1-\alpha}S(g)^{\rm T}\right)/2\right]}\,,

(71)

where
\boldsymbol{d}_{g}\coloneqq(I-S(g))\boldsymbol{d}
is non-zero if
S(g)\neq I
, and

\sigma_{\alpha}\coloneqq\coth(\alpha\ {\rm coth}^{-1}(\sigma i\Omega))i\Omega\,,

(72)

for
\alpha\in(0,1)
, is the covariance matrix of
\hat{\rho}^{\alpha}/{\rm{tr}}[\hat{\rho}^{\alpha}]
, i.e. the thermal state of Hamiltonian
\hat{H}
at temperature
(\alpha\beta)^{-1}
, where
\hat{\rho}
is the thermal state of Hamiltonian
\hat{H}
at temperature
\beta^{-1}
.
This follows from the well-known relation
\sigma i\Omega=\coth\left(\frac{1}{2}i\Omega H\right)
between the covariance matrix
\sigma
of state
\hat{\rho}
and the Hamiltonian matrix
H
of
\hat{H}
.
Inserting this relation in Eq.(
72
) gives
\sigma_{\alpha}i\Omega=\coth\left(\frac{1}{2}i\Omega(\alpha H)\right)
.

Note that if
\hat{\rho}
is pure, so is
\hat{\rho}^{\alpha}/{\rm{tr}}[\hat{\rho}^{\alpha}]
and
\sigma_{\alpha}=\sigma
for all
\alpha\in(0,1)
.
Then, Eqs.(
70
,
71
) simplify to

\displaystyle f_{\alpha,g}^{(1)}(\hat{\rho})

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{d}_{g}^{\rm T}\left(\sigma+S(g)\sigma S(g)^{\rm T}\right)^{-1}\boldsymbol{d}_{g}\,,

(73)

\displaystyle f_{\alpha,g}^{(2)}(\hat{\rho})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\log{\rm{det}}\left[\left(\sigma+S(g)\sigma S(g)^{\rm T}\right)/2\right]\,.

(74)

We emphasize that the expressions in Eqs.(
69
–
71
,
73
,
74
) are not valid for non-Gaussian states.

Under closed covariant dynamics, the monotone
f_{\alpha,g}
is conserved, since Ineq.(
67
) holds as equality.
If the dynamics is Gaussian, both
f_{\alpha,g}^{(1)},f_{\alpha,g}^{(2)}
are independently conserved, i.e. for any invariant Gaussian unitary
\hat{U}
, it is evident that
f_{\alpha,g}^{(k)}(\hat{U}\hat{\rho}\hat{U}^{\dagger})=f_{\alpha,g}^{(k)}(\hat{\rho})
, for
k=1,2
.

Under open dynamics, the quantity
f_{\alpha,g}^{(2)}
is a monotone for type–2 asymmetry due to Eq.(
66
).
We can rewrite
f_{\alpha,g}^{(2)}
for
\alpha=1/2
as follows,

\displaystyle f_{1/2,\hskip 1.0ptg}^{(2)}(\hat{\rho})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{{\rm{det}}[\sigma_{1/2}]}{{\rm{det}}[\left(\sigma_{1/2}+S(g)\sigma_{1/2}S(g)^{\rm T}\right)\hskip-2.0pt/2]}

(75)

\displaystyle=\log(\frac{\tr[\hat{\rho}_{1/2}^2]}{\tr\left[\D_{\bmd}^{(1)}(\hat{\rho}_{1/2})\D_{S(g)\bmd}^{(1)}(\hat{S}(g)\hat{\rho}_{1/2}\hat{S}(g)^\dag)\right]})\,,

which expresses the ratio of the purity of state
\hat{\rho}_{1/2}
over the overlap between state
\hat{\rho}_{1/2}
with removed displacement vector and its rotation by
g\in G
.

Consider the
\operatorname{U}(1)
symmetry generated by a purely quadratic observable
\widehat{Q}
, given by
\hat{S}(t)=e^{i\widehat{Q}t}
with associated symplectic matrix
S(t)=e^{-\Omega Qt}
.
In Appendix
C.7
, we show that, for this continuous symmetry, we obtain additional monotones of Gaussian asymmetry and type–2 asymmetry as the second derivatives
F_{Q}
of
f_{1/2,t}
and
F_{Q}^{(2)}
of
f_{1/2,t}^{(2)}
, respectively.
In particular,

\displaystyle F_{Q}

\displaystyle\coloneqq F_{Q}^{(1)}+F_{Q}^{(2)}

(76)

\displaystyle F_{Q}^{(1)}(\hat{\rho})

\displaystyle\coloneqq-\boldsymbol{d}^{\rm T}Q\Omega\sigma_{1/2}^{-1}\Omega Q\boldsymbol{d}\,,

(77)

\displaystyle F_{Q}^{(2)}(\hat{\rho})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{1}{4}{\rm{tr}}\Big[\Omega Q\Omega\sigma_{1/2}^{-1}[\Omega Q,\sigma_{1/2}\Omega]\Big]\,.

(78)

Note that if
Q
commutes with
\Omega
, then
F_{Q}^{(2)}(\hat{\rho})
attains a zero value for every state with
\sigma_{1/2}=I
, e.g. the vacuum.
On the contrary, if
Q
does not commute with
\Omega
, then the representation is not passive (see Fig.
2
), so, for example, the vacuum is no longer a free state.

As an example, consider free evolution with frequency
\omega
, i.e.
Q=\omega I
, on 1 mode.
For a coherent state
\ket{\gamma}
, we have
f_{\alpha,t}^{(2)}(\ket{\gamma})=0
, and

f_{\alpha,t}(\ket{\gamma})=f_{\alpha,t}^{(1)}(\ket{\gamma})=2(1-\cos(\omega t))|\gamma|^{2}\,,

(79)

for all
\alpha\in(0,1)
.
Therefore,
F_{Q}^{(2)}(\ket{\gamma})=0
, and

F_{Q}(\ket{\gamma})=F_{Q}^{(1)}(\ket{\gamma})=2\omega^{2}|\gamma|^{2}\,.

(80)

For a squeezed state
\ket{r}
, we have
f_{\alpha,t}^{(1)}(\ket{r})=0
, and

f_{\alpha,t}=f_{\alpha,t}^{(2)}(\ket{r})=\frac{1}{2}\log\left(1+\sin^{2}(\omega t)\sinh^{2}(2r)\right)\,,

(81)

for all
\alpha\in(0,1)
.
Therefore,
F_{Q}^{(1)}(\ket{r})=0
and

F_{Q}(\ket{r})=F_{Q}^{(2)}(\ket{r})=\omega^{2}\sinh^{2}(2r)\,.

(82)

VI
Conservation Laws in closed Gaussian systems

It is well known that, in the case of closed systems, symmetries of the dynamics imply conservation laws, a result often colloquially referred to as Noether’s theorem
Noether 1918
.
For instance, in the presence of rotational
\operatorname{SU}(2)
symmetry, the angular momentum operators remain conserved. More generally, for a symmetry group
G
, if an initial state
\hat{\rho}
evolves under a symmetry-respecting unitary into a final state
\hat{\rho}^{\prime}
, then we have

{\rm{tr}}[\hat{\rho}\hat{U}(g)]={\rm{tr}}[\hat{\rho}^{\prime}\hat{U}(g)]\,,\quad\text{for all }g\in G\,.

(83)

If the group is generated by an observable
\widehat{Q}
such that
\hat{U}(s)=e^{i\widehat{Q}s}
,
s\in\mathbb{R}
, we find that, for arbitrary integer
k
,

{\rm{tr}}[\hat{\rho}\widehat{Q}^{k}]={\rm{tr}}[\hat{\rho}^{\prime}\widehat{Q}^{k}]\,.

(84)

In the aforementioned example of
\operatorname{SU}(2)
symmetry, this equation implies that all expectation values of polynomials of angular momentum operators remain conserved.

When
\hat{\rho}
is a pure state, Eq.(
83
) captures all the implications of the symmetry in the following sense: if this conservation law holds for all
g\in G
, then there exists a unitary transformation
\hat{V}
that respects the symmetry, i.e.
[\hat{V},\hat{U}(g)]=0
for all
g\in G
, and converts between the two states
\hat{\rho}^{\prime}=\hat{V}\hat{\rho}\hat{V}^{\dagger}

Marvian and Spekkens 2013
8
8

8

It is worth noting that this does not generally hold when
\hat{\rho}
is mixed; in fact, the conservation of measures of asymmetry imposes independent conservation laws on the dynamics of the system that are not captured by Eq.(
83
)
Marvian and Spekkens 2014b
.
.

Clearly, if the dynamics of a closed system that respects the symmetry is Gaussian, they satisfy the conservation laws in Eqs.(
83
,
84
). This naturally raises the following question: Are there conservation laws that are satisfied by Gaussian closed systems that respect the symmetry, but are not necessarily satisfied by non-Gaussian closed systems that respect the symmetry? In other words, does the presence of symmetries in Gaussian systems impose any additional constraints on the dynamics? In the following, we show that the answer is affirmative, and we present a class of such conservation laws.

The intuition is simple: conservation laws of the form given in Eq.(
84
) only consider the total amount of charge in the system. However, roughly speaking, the conserved charge and asymmetry can be decomposed into different components corresponding to central moments of different order, which do not mix under Gaussian dynamics that respect the symmetry. Therefore, by analyzing the contributions to charge and asymmetry in these different central moments, one can identify new independent conserved quantities.

VI.1
Conservation laws for closed Gaussian systems

Suppose that under a Gaussian unitary evolution described by
\hat{V}
, an arbitrary, possibly non-Gaussian, initial state
\hat{\rho}_{1}
, with displacement vector
\boldsymbol{d}_{1}
and covariance matrix
\sigma_{1}
, evolves to the final state
\hat{\rho}_{2}=\hat{V}\hat{\rho}_{1}\hat{V}^{\dagger}
, with displacement vector
\boldsymbol{d}_{2}
and covariance matrix
\sigma_{2}
.
Suppose further that
\hat{V}
is invariant under a symplectic Gaussian representation of symmetry
e^{ir\widehat{Q}}
with quadratic charge

\widehat{Q}=\sum_{j,k}Q_{jk}\hat{r}_{j}\hat{r}_{k}\,,\quad Q=Q^{\rm T}\,,

(85)

for all
r\in\mathbb{R}
, which corresponds to representation
e^{-r\Omega Q}
in the phase space.
Then,

\boldsymbol{d}_{1}^{\rm T}Q\boldsymbol{d}_{1}=\boldsymbol{d}_{2}^{\rm T}Q\boldsymbol{d}_{2}\,,\quad\quad{\rm{tr}}[\sigma_{1}Q]={\rm{tr}}[\sigma_{2}Q]\,.

(86)

Crucially, while in a Gaussian charge-conserving closed system each of these quantities is individually conserved, this is not generally the case in non-Gaussian charge-conserving closed systems. We provide a counter-example in Section
VI.2
.
Instead, due to Eq.(
84
) for
k=1
,
{\rm{tr}}[\hat{\rho}_{1}\widehat{Q}]={\rm{tr}}[\hat{\rho}_{2}\widehat{Q}]
, which, in turn, implies the following linear combination remains conserved,

{\rm{tr}}\left[\sigma_{1}Q\right]+2\boldsymbol{d}_{1}^{\rm T}Q\boldsymbol{d}_{1}={\rm{tr}}\left[\sigma_{2}Q\right]+2\boldsymbol{d}_{2}^{\rm T}Q\boldsymbol{d}_{2}\,.

(87)

To derive more general conservation laws, we first establish the following simple result.

Lemma 10
.

Consider the function

F_{B}(A)={\rm{tr}}[f(A\Omega,\Omega B)]\,,

(88)

where
A
and
B
are
(2n\times 2n)
matrices, and
f
is any arbitrary non-commutative polynomial.
Under any symplectic transformation
V\in\operatorname{Sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
that commutes with
\Omega B
, i.e.
[V,\Omega B]=0
, we have

F_{B}(A)=F_{B}(VAV^{\rm T})\,.

(89)

Proof.

For any invertible matrix
V
,

\displaystyle{\rm{tr}}[f(A\Omega,\Omega B)]

\displaystyle={\rm{tr}}[Vf(A\Omega,\Omega B)V^{-1}]

(90)

\displaystyle={\rm{tr}}[f(VA\Omega V^{-1},V\Omega BV^{-1})]

(91)

\displaystyle={\rm{tr}}(f(VAV^{\rm T}\Omega,\Omega B))\,,

(92)

where the second equality follows from the property
Vf(X,Y)V^{-1}=f(VXV^{-1},VYV^{-1})
of non-commutative polynomials, and the third from the assumptions that
[V,\Omega B]=0
and that
V
is symplectic.
∎

For instance, choosing
f(X,Y)=(XY)^{k}
, for integer
k
, and
A=\sigma
, we obtain
F_{B}(A)={\rm{tr}}[(AB)^{k}]
.
Then, assuming
V
commutes with
\Omega Q
for
Q
in Eq.(
85
), choosing
B=Q
leads to the conserved quantity
F_{Q}(A)={\rm{tr}}\left[(AQ)^{k}\right]
, which in turn implies the conservation law

{\rm{tr}}\left[(\sigma_{1}Q)^{k}\right]={\rm{tr}}\left[(\sigma_{2}Q)^{k}\right]\,.

(93)

Similarly, if the Gaussian unitary
\hat{V}
respects a group
G
with symplectic representation
S:G\rightarrow\operatorname{Sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
, then choosing
B=-\Omega S(g)
yields the conservation law

{\rm{tr}}\left[(\sigma_{1}\Omega S(g))^{k}\right]={\rm{tr}}\left[(\sigma_{2}\Omega S(g))^{k}\right]\,.

(94)

for every group element
g\in G
.

Example:
\operatorname{SU}(2)
symmetry and conservation of angular momentum

Recall the
\operatorname{SU}(2)
example with the angular operators defined in Eq.(
27
).
Let
\boldsymbol{d}_{j,a}
and
\sigma_{j,a}
denote the displacement vector and covariance matrix of mode
a
in subsystem
j
, and let
\boldsymbol{d}_{j,b}
and
\sigma_{j,b}
denote the displacement vector and covariance matrix of mode
b
in subsystem
j
.

The expectation value
{\rm{tr}}[\hat{\rho}\hat{J}_{z}]
remains conserved under general
\operatorname{SU}(2)
–invariant dynamics, and, if the dynamics is also Gaussian, Eq.(
86
) implies the independent conservation of the following two quantities,

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(\boldsymbol{d}_{j,a}^{\rm T}\boldsymbol{d}_{j,a}-\boldsymbol{d}_{j,b}^{\rm T}\boldsymbol{d}_{j,b}\right)\,,\quad\sum_{j=1}^{n}\Big({\rm{tr}}[\sigma_{j,a}]-{\rm{tr}}[\sigma_{j,b}]\Big)\,.

(95)

VI.2
Violation of conservation laws
in non-Gaussian closed systems

Consider a single mode with
\operatorname{U}(1)
representation
\exp({i\phi\hat{a}^\dag\hat{a}})
,
\phi\in[0,2\pi)
, which corresponds to the conserved charge
\widehat{Q}=\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a}
and
Q=I_{2}
is the
(2\times 2)
identity matrix.
Consider a system initially in the pure coherent state
\ket{\alpha}\coloneqq\hat{D}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{1}}\ket{0}
, with first moment
\boldsymbol{d}_{1}\coloneqq\sqrt{2}({\rm Re}(\alpha),{\rm Im}(\alpha))^{\rm T}
, and covariance matrix
\sigma_{1}=I_{2}
, and suppose it evolves to state
\hat{U}\ket{\alpha}
, where
\hat{U}=\hat{I}-2\outerproduct{0}{0}
is a non-Gaussian U(1)–invariant unitary. Under this transformation, the state’s displacement vector and covariance matrix become

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{d}_{2}

\displaystyle=c\boldsymbol{d}_{1}\,,

(96)

\displaystyle\sigma_{2}

\displaystyle=(1+(1-c)\boldsymbol{d}_{1}^{\rm T}\boldsymbol{d}_{1})I_{2}+2c(1-c)\boldsymbol{d}_{1}\boldsymbol{d}_{1}^{\rm T}\,,

(97)

where
c\coloneqq 1-2e^{-|\alpha|^{2}}
.
Although the conservation law in Eq.(
87
) still holds, i.e.

{\rm{tr}}\left[\left(\sigma_{k}+2\boldsymbol{d}_{k}\boldsymbol{d}_{k}^{\rm T}\right)Q\right]=2(1+2|\alpha|^{2})\,,

(98)

for
k=1,2
, the conservation laws in Eq.(
86
) are not satisfied for non-zero displacement
\alpha
.
It is worth noting that a non-zero displacement at the input causes the output covariance matrix to acquire off-diagonal elements.
As a result, it no longer commutes with
\Omega_{1}
, and so, unlike the input covariance matrix, it breaks the
\operatorname{U}(1)
symmetry.

VI.3
A complete set of conservation laws for incoherent states

An important case of interest is when the symmetry is represented by a passive quadratic charge
\widehat{Q}
.
Let
Q=\sum_{q\in{\rm Eig}(Q)}q\Pi_{q}
be the spectral decomposition of the symmetric matrix
Q
, where
\Pi_{q}
is the projector on the modes
\{j:q_{j}=q\}
with charge
q
.
Then, using Lemma
10
, one can directly obtain the conservation laws

{\rm{tr}}\left[(\sigma_{1}(\Pi_{q}-\Pi_{-q}))^{k}\right]={\rm{tr}}\left[(\sigma_{2}(\Pi_{q}-\Pi_{-q}))^{k}\right]\,,

(99)

for
|q|>0
and all integers
k
.
To see this, first note that
according to Proposition
1
,
[Q,\Omega]=0
when
\widehat{Q}
is passive.
Thus, any symplectic matrix that commutes with
\Omega Q
, also commutes with
Q^{2}=-(Q\Omega)^{2}
, hence also with projectors to the eigenspaces of
Q^{2}
, namely
\Pi_{q}+\Pi_{-q}
for
q\in{\rm Eig}(Q)
.
This holds because each
\Pi_{q}+\Pi_{-q}
can be written as a polynomial
p_{q}
of
Q^{2}
, i.e.

\Pi_{q}+\Pi_{-q}=p_{q}(-(Q\Omega)^{2})=p_{q}(Q^{2})\,.

(100)

It follows that by choosing
f(X,Y)=(XYp_{q}(-Y^{2}))^{k}
in Proposition
10
, and
A=\sigma
and
B=Q
, we obtain the above conservation laws.

The conservation laws in Eq.(
99
) only restrict sectors with
q\neq 0
.
In the sectors with
q=0
, the symmetry does not impose any restrictions.
Nevertheless, the fact that the dynamics is closed and Gaussian imposes non-trivial constraints on the dynamics, namely,

{\rm{tr}}[(\sigma_{1}\Omega\Pi_{0})^{k}]={\rm{tr}}[(\sigma_{2}\Omega\Pi_{0})^{k}]\,,

(101)

The above equations can be equivalently stated as equality of symplectic eigenvalues of
\sigma_{1}\Pi_{0}
and
\sigma_{2}\Pi_{0}
on modes with zero charge, and equality of ordinary eigenvalues of
\sigma_{1}[\Pi_{q}-\Pi_{-q}]
and
\sigma_{2}[\Pi_{q}-\Pi_{-q}]
on modes with non-zero charge, i.e.

\displaystyle{\rm SympEig}\left(\sigma_{1}\Pi_{0}\right)

\displaystyle={\rm SympEig}\left(\sigma_{2}\Pi_{0}\right)\,,\hskip 13.0ptq=0\,,

(102)

\displaystyle{\rm Eig}(\sigma_{1}(\Pi_{q}-\Pi_{-q}))

\displaystyle={\rm Eig}(\sigma_{2}(\Pi_{q}-\Pi_{-q}))\,,\ q\neq 0\,.

(103)

We emphasize that, for any covariance matrix
\sigma
, matrix
\sigma(\Pi_{q}-\Pi_{-q})
is indeed diagonalizable, and the number of its positive and negative eigenvalues is equal to the number of modes with charge
|q|
and
-|q|
, respectively
9
9

9

In general, for any positive real symmetric matrix
A
and real symmetric matrix
B
,
AB
is diagonalizable, and the number of its positive, negative and zero eigenvalues, are equal to those of
B
. To see this consider the similarity transformation
AB\rightarrow A^{-1/2}ABA^{1/2}
. This implies
AB
is similar to
A^{1/2}BA^{1/2}
. Then, applying the Sylvester’s law of inertia, we find that the number of positive, negative, and zero eigenvalues of
B
is equal to those of
A^{1/2}BA^{1/2}
, and thus to those of
AB
. Applying this to
A=(\Pi_{q}+\Pi_{-q})\sigma(\Pi_{q}+\Pi_{-q})
and
B=Q
proves the claim.
.

Interestingly, it turns out that for Gaussian states that (i) have a vanishing displacement vector, and (ii) contain no coherence with respect to the charge
\widehat{Q}
, these conservation laws capture all the implications of symmetry: if they hold, then there exists a
G
–invariant Gaussian unitary transformation that converts one into the other.
In particular, we show the following statement.

Theorem 11
.

Consider a symmetric matrix

Q=\sum_{q\in{\rm Eig}(Q)}q\Pi_{q}\,,

(104)

such that
[Q,\Omega]=0
, where
\Pi_{q}
projects on the sector with charge
q
, i.e. on modes
\{j:q_{j}=q\}
.

Suppose
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}
are two covariance matrices such that

[\sigma_{1},\Omega Q]=[\sigma_{2},\Omega Q]=0\,.

(105)

Then, there exists a symplectic transformation
S
such that
SQS^{\rm T}=Q
and
S\sigma_{1}S^{\rm T}=\sigma_{2}
if and only if conditions

\displaystyle{\rm{tr}}[(\sigma_{1}\Omega\Pi_{0})^{k}]

\displaystyle={\rm{tr}}[(\sigma_{2}\Omega\Pi_{0})^{k}]\,,

(106)

\displaystyle{\rm{tr}}\left[(\sigma_{1}(\Pi_{q}-\Pi_{-q}))^{k}\right]

\displaystyle={\rm{tr}}\left[(\sigma_{2}(\Pi_{q}-\Pi_{-q}))^{k}\right]\,,

(107)

hold for all
|q|>0
and integers
k
.

The proof is deferred to Appendix
D.2
.
Note that one only needs to check the conditions of Eqs.(
106
,
107
) for
k\leq n(q)
, where
n(q)
is the total number of modes
\{j:|q_{j}|=|q|\}
with charge
\pm q
.

Theorem
11
allows one to determine what quantum states are reachable from a given initial state via invariant symplectic transformations.
As an example, suppose a system of
n
modes is initially prepared in a product of uncorrelated thermal states, with covariance matrix
\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}\nu_{j}I_{2}
. Then, a Gaussian state with covariance matrix
\sigma
is reachable from this state via a charge-conserving symplectic transformation if and only if

\displaystyle{\rm Eig}\left(\sigma(\Pi_{q}-\Pi_{-q})\right)=\left\{\frac{q_{j}}{|q|}\nu_{j}:|q_{j}|=|q|\right\}\,,

for all
|q|>0
, and
{\rm SympEig}\left(\sigma_{1}\Pi_{0}\right)={\rm SympEig}\left(\sigma_{2}\Pi_{0}\right)
.
We conclude that

\frac{1}{2}\left({\rm{tr}}[\sigma_{q}]-{\rm{tr}}[\sigma_{-q}]\right)=\sum_{j:q_{j}=|q|}\nu_{j}\hskip 2.0pt-\hskip-8.0pt\sum_{j:q_{j}=-|q|}\nu_{j}\,,

(108)

where we have defined
\sigma_{p}\coloneqq\Pi_{p}\sigma\Pi_{p}
to be the restriction of covariance matrix
\sigma
on the modes with charge
p
.

Similarly, for
k=2
we obtain

\|\sigma_{q}\|_{2}^{2}+\|\sigma_{-q}\|_{2}^{2}-2\|\sigma_{q,-q}\|^{2}_{2}=\sum_{j:|q_{j}|=|q|}\nu^{2}_{j}\,,

(109)

where
\|A\|_{2}\coloneqq({\rm{tr}}[A^{2}])^{1/2}
, and
\sigma_{q,-q}\coloneqq\Pi_{q}\sigma\Pi_{-q}
.

Concretely, consider the symmetry generated by
Q=I\oplus-I
in the
(\hat{x}_{1},\hat{p}_{1},\hat{x}_{2},\hat{p}_{2})
basis.
According to Eq.(
44
), the invariant covariance matrices take the form

\sigma=\begin{pmatrix}a_{11}I&a_{12}Ze^{\theta\Omega}\\
a_{12}Ze^{\theta\Omega}&a_{22}I\end{pmatrix}\,,

(110)

for
a_{11},a_{22},a_{12}\geq 0
and
\theta\in[0,2\pi)
.
Then, the conservation laws as written in Eqs.(
108
,
109
) imply

\displaystyle a_{11}-a_{22}

\displaystyle=\nu_{1}-\nu_{2}\,,

(111)

\displaystyle a_{11}^{2}+a_{22}^{2}-2a_{12}^{2}

\displaystyle=\nu_{1}^{2}+\nu_{2}^{2}\,,

which, in particular, combine to give the conservation of the determinant of
\sigma
under symplectic transformations,

a_{11}a_{22}-a_{12}^{2}=\nu_{1}\nu_{2}\,.

(112)

We conclude that, once any of
a_{11}
,
a_{22}
, or
a_{12}
is chosen, the other two are uniquely determined.

VI.4
Extension of Williamson’s theorem (simultaneous normal mode decomposition)

In this section, we establish a symmetric extension of Williamson’s seminal theorem
Williamson 1936
;
Nicacio 2021
, which is a critical step in the proof of Theorem
11
.

The content of Williamson’s theorem
Williamson 1936
is that any
(2n\times 2n)
real symmetric positive-definite matrix
M
can be diagonalized by congruence using a symplectic matrix
S
, such that the symplectic eigenvalues appear in pairs, i.e.
SMS^{\rm T}=\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}\nu_{j}I_{2}
for real positive
\nu_{j}
, where
I_{2}
denotes the
(2\times 2)
identity matrix.
It has recently been shown
Kamat and Mishra 2025
that two
(2n\times 2n)
real symmetric positive-definite matrices
M_{1},M_{2}
can be symplectically diagonalized by congruence using one symplectic matrix if and only if
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
symplectically commute, i.e.
[\Omega M_{1},\Omega M_{2}]=0
.
In such a scenario, we say that
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
admit a simultaneous normal mode decomposition.

For applications in the context of symmetry, we need to consider a different extension of Williamson’s theorem, in which two
(2n\times 2n)
real symmetric matrices
M
and
Q
are given, with only
M
required to be positive-definite, and find necessary and sufficient conditions for
M
and
Q
to admit a simultaneous normal-mode decomposition.

Theorem 12
.

Let
M
and
Q
be
(2n\times 2n)
real symmetric matrices, such that
M
is positive-definite and
[Q,\Omega]=0
.

Then, there exists a symplectic matrix
S
that diagonalizes both
Q
and
M
by congruence,

\displaystyle SMS^{\rm T}

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}\nu_{j}I_{2}\,,\quad\nu_{j}\geq 0\,,

(113)

\displaystyle SQS^{\rm T}

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}q_{j}I_{2}\,,\quad q_{j}\in\mathbb{R}\,,

(114)

if and only if
M
and
Q
satisfy

[\Omega M,\Omega Q]=0\,,

(115)

where
I_{2}
denotes the
(2\times 2)
identity matrix.

The proof is deferred to Appendix
D.1
.
Note that the condition in Eq.(
115
) can be equivalently stated as

\displaystyle[\Omega M,\Omega Q]=[\Omega M,Q]=[M,\Omega Q]=0\,.

(116)

If
Q
is taken as the symmetric matrix that corresponds to a conserved charge
\widehat{Q}
and
M
is a Hamiltonian matrix or the covariance matrix of a quantum state, the first commutation relation in Eq.(
115
) represents invariance of
M
under the symmetry generated by
\widehat{Q}
.
The second commutation relation in Eq.(
115
) implies that the charge is passive.
The standard Williamson’s theorem
Williamson 1936
corresponds to the special case of trivial symmetry, i.e.
Q=0
.

Normal mode decomposition when all charges
q_{j}
are equal, i.e.
Q=qI
,
q\in\mathbb{R}
is an important special case of Theorem
12
, and we highlight it here.

Proposition 13
.

For any Gaussian state
\hat{\rho}
with covariance matrix
\sigma
, the following statements are equivalent:

(1)

The covariance matrix satisfies
[\sigma,\Omega]=0
;

(2)

There exists a passive symplectic Gaussian unitary
\hat{S}
such that, up to displacement,

\hat{S}\hat{\rho}\hat{S}^{\dagger}=\bigotimes_{j=1}^{n}\frac{e^{-\beta_{j}\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{j}}}{{\rm{tr}}[e^{-\beta_{j}\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{j}}]}\,,

(117)

is a product of thermal states with
\beta_{j}>0
;

(3)

The covariance matrix consists of
(2\times 2)
blocks describing correlations between modes
j
and
k
, that have the form
\sigma_{jk}=a_{jk}\exp(\theta_{jk} \Omega)
, with
a_{jk}=a_{kj}
, and
\theta_{jk}=-\theta_{kj}
for all
j,k=1,\dots,n
.

Proof.

(1)\Rightarrow(2)
. If
[\sigma,\Omega]=0
, the charge matrix
Q=q\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}I_{2}

satisfies both assumptions in Eq.(
115
) of Theorem
12
, which then implies that there exists a symplectic matrix
S
such that
SMS^{\rm T}=\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}\nu_{j}I_{2}
and
SS^{\rm T}=\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}I_{2}
.
The RHS in the former equation is exactly the covariance matrix of the thermal states in the RHS of Eq.(
117
) with
\beta_{j}\coloneqq\log\left(\frac{\nu_{j}+1}{\nu_{j}-1}\right)
, while the latter equation ensures that
\hat{S}
is passive.

(2)\Rightarrow(3)
. This follows immediately from the form of all U(1)–invariant matrices given in Eq.(
44
).

(3)\Rightarrow(1)
. The form of
\sigma_{jk}
directly yields
(1)
.
∎

Proposition
13
is the main result of Ref.
John et al. 2025
, where it was derived with no reference to symmetry considerations.

VII
Covariant Gaussian dilation

Any Gaussian channel can be realized via a Gaussian unitary that acts on the system and on an environment prepared in a Gaussian pure state
Caruso et al. 2008
, a result which extends the Stinespring dilation theorem
Stinespring 1955
.
In this section, we generalize this result to show that any covariant Gaussian channel can be realized via a symmetry-respecting Gaussian unitary that acts on the system and on an environment prepared in a Gaussian pure state that also respects the symmetry.
To prove this generalization, we develop an approach for constructing such dilations, which is different from both the proof of the aforementioned result for Gaussian channels in the absence of symmetries
Caruso et al. 2008
and the proof of the covariant Stinespring dilation theorem for finite-dimensional systems, which relies on the Kraus decomposition
Marvian 2012
.
Naturally, the symplectic representations under consideration are assumed to admit invariant states, which, by Proposition
3
, implies that they are squeezed passive representations.
We start by a quick review of Gaussian purifications which play a key role in our main proof of the dilation theorem (see Fig.
6
for a proof sketch).

VII.1
Invariant Gaussian purification

Consider an
n
–mode system
A
and a symmetry group
G
with symplectic Gaussian representation
\hat{S}_{A}(g):g\in G
in Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{A}
.
Given any mixed Gaussian state
\hat{\rho}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{A})
that is invariant under representation
\hat{S}_{A}
, does there exist a purification
\ket{\psi}\in\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
of
\hat{\rho}
that is Gaussian, and invariant under tensor representation
\hat{S}_{A}\otimes\hat{S}_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
, for an ancillary system
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
and an appropriately chosen representation
\hat{S}_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
?
The answer is positive as we show in Appendix
E.1
, and, analogously to the case of discrete-variable systems
Nielsen and Chuang 2010
, we prove in Appendix
E.2
that it is unique up to a symmetry-respecting Gaussian unitary on the ancilla.
We sketch the construction of such purification in Fig.
5
.

Figure 5:
Circuit for purification
.
We consider an invariant Gaussian state
\hat{\rho}_{A}
with covariance matrix
\sigma=VDV^{\rm T}
, for symplectic
V
and diagonal
D\geq I
.
Registers
A
and
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
both comprise
n
modes.
The circuit operates in three steps: (i) preparation of the vacuum state on both registers, (ii) coupling of the two registers via
n
2–mode squeezers, each depending on a symplectic eigenvalue in
D
and depicted by an arched line inside the box, and (iii) symplectic rotation on each of the registers
A
and
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
(
\hat{V}
and
\hat{V}^{\prime}
are associated with symplectic matrices
V
and
V\Omega
, respectively).
Overall this circuit realizes the purification
\ket{\psi}
with covariance matrix
\sigma_{\psi}=(V\oplus V\Omega)V_{\rm 2sq}(\tfrac{1}{2}\cosh^{-1}D)V_{\rm 2sq}(\tfrac{1}{2}\cosh^{-1}D)^{\rm T}(V\oplus V\Omega)^{\rm T}
, as given in Eq.(
120
).
Lemma
14
proves that this is an invariant Gaussian purification of
\hat{\rho}
and guarantees that it is unique up to an invariant Gaussian unitary
\hat{U}
applied on the ancillary register
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
according to Eq.(
121
).
We note that steps (i), (ii) and (iii) are generally not individually invariant, but Proposition
6
gives a
G
–invariant Gaussian unitary that prepares
\sigma_{\psi}
from a
G
–invariant state that is uncorrelated across
A
and
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
which, however, requires the implementation of the globally correlated symplectic transformation
\sqrt{\sigma_{\psi}}
.

Lemma 14

(Invariant Gaussian purification)
.

Consider a system with a finite number of bosonic modes and Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{A}
equipped with symplectic Gaussian representation
\hat{S}_{A}(g):g\in G
of a group
G
.
Let
\hat{\rho}_{A}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{A})
be an invariant state, i.e.
\hat{S}_{A}(g)\hat{\rho}_{A}\hat{S}_{A}(g)^{\dagger}=\hat{\rho}_{A}
for all
g\in G
.
Then, the following statements hold true.

(i)

There exists a Gaussian pure state
\ket{\psi}_{A\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\in\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
for ancilla
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
with Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\cong\mathcal{H}_{A}
equipped with representation
\hat{S}_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}(g)\coloneqq\hat{S}^{*}_{A}(g):g\in G
, where the complex conjugate is defined in the common eigenbasis of position operators, such that
\ket{\psi}_{A\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
is a purification of
\hat{\rho}_{A}
,

\hat{\rho}_{A}={\rm{tr}}_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}[\outerproduct{\psi}{\psi}_{A\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}]\,,

(118)

and is
G
–invariant, i.e. for all
g\in G
,

(\hat{S}_{A}(g)\otimes\hat{S}_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}(g))\ket{\psi}_{A\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}=\ket{\psi}_{A\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\,.

(119)

(ii)

Assuming
\boldsymbol{d}
and
\sigma
are the displacement vector and covariance matrix of state
\hat{\rho}_{A}
, respectively, then,
\ket{\psi}
can be chosen to have displacement vector
\boldsymbol{d}\oplus\boldsymbol{0}
, and covariance matrix given in basis
\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}_{A}\oplus\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
by

\hskip 15.0pt\begin{pmatrix}\sigma&\hskip-10.0pt-\sqrt{-\sigma\Omega\sigma\Omega-I}\ \Omega Z_{A}\\[5.0pt]
Z_{A}\Omega\sqrt{-\Omega\sigma\Omega\sigma-I}&\hskip-10.0ptZ_{A}\sigma Z_{A}\end{pmatrix}\,,

(120)

where
\boldsymbol{0}
is the zero vector on
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
,
Z_{A}\coloneqq(I_{A}\otimes Z)
, and
\Omega
is the symplectic form on
A
.

(iii)

Suppose that
\hat{\rho}_{A}
admits two Gaussian purifications
\ket{\psi_1}_{AB}
and
\ket{\psi_2}_{AB}
for some ancilla
B
with Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{B}
equipped with symplectic Gaussian representation
\hat{S}_{B}(g):g\in G
.
If
\ket{\psi_1}_{AB}
and
\ket{\psi_2}_{AB}
are invariant, i.e.
(\hat{S}_{A}(g)\otimes\hat{S}_{B}(g))\ket{\psi_k}_{AB}=\ket{\psi_k}_{AB}
for
k=1,2
and all
g\in G
, then there exists a Gaussian unitary
\hat{U}_{B}
that commutes with representation
\hat{S}_{B}(g):g\in G
such that

\ket{\psi_2}_{AB}=\left(\hat{I}_{A}\otimes\hat{U}_{B}\right)\ket{\psi_1}_{AB}\,.

(121)

We note that the purification given in Eq.(
120
) is a slight modification of the Gaussian purification
10
10

10

The original Gaussian purification
Holevo and Werner 2001
considers covariance matrix
Z_{A}\sigma_{\psi}Z_{A}
, which results in a symplectic form
\Omega_{A}\oplus-\Omega_{A}
that is reflected on the second subsystem. Other Gaussian purifications have been considered, e.g. in Ref.
Weedbrook et al. 2012
, but they generally do not respect the symmetries of the original state.
previously found by Holevo and Werner
Holevo and Werner 2001
.
We also emphasize that invariance is considered at the state vector level, i.e.
(\hat{S}_{A}(g)\otimes\hat{S}_{B}(g))\ket{\psi}_{AB}=\ket{\psi}_{AB}
, for all
g\in G
.
This is a stronger condition than the density operator being
G
–invariant, requiring that the state vector itself must carry the trivial representation of the group, as opposed to a general non-trivial one-dimensional representation.

VII.2
Covariant Gaussian dilation

Equipped with invariant Gaussian purifications, we are ready to establish covariant Gaussian dilations of covariant Gaussian channels.
Recall that Gaussian channels are completely Gaussianity-preserving CPTP operations (see Appendix
F.1
), i.e. CPTP maps which send Gaussian states to Gaussian states, even when acting only on a subsystem of a larger Gaussian system.

Theorem 15

(Covariant Gaussian dilation)
.

Consider a pair of systems
A
and
B
with finite numbers of bosonic modes and Hilbert spaces
\mathcal{H}_{A}
and
\mathcal{H}_{B}
.
Suppose each system is equipped with a symplectic Gaussian representation of a group
G
that admits an invariant state (as characterized in Proposition
3
), denoted by
\hat{S}_{A}(g):g\in G
, and
\hat{S}_{B}(g):g\in G
, respectively.

For any Gaussian channel
\mathcal{E}:\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{A})\rightarrow\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{B})
that is covariant, i.e.
\mathcal{E}(\hat{S}_{A}(g)(\cdot)\hat{S}_{A}(g)^{\dagger})=\hat{S}_{B}(g)\mathcal{E}(\cdot)\hat{S}_{B}(g)^{\dagger}
for all
g\in G
, there exists a dilation of the form

\mathcal{E}(\cdot)={\rm{tr}}_{A\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\left[\hat{V}_{AB\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\big((\cdot)\otimes\outerproduct{\eta}{\eta}_{B\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\big)\hat{V}_{AB\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}^{\dagger}\right]\,,

(122)

where ancilla
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
has Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\cong\mathcal{H}_{B}
equipped with representation
\hat{S}_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}(g)\coloneqq\hat{S}^{*}_{B}(g):g\in G
, where the complex conjugate is defined in the common eigenbasis of position operators,
\ket{\eta}_{B\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
is an invariant pure Gaussian state, i.e.
(\hat{S}_{B}(g)\otimes\hat{S}_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}(g))\ket{\eta}_{B\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}=\ket{\eta}_{B\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
for all
g\in G
, and
\hat{V}_{AB\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
is a symplectic Gaussian unitary that commutes with representation
\hat{S}_{A}(g)\otimes\hat{S}_{B}(g)\otimes\hat{S}_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}(g):g\in G
.

Furthermore,
\ket{\eta}_{B\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
can be chosen to be uncorrelated between
B
and
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
.

Fig.
6
provides a sketch of the proof based on the notion of invariant purification and decoupling. The complete proof is deferred to Appendix
F.2
, where we also present a phase space version of the statement and the converse of the theorem. Note that when the symmetry representations are trivial, we obtain the dilation of Gaussian channels, which was previously shown
Caruso et al. 2008
;
Serafini 2017
using a different approach.

Figure 6:

Gaussian Stinespring dilation via purification and decoupling
.
Our proof of Theorem
15
provides a constructive method for implementing a covariant Gaussian channel
\mathcal{E}
using
G
–invariant Gaussian unitaries that couple the system to an environment in a
G
–invariant Gaussian pure state.
First, the channel is applied to the subsystem
A
of a composite system
A\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
, initially prepared in a
G
–invariant Gaussian pure state
\ket{\psi}_{A\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
, resulting in state
\hat{\tau}_{B\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
.
In step
{\rm(i)}
, we construct a
G
–invariant Gaussian purification of
\hat{\tau}_{B\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
, denoted
\ket{\Phi}_{A\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5muB\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
, whose existence is guaranteed by Lemma
14
.
In step
{\rm(ii)}
, we decouple
\ket{\Phi}_{A\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5muB\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
via a
G
–invariant Gaussian unitary
\hat{V}^{\dagger}_{AB\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
, obtaining
\ket{\psi}_{A\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\otimes\ket{\eta}_{B\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
, which is an equivalent purification of
\hat{\rho}_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\coloneqq{\rm{tr}}_{A}[\outerproduct{\psi}{\psi}_{A\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}]
.
Lemma
14
ensures that such a unitary exists. Therefore, if
\hat{\rho}_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
is full rank, we obtain the channel equality in Eq. (
122
).
Note that
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muX\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
denotes a system whose Hilbert space is isomorphic to that of system
X
but carries the complex conjugate representation of symmetry.
This approach to dilation can also be applied in the setting of discrete variables.

Theorem
15
demonstrates that, for any symplectic Gaussian representation, a (covariant) Gaussian channel mapping
n_{A}
to
n_{B}
modes is guaranteed to have a (covariant) Gaussian dilation with ancillary qubits prepared in (pure) vacuum using at most
2n_{B}
ancillary modes.
Therefore, similar to the dilation of general Gaussian channels, a covariant Gaussian channel from
n_{A}
to
n_{B}
modes can be realized by discarding an environment with at most
n_{A}+n_{B}
modes.

The fact that the ancilla state
\ket{\eta}_{B\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
can be chosen unentangled across the bipartition
B:\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
follows from Proposition
3
, which guarantees that the symplectic Gaussian representation
\hat{S}_{B}
admits a
G
–invariant pure state, together with Proposition
6
which states that
G
–invariant Gaussian transformations act transitively on the space of
G
–invariant Gaussian pure states.

It is worth noting that the proof of Theorem
15
provides a method to construct the dilating unitary
\hat{V}
.
One has freedom to choose an initial pure state
\ket{\psi}_{A\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
provided that its reduced state on
A
is full-rank and invariant under input representation
\hat{S}_{A}
.
Then, the purification step (i) outlined in Fig.
6
constructs
G
–invariant Gaussian state
\ket{\Phi}_{A\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5muB\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
, e.g. using the purification in Eq.(
120
).
The decoupling step (ii) consists of identifying a
G
–invariant symplectic Gaussian unitary
\hat{V}_{AB\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
that maps between the two purifications of marginal state
{\rm{tr}}_{A}[\outerproduct{\psi}{\psi}_{A\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}]
.
This is done by first identifying an intertwiner that maps between the modes in
AB\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
that are correlated with modes in
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
, and then completing it into a full
G
–invariant symplectic transformation (see Appendix
E.3
).

As an example, consider a Gaussian channel
\mathcal{E}
that acts on a system
A
with
n
modes equipped with
\operatorname{U}(1)
representation
\hat{O}(\theta)=\bigotimes_{j=1}^{n}\exp(i\theta q_j \hat{a}^\dag_j\hat{a}_j)
with integer charges
q_{j}
.
If
\mathcal{E}
is covariant with respect to the
\operatorname{U}(1)
symmetry, i.e.

\mathcal{E}\left(\hat{O}(\theta)(\cdot)\hat{O}(\theta)^{\dagger}\right)=\hat{O}(\theta)\mathcal{E}(\cdot)\hat{O}(\theta)^{\dagger}\,,

(123)

then Theorem
15
implies that it can be realized by coupling
A
to
n
–mode ancillary systems
B
with charges
\{q_{j}:j=1,\dots,n\}
, and
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
with charges
\{-q_{j}:j=1,\dots,n\}
, all initially prepared in vacuum
\ket{0}^{\otimes 2n}
, using U(1)–invariant Gaussian unitaries.

We obtain covariant Gaussian dilations for all 1–mode phase-insensitive, or, equivalently, energy-conserving, channels via the constructive proof of Theorem
15
in Appendix
F.3
.
In particular, we directly find dilating unitaries for all attenuator and amplifier channels, which are slight modifications of the standard dilating unitaries
Weedbrook et al. 2012
, guaranteeing
\operatorname{U}(1)
–invariance.

VIII
Summary & Outlook

We develop a framework that characterizes symmetry-respecting Gaussian dynamics in bosonic systems.
Within this framework, we demonstrate that asymmetry splits into different types and derive families of monotones that are tractable for Gaussian states, quantifying asymmetry in the state’s displacement vector and covariance matrix.
We also derive a family of conservation laws for Gaussian closed dynamics, some of which are violated when the dynamics is non-Gaussian.
Finally, we develop a new approach to channel dilation which relies only on an appropriate equivalence of purifications.
In the presence of symmetries, our dilation provides a constructive method of realizing covariant Gaussian channels via invariant Gaussian unitaries and pure states.

It is worth exploring the restrictions that phase space representations of symmetry impose on the characteristic functions of quantum states, which may lead to practical asymmetry witnesses.
In particular, the characteristic function gives rise to the
s
–ordered representations
Cahill and Glauber 1969
, such as the Wigner, Glauber, and Hussimi representations, which are reconstructible in a lab
Banaszek and Wódkiewicz 1996
;
Leibfried et al. 1996
.

Our statement of channel dilation can be quantified beyond the context of Gaussian covariant channels, by asking the question of whether channels with limited non-Gaussianity or asymmetry can be dilated via unitaries and ancillary states that themselves contain limited non-Gaussianity or asymmetry as measured by a chosen monotone.
For instance, one could employ a monotone such as the Wigner negativity
Kenfack and Życzkowski 2004
of non-Gaussian states, and ask what quantum channels may be implemented via Gaussian unitaries and ancillary states of fixed maximum non-Gaussianity as quantified by the chosen monotone.

Fermions are the counterpart of bosons, following canonical anti-commutation relations
Tong 2016
.
Besides their natural significance, fermionic simulation is a promising path towards quantum advantage
Ortiz et al. 2001
, in particular due to the Jordan-Wigner transform
Jordan and Wigner 1928
which allows fermionic simulation on qubit devices.
Much like for bosonic systems, fermionic Gaussian systems are the most easily engineered, and so an analog of our analysis of symmetric Gaussian dynamics can be useful in the context of fermions as well.
It may also be of interest to investigate a resource theory of asymmetry on hybrid setups of continuous and discrete variables.

In general, specific quantum information tasks, such as property testing for Gaussian states
Girardi et al. 2025
, motivate the study of specific symmetry representations.
We hope that our framework paves the way to exploring CV quantum information tasks that hinge on Gaussian representations of symmetry.

IX

We thank David Jakab for numerous helpful discussions.
We also acknowledge support from NSF Phy-2046195, and NSF QLCI grant OMA-2120757.

Appendix A
Notation and background on Gaussian CV

A.1
Notation

The complex conjugate is denoted by star (
∗
).
The zero matrix is denoted by
0
.
The inequality
A\geq B
between square matrices
A,B
of the same dimension means that
A-B
has non-negative eigenvalues.
The space of bounded linear operators for Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
is written as
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
.
The qubit Pauli operators are denoted by
I,X,Y,Z
.
We denote an operator
\hat{a}
acting on the Hilbert space by a hat, and an operator
a
acting in phase space without the hat.
We use the shorthand
\hat{a}\pm\text{H.c.}=\hat{a}\pm\hat{a}^{\dagger}
for operator
\hat{a}
.
Vectors of operators,
\boldsymbol{a}
or
\hat{\boldsymbol{a}}
, are denoted by boldface.

We follow Serafini’s notation
Serafini 2017
for calculations involving vectors of operators.
We write the inner product as
\hat{\boldsymbol{a}}^{\rm T}\hat{\boldsymbol{b}}=\sum_{j}\hat{a}_{j}\hat{b}_{j}
, and the outer product as
\hat{\boldsymbol{a}}\hat{\boldsymbol{b}}^{\rm T}
, with components
\left[\hat{\boldsymbol{a}}\hat{\boldsymbol{b}}^{\rm T}\right]_{jk}=\hat{a}_{j}\hat{b}_{k}
.
Note that in general
\left(\hat{\boldsymbol{a}}\hat{\boldsymbol{a}}^{\rm T}\right)^{\rm T}\neq\hat{\boldsymbol{a}}\hat{\boldsymbol{a}}^{\rm T}
since operators
\hat{a}_{j}
and
\hat{a}_{k}
may not commute for
j\neq k
.
The commutators and anti-commutators of a list of operators can thus be succinctly expressed as

\left[\hat{\boldsymbol{a}},\hat{\boldsymbol{a}}^{\rm T}\right]=\hat{\boldsymbol{a}}\hat{\boldsymbol{a}}^{\rm T}-\left(\hat{\boldsymbol{a}}\hat{\boldsymbol{a}}^{\rm T}\right)^{\rm T}\,,\quad\quad\quad\left\{\hat{\boldsymbol{a}},\hat{\boldsymbol{a}}^{\rm T}\right\}=\hat{\boldsymbol{a}}\hat{\boldsymbol{a}}^{\rm T}+\left(\hat{\boldsymbol{a}}\hat{\boldsymbol{a}}^{\rm T}\right)^{\rm T}\,.

(124)

A.2
Elements of the Gaussian formalism

A.2.1
Bases for CV systems

The choice of basis does not affect operators in the Hilbert space, but it determines matrix representations in the phase space.
We often make use of two common bases for CV systems.
The real basis is represented by the vector of position and momentum (quadrature) operators,

\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}\coloneqq(\hat{x}_{1},\hat{x}_{n},\dots,\hat{p}_{1},\hat{p}_{n})^{\rm T}\,.

(125)

The complex basis is represented by the vector of creation and annihilation operators,

\hat{\boldsymbol{a}}\coloneqq(\hat{a}_{1},\dots,\hat{a}_{n},\hat{a}_{1}^{\dagger},\dots,\hat{a}_{n}^{\dagger})^{\rm T}\,.

(126)

We use symbol
\overset{\boldsymbol{r}}{=}
or
\overset{\boldsymbol{a}}{=}
in expressions when the RHS is a matrix representation of the LHS given in the real basis (
125
) or the complex basis (
126
), respectively.

Both basis operator vectors satisfy the canonical commutation relation (CCR),

\left[\hat{\boldsymbol{r}},\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}^{\rm T}\right]=\left[\hat{\boldsymbol{a}},\hat{\boldsymbol{a}}^{\rm T}\right]=i\Omega_{n}\,,\quad\text{where}\quad\Omega_{n}\overset{\boldsymbol{r}}{=}\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}\overset{\boldsymbol{a}}{=}-i\begin{pmatrix}0&I\\
-I&0\end{pmatrix}\,,

(127)

is the
(2n\times 2n)
symplectic form
11
11

11

It is common in the literature (e.g.
Serafini 2017
;
Adesso et al. 2014
) to consider the commutation relation
\left[\hat{\boldsymbol{a}},\hat{\boldsymbol{a}}^{\dagger}\right]
instead, leading to the mirrored symplectic form

\Omega_{n}\overset{\boldsymbol{a}}{=}-i\begin{pmatrix}I&0\\
0&-I\end{pmatrix}
in the complex basis.
and
I
here denotes the
(n\times n)
identity matrix.

Phase space objects that appear in Gaussian CV theory transform differently under a change of basis as demonstrated in Table
1
according to their tensor type.

(1,1)
–tensors, such as a symplectic matrix
V
, transform by similarity, while
(0,2)
–tensors, such as a Hamiltonian matrix
H
, and
(2,0)
–tensors, such as the covariance matrix
\sigma
of a quantum state, transform by congruence.
All
(2,0)
–tensors and
(0,2)
–tensors can contract into
(1,1)
–tensors.
For example,
\Omega H
and
\sigma\Omega^{-1}
are
(1,1)
–tensors.
As a consequence,
\Omega H\Omega
is a
(2,0)
–tensor and transforms identically to
\sigma
.

Any
(1,1)
–tensor
A
expressed in terms of
(n\times n)
real blocks as
A\overset{\boldsymbol{\xi}}{=}\begin{pmatrix}A_{1}&A_{2}\\
A_{3}&A_{4}\end{pmatrix}
in the permuted (“
xp
–ordered”) real basis
\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}=(\hat{x}_{1},\dots,\hat{x}_{n},\hat{p}_{1},\dots,\hat{p}_{n})
, takes the following block form in the complex basis,

A\overset{\boldsymbol{a}}{=}\begin{pmatrix}B_{1}&B_{2}\\
B_{2}^{*}&B_{1}^{*}\end{pmatrix}\,,\quad\text{where}\quad B_{1}=\frac{A_{1}+A_{4}}{2}-i\frac{A_{2}-A_{3}}{2}\,,\quad B_{2}=\frac{A_{1}-A_{4}}{2}+i\frac{A_{2}+A_{3}}{2}\,.

(128)

A
(2,0)
–tensor or
(0,2)
–tensor, such as
\Omega_{n}
, requires a subsequent “left-right mirroring”, achieved by right-multiplying with
X\otimes I_{n}
.

A.2.2
Second-order Hamiltonians

Recall that a Gaussian CV system of
n
bosonic modes is described by a second order Hamiltonian
\hat{H}
as given in Eq.(
3
), where additive constants are ignored as they amount to unobservable global phases in the dynamics.
Using Eq.(
128
), we express the Hamiltonian matrix
H
in the complex basis in terms of two
(n\times n)
blocks
12
12

12

In the main text, we use
H^{\text{p}}
instead of
F
and
H^{\text{np}}
instead of
G
.

F
and
G
as

H\overset{\boldsymbol{a}}{=}\begin{pmatrix}G&F\\
F^{*}&G^{*}\end{pmatrix}\,,

(129)

where we require that
G=G^{\rm T}
, and
F=F^{\dagger}
because
H
is symmetric in the real basis.

For purely quadratic
\hat{H}_{A},\hat{H}_{B},\hat{H}_{C}
, the relation
[i\hat{H}_{B},i\hat{H}_{C}]\overset{\boldsymbol{r}}{=}i\frac{1}{2}\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}^{\rm T}\Big(\Omega[-\Omega H_{B},-\Omega H_{C}]\Big)\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}
leads to the equivalence

i\hat{H}_{A}=[i\hat{H}_{B},i\hat{H}_{C}]\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad-\Omega H_{A}=[-\Omega H_{B},-\Omega H_{C}]\,.

(130)

This allows us to prove Proposition
1
on passive purely quadratic observables.
See
1

Proof.

(1)\Leftrightarrow(3)
.
[\widehat{Q},\hat{H}_{\rm free}]=0
implies
[e^{i\widehat{Q}s},\hat{H}_{\rm free}]=0
for all
s\in\mathbb{R}
.
Conversely, by differentiating
[e^{i\widehat{Q}s},\hat{H}_{\rm free}]=0
with respect to
s
and setting
s=0
, we get
[\widehat{Q},\hat{H}_{\rm free}]=0
.

(3)\Leftrightarrow(4)
. This follows directly from Eq.(
130
) with
H_{A}=0
,
H_{B}=H_{\rm free}
and
H_{C}=Q
.

(4)\Leftrightarrow(2)
. Matrix
e^{-\Omega Qs}
is the symplectic matrix associated with operator
e^{i\widehat{Q}s}
, for all
s\in\mathbb{R}
.
Note that
\left(e^{-\Omega Qs}\right)^{\rm T}=e^{Q\Omega s}
.
Then,
e^{-\Omega Qs}\left(e^{-\Omega Qs}\right)^{\rm T}=I
for all
s\in\mathbb{R}
if and only if
[Q,\Omega]=0
.

(3)\Rightarrow(5)
and

(5)\Rightarrow(2)
. The vacuum state is a non-degenerate eigenstate of
\hat{H}_{\rm free}
.
Hence, if
\widehat{Q}
commutes with
\hat{H}_{\rm free}
, it is also an eigenstate of
\widehat{Q}
.
Conversely, if the vacuum state is an eigenstate of
\widehat{Q}
, then
e^{-\Omega Qs}\left(e^{-\Omega Qs}\right)^{\rm T}=I
in order to preserve its covariance matrix.
∎

A.2.3
Gaussian unitaries

A purely quadratic Hamiltonian
\hat{H}
generates a symplectic Gaussian unitary
\hat{S}=e^{i\hat{H}}=e^{\tfrac{i}{2}\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}^{\raisebox{-0.64583pt}{\small$\scriptstyle T$}}H\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}
, which acts on the basis
\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}
as
\hat{S}^{\dagger}\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\hat{S}=S\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}
, where the symplectic matrix
S
associated with operator
\hat{S}
is

S=e^{-\Omega H}\overset{\boldsymbol{a}}{=}\begin{pmatrix}U&V\\
V^{*}&U^{*}\end{pmatrix}\,,

(131)

for
(n\times n)
blocks
U,V
satisfying
UU^{\dagger}-VV^{\dagger}=I
and
UV^{\rm T}-VU^{\rm T}=0
, in order to ensure that the transformed basis
S\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}
satisfies the CCR in Eq.(
127
).
Passive symplectic Gaussian unitaries do not mix creation and annihilation operators, so their associated symplectic matrices simplify to

S\overset{\boldsymbol{a}}{=}U\oplus U^{*}
, where
U
is now a
(n\times n)
unitary.

The real symplectic group
\operatorname{Sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
is connected, but not compact, so not all elements can be expressed as a single exponential
S=e^{-\Omega H}
.
Instead, every
S\in\operatorname{Sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
admits a singular value decomposition
Arvind et al. 1995
;
Braunstein 2005
in terms of compact and non-compact symplectic transformations,

S\overset{\boldsymbol{r}}{=}O^{\prime}\left(\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}\begin{pmatrix}e^{r_{j}}&0\\
0&e^{-r_{j}}\end{pmatrix}\right)O^{\rm T}\overset{\boldsymbol{a}}{=}O^{\prime}\begin{pmatrix}\cosh{r}&\sinh{r}\\
\sinh{r}&\cosh{r}\end{pmatrix}O^{\rm T}\,,

(132)

where
O,O^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})\cap\operatorname{O}(2n)
and
r=\mathrm{diag}(r_{1},\dots,r_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
Phase-shifters and beam-splitters generate the maximal compact subgroup
\operatorname{Sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})\cap\operatorname{O}(2n)\cong\operatorname{U}(n)

Bloch and Messiah 1962
.
Together with the 1–mode squeezer, they generate the entire symplectic group
\operatorname{Sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})

Knill et al. 2001
.

Finally, recall that every Gaussian unitary can be brought into the standard form
\hat{U}=\hat{D}_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\hat{S}
for some Weyl displacement operator
\hat{D}_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}
, up to an unobservable global phase.

A.2.4
Gaussian states

The set of Gaussian states is the collection of thermal and ground states of positive semi-definite second-order Hamiltonians.
They are fully characterized by the first two statistical moments, as given in Eq.(
6
).
The covariance matrix takes the form

\sigma\overset{\boldsymbol{a}}{=}\begin{pmatrix}G&F\\
F^{*}&G^{*}\end{pmatrix}\,,

(133)

for
(n\times n)
blocks
G=G^{\rm T}
, and
F=F^{\dagger}
given by

F_{jk}=\langle\hat{a}_{j}\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{k}+\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{k}\hat{a}_{j}\rangle_{\hat{\rho}}-2\langle\hat{a}_{j}\rangle_{\hat{\rho}}\langle\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{k}\rangle_{\hat{\rho}}\,,\quad\quad G_{jk}=\langle\hat{a}_{j}\hat{a}_{k}+\hat{a}_{k}\hat{a}_{j}\rangle_{\hat{\rho}}-2\langle\hat{a}_{j}\rangle_{\hat{\rho}}\langle\hat{a}_{k}\rangle_{\hat{\rho}}\,,

(134)

where
\langle(\cdot)\rangle_{\hat{\rho}}\coloneqq{\rm{tr}}[(\cdot)\hat{\rho}]
.
The covariance matrix
\sigma
satisfies the uncertainty relation
\sigma+i\Omega\geq 0
in the real basis
13
13

13

In our complex basis, the uncertainty relation looks like
(\sigma+i\Omega)(I\otimes X)\geq 0
for Pauli
X
.
, and its symplectic eigenvalues are the ordinary eigenvalues of
|i\Omega\sigma|
.
The uncertainty relation is equivalent to the symplectic eigenvalues being greater than 1, i.e.
|i\Omega\sigma|^{2}\geq I
, giving the relation

-\sigma\Omega\sigma\Omega-I\geq 0\,,

(135)

with equality if and only if
\sigma
is the covariance matrix of a pure state, i.e. all symplectic eigenvalues are 1.

Three notable 1–mode states that we use throughout our discussion are the thermal state
\hat{\rho}_{\rm th}(\bar{n})
of the free Hamiltonian in Eq.(
14
) with
\bar{n}\coloneqq(e^{\beta}-1)^{-1}
, the coherent state
\ket{\alpha}\coloneqq\hat{D}_{\alpha}\ket{0}
and the squeezed state
\ket{r}\coloneqq\hat{V}_{\rm 1sq}\ket{0}
.
Their decompositions
Glauber 1963
;
Stoler 1970
in the eigenbasis of the number operator
\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a}
(Fock basis)
\{\ket{k}:k\in\mathbb{N}\}
are

\hat{\rho}_{\rm th}(\bar{n})=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{\bar{n}^{k}}{(\bar{n}+1)^{k+1}}\outerproduct{k}{k}\,,\quad\ket{\alpha}=e^{-\frac{|\alpha|^{2}}{2}}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{\alpha^{k}}{\sqrt{k!}}\ket{k}\,,\quad\ket{r}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\cosh r}}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-\tanh r)^{k}}{\sqrt{(2k)!}}\ket{2k}\,.

(136)

A.3
Engineering second-order Hamiltonians with negative energies

We address the question of applicability of
\operatorname{U}(1)
representations with negative charges in the context of quantum thermodynamics, where physical Hamiltonians have positive frequencies.

We can engineer negative frequencies via modulation
Caves and Schumaker 1985
;
Tsang and Caves 2012
.
Consider a pair of harmonic oscillators with frequencies
\omega_{c}\pm\delta>0
, where
\omega_{c}>0
is called the carrier frequency and
\delta>0
is called the detuning from the carrier frequency.
The total Hamiltonian is

\hat{H}(t)=(\omega_{c}+\delta)\hat{a}_{1}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{1}+(\omega_{c}-\delta)\hat{a}_{2}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{2}+f(t)i\left(e^{i2\omega_{c}t}\hat{a}_{1}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{2}^{\dagger}-e^{-i2\omega_{c}t}\hat{a}_{1}\hat{a}_{2}\right)\,,

(137)

where
f(t)
is a time-dependent real function.
In the rotating frame defined by the free Hamiltonian
\omega_{c}(\hat{a}_{1}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{1}+\hat{a}_{2}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{2})
,
\hat{H}(t)
is transformed to

\hat{H}_{0}+f(t)\hat{H}_{\text{int}}\coloneqq\delta\hat{a}_{1}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{1}-\delta\hat{a}_{2}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{2}+f(t)i\left(\hat{a}_{1}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{2}^{\dagger}-\hat{a}_{1}\hat{a}_{2}\right)\,,

(138)

which corresponds to two harmonic oscillators with frequencies
\pm\delta
.
Since the interaction Hamiltonian
f(t)\hat{H}_{\text{int}}
commutes with the intrinsic Hamiltonian
\hat{H}_{0}
, it generates an energy-conserving unitary.
More precisely, the overall realized unitary in the rotating frame is

\hat{U}(t)=\exp(-i t \hat{H}_0)\exp(-i \hat{H}_{\text{int}}\int_0^t ds f(s))\,.

(139)

Appendix B
Symplectic symmetry representations

The central objects in the theory of asymmetry are operators that remain invariant under the action of a symmetry representation
\hat{U}
and the operations that are covariant with the action of
\hat{U}
.

Definition 16
.

Let
G
be a symmetry group with unitary representation
\{\hat{U}_{A}(g):g\in G\}
in Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{A}
.

We call an operator
\hat{V}:\mathcal{H}_{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{A}
invariant under representation
\hat{U}_{A}
if

[\hat{V},\hat{U}_{A}(g)]=0\,,\quad\quad\text{ for all }g\in G\,.

(140)

Let
\{\hat{U}_{B}(g):g\in G\}
be a unitary representation of
G
in Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{B}
.

We call a quantum channel
\mathcal{E}:\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{A})\rightarrow\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{B})
covariant under the input/output representations
\hat{U}_{A},\hat{U}_{B}
if

\mathcal{E}\left(\hat{U}_{A}(g)(\cdot)\hat{U}^{\dagger}_{A}(g)\right)=\hat{U}_{B}(g)\mathcal{E}(\cdot)\hat{U}_{B}(g)^{\dagger}\,,\quad\quad\text{ for all }g\in G\,.

(141)

We may write
G
–invariant/
G
–covariant if the group representations are clear from context, or, simply invariant/covariant if the group is also clear from context.

B.1
Squeezed passive representations in phase space

A general Gaussian transformation with non-zero displacement
\hat{U}=\hat{D}_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\hat{S}
transforms the basis
\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}
as

\hat{U}^{\dagger}\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\hat{U}=S\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}-\boldsymbol{\xi}\,.

(142)

Therefore, every Gaussian representation
g\mapsto\hat{U}(g)=\hat{D}_{\boldsymbol{\xi}(g)}\hat{S}(g)
in the Hilbert space corresponds to a semi-direct product in the phase space,

S(g_{2}g_{1})=S(g_{2})S(g_{1})\quad\quad\text{and}\quad\quad\boldsymbol{\xi}(g_{2}g_{1})=S(g_{2})\boldsymbol{\xi}(g_{1})+\boldsymbol{\xi}(g_{2})\,,\quad\quad\text{for all }g_{1},g_{2}\in G\,.

(143)

When interested in symplectic representations, i.e. representations with
\boldsymbol{\xi}(g)=0
for all
g\in G
.

Recall that every symplectic representation that admits an invariant state is a squeezed passive representation
g\mapsto S(g)\overset{\boldsymbol{a}}{=}R(U(g)\oplus U(g)^{*})R^{-1}
due to Proposition
3
which is equivalent to a passive representation up to a symplectic matrix
R
.
We thus focus on passive representations.

Every passive representation
g\mapsto\hat{S}(g)
in the Hilbert space induces a unitary representation in the phase space via

g\mapsto S(g)\overset{\boldsymbol{a}}{=}U(g)\oplus U(g)^{*}\,,

(144)

where
U(g)
is unitary, for all
g\in G
.
In order to make the unitarity of the passive representation apparent, we work in the complex basis using the block form of Eq.(
144
).
To unify notation for this discussion, consider the
(1,1)
–tensor

V\overset{\boldsymbol{a}}{=}\begin{pmatrix}F&G\\
G^{*}&F^{*}\end{pmatrix}\,,

(145)

which may, for instance, represent a symplectic matrix
V
, a Hamiltonian matrix
V=\Omega H
, or a covariance matrix
V=\sigma\Omega^{-1}
.
Hence, blocks
F,G
may need to satisfy additional constraints imposed by the represented tensor, e.g.
V\Omega V^{\rm T}=\Omega
,
H=H^{\rm T}
or
\sigma=\sigma^{\rm T}
.

Using the transformation rule for
(1,1)
–tensors listed in Table
1
, we find that block
F
transforms by similarity,
F\rightarrow U(g)FU(g)^{\dagger}
, while
G
transforms by congruency,
G\rightarrow U(g)GU(g)^{\rm T}
,

\begin{pmatrix}F&G\\
G^{*}&F^{*}\end{pmatrix}\rightarrow\begin{pmatrix}U(g)&0\\
0&U(g)^{*}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}F&G\\
G^{*}&F^{*}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}U(g)&0\\
0&U(g)^{*}\end{pmatrix}^{\dagger}=\begin{pmatrix}U(g)FU(g)^{\dagger}&U(g)GU(g)^{\rm T}\\
\left(U(g)GU(g)^{\rm T}\right)^{*}&\left(U(g)FU(g)^{\dagger}\right)^{*}\end{pmatrix}\,,

(146)

for all
g\in G
.
Therefore, a
(1,1)
–tensor
V
is invariant if and only if, for all
g\in G
,

\begin{split}U(g)FU(g)^{\dagger}&=F\,,\\
U(g)GU(g)^{\rm T}&=G\,.\end{split}

(147)

Importantly, had we chosen a
(2,0)
–tensor
\begin{pmatrix}G&F\\
F^{*}&G^{*}\end{pmatrix}
instead, such as
\Omega H\Omega
or
\sigma
, we would have arrived at the same invariance conditions in Eq.(
147
) for
F
and
G
, because a
(2,0)
–tensor transforms by congruence rather than similarity.

Invariance of transposed (1,1)–tensors and inverse (2,0)–tensors

It is clear that if a
(1,1)
–tensor
V
is invariant under a squeezed passive representation, then
V^{-1}
is also invariant.
However, it is less clear whether
V^{\rm T}
is invariant.
If
V
corresponds to symplectic Gaussian unitary
\hat{V}
, then
V^{\rm T}=\Omega^{\rm T}V^{-1}\Omega
, with the transposes taken in the real basis, corresponds to symplectic Gaussian unitary

\hat{V}^{\prime}=\exp(-i\frac{\pi}{2} \hat{H}_{\rm free})\hat{V}^{-1}\exp(i\frac{\pi}{2} \hat{H}_{\rm free})

(148)

In general, if
\hat{V}
respects a passive representation of symmetry
g\mapsto O(g)
,
\hat{V}^{\prime}
also respects the symmetry, i.e.
[V,O(g)]=0
for all
g\in G
implies
[V^{\rm T},O(g)]=0
for all
g\in G
.
However, this is not generally true for squeezed passive representations.
In fact,
[V,S(g)]=0
for all
g\in G
implies that
[V^{\rm T},S^{\prime}(g)]=0
for all
g\in G
, where
g\mapsto S^{\prime}(g)=\Omega^{\rm T}S(g)\Omega
is an equivalent, but, in general, different representation of symmetry.
We provide a condition that guarantees invariance of
V^{\rm T}
under squeezed passive representations.

Proposition 17
.

Consider a group
G
with squeezed passive representation
g\mapsto S(g)=RO(g)R^{-1}
, where
R
is a symplectic matrix and
g\mapsto O(g)
is a passive representation.
Denote by
C\coloneqq{\rm comm}\{O(g):g\in G\}
the commutant of the passive representation and suppose that
R^{\rm T}R
is in the normalizer of
C
, i.e.
(R^{\rm T}R)C(R^{\rm T}R)^{-1}=C
.
Then, a
(1,1)
–tensor
V
is invariant only if
V^{\rm T}
is also invariant under
S
.

Proof.

V
is invariant under
S
, i.e.
[V,RO(g)R^{-1}]=0
, or, equivalently,

O(g)(R^{-1}VR)=(R^{-1}VR)O(g)\,,

(149)

for all
g\in G
, which implies that
R^{-1}VR\in C
.
Assuming that
R^{\rm T}R
normalizes the commutant
C
, we also have

R^{\rm T}VR^{\rm-T}=(R^{\rm T}R)(R^{-1}VR)(R^{\rm T}R)^{-1}\in C\,.

(150)

Therefore,
O(g)(R^{\rm T}VR^{-\rm T})=(R^{\rm T}VR^{-\rm T})O(g)
for all
g\in G
, which implies, by taking the transpose, that

O(g)^{\rm T}(R^{-1}V^{\rm T}R)=(R^{-1}V^{\rm T}R)O(g)^{\rm T}\,,

(151)

for all
g\in G
.
Since
g\mapsto O(g)
is an orthogonal representation, we have
O(g)^{\rm T}=O(g^{-1})
for all
g\in G
, so, by replacing
g^{-1}
with
g
in Eq.(
151
), we obtain

O(g)(R^{-1}V^{\rm T}R)=(R^{-1}V^{\rm T}R)O(g)\,,

(152)

for all
g\in G
, which is equivalent to

[V^{\rm T},RO(g)R^{-1}]=0
for all
g\in G
, i.e.
V^{\rm T}
is invariant under
S
.
∎

The statement of Proposition
17
holds also for a
(2,0)
–tensor
V
and its inverse
V^{-1}
instead of its transpose.
The assumption of Proposition
17
is trivially satisfied when
R^{\rm T}R\in C
, for example when
R=I
.
On the contrary, consider
R=V_{\rm 1sq}(r)
for some
r\neq 0
and a passive representation
g\mapsto O(\theta)
of
\operatorname{U}(1)
symmetry on 1 mode (see Fig.
2
), so that
\theta\mapsto RO(\theta)R^{-1}
admits
RR^{\rm T}
as an invariant covariance matrix, corresponding to some squeezing parameter
r
.
Due to Eq.(
44
), the relevant commutant is
C=\{m\exp(\theta\Omega):m\in\mathbb{R}\,,\theta\in[0,2\pi)\}
, so
R^{\rm T}R=V_{\rm 1sq}(2r)
does not normalize
C
.
As a consequence, the squeezed state with opposite squeezing
-r
is not invariant because its covariance matrix is the inverse
(RR^{\rm T})^{-1}
.

Complex conjugate representation

The complex conjugate representation
g\mapsto\hat{S}(g)
has associated phase space representation
g\mapsto S(g)
given by

\widetilde{S}(g)\overset{\boldsymbol{r}}{=}(I_{n}\otimes Z)S(g)(I_{n}\otimes Z)\overset{\boldsymbol{a}}{=}(X\otimes I_{n})S(g)(X\otimes I_{n})\overset{\boldsymbol{a}}{=}U(g)^{*}\oplus U(g)\,,

(153)

where
I_{n}\otimes Z
maps
\hat{x}_{j}\mapsto\hat{x}_{j}
and
\hat{p}_{j}\mapsto-\hat{p}_{j}
for all
j
in the real basis, and
X,Z
are the qubit Pauli operators.

B.2
Invariance in phase space

The transformation rules in Table
1
determine the invariance conditions under symplectic representations.

Proposition 18
.

Let
G
be a symmetry group with symplectic Gaussian representation
g\mapsto\hat{S}(g)
in Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
, and associated representation
g\mapsto S(g)
in phase space.
Then,

(i)

a second-order Hamiltonian
\hat{H}=\frac{1}{2}\hat{D}_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}^{\dagger}\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}^{\raisebox{-1.50694pt}{$\scriptstyle T$}}H\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\hat{D}_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}
is invariant if and only if

S(g)\boldsymbol{\xi}=\boldsymbol{\xi}\,,\quad\text{ and }\quad S(g)\Omega H\Omega S(g)^{\rm T}=\Omega H\Omega\,,\quad\quad\text{ for all }g\in G\,,

(154)

(ii)

a Gaussian state
\hat{\rho}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
is invariant if and only if its displacement vector
\boldsymbol{d}
and covariance matrix
\sigma
satisfy

S(g)\boldsymbol{d}=\boldsymbol{d}\,,\quad\text{ and }\quad S(g)\sigma S(g)^{\rm T}=\sigma\,,\quad\quad\text{ for all }g\in G\,,

(155)

(iii)

a Gaussian unitary
\hat{U}=\hat{D}_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\hat{V}:\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})\rightarrow\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
is invariant if and only if

S(g)\boldsymbol{\xi}=\boldsymbol{\xi}\,,\quad\text{ and }\quad S(g)VS(g)^{-1}=V\,,\quad\quad\text{ for all }g\in G\,.

(156)

Let
G
have squeezed passive representations
g\mapsto\hat{S}_{A}(g)
and
g\mapsto\hat{S}_{B}(g)
in input and output Hilbert spaces
\mathcal{H}_{A},\mathcal{H}_{B}
, with associated representations
g\mapsto S_{A}(g)
and
g\mapsto S_{B}(g)
in phase space.
Then,

(iv)

a Gaussian channel
\mathcal{E}:\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{A})\rightarrow\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{B})
with phase space action as given in Eq.(
7
) is covariant if and only if

S_{B}(g)\boldsymbol{\xi}=\boldsymbol{\xi}\,,\quad XS_{A}(g)=S_{B}(g)X\,,\quad\text{ and }\quad S_{B}(g)YS_{B}(g)^{\rm T}=Y\,,\quad\quad\text{ for all }g\in G\,.

(157)

Proof.

First, note that a displacement operator
\hat{D}_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}
is invariant under a representation
g\mapsto S(g)
if and only if vector
\boldsymbol{\xi}
is invariant, because
\hat{S}(g)\hat{D}_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\hat{S}(g)^{\dagger}=\hat{D}_{S(g)\boldsymbol{\xi}}
, for all
g\in G
.

Statement
(i)
follows directly from
\hat{S}(g)^{\dagger}\hat{H}\hat{S}(g)=\frac{1}{2}\hat{D}_{S(g)\boldsymbol{\xi}}^{\dagger}\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}^{\raisebox{-1.50694pt}{$\scriptstyle T$}}S(g)^{\rm-T}HS(g)\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\hat{D}_{S(g)\boldsymbol{\xi}}
, for all
g\in G
and the transformation rule for
H
listed in Table
1
.

Statement
(ii)
follows directly from the fact that Gaussian state
\hat{\rho}
is uniquely specified by its displacement vector
\boldsymbol{d}
and covariance matrix
\sigma
and the transformation rules for
\boldsymbol{d},\sigma
listed in Table
1
.

The direct part of statement
(iii)
, i.e. the part where we assume that
\hat{U}
is invariant, follows immediately from the transformation rule for
V
listed in Table
1
.
The converse needs care, since, in general, a symplectic matrix
V
corresponds to a symplectic Gaussian unitary
\hat{V}=e^{i\hat{H}_{1}}e^{i\hat{H}_{2}}
that cannot be expressed as a single exponential due to the non-compactness of the real symplectic group.
Consider the singular value decomposition in Eq.(
132
), which can be re-expressed as
V=O\sqrt{V^{\rm T}V}
for an orthogonal symplectic
O
and positive-definite symplectic
\sqrt{V^{\rm T}V}
.
First, note that we can write
\sqrt{V^{\rm T}V}
in terms of an exponential, i.e.
\sqrt{V^{\rm T}V}=e^{-\Omega H}
, because, by Eq.(
132
),
V^{\rm T}V\overset{\boldsymbol{r}}{=}O\left(\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}\mathrm{diag}\left(e^{2r_{j}},e^{-2r_{j}}\right)\right)O^{\rm T}
, and taking the square root gives

\sqrt{V^{\rm T}V}=\exp(O\left(\bigoplus_{j=1}^n \diag\left(r_j,-r_j\right)\right)O^{\rm T})=e^{-\Omega H}\,,

(158)

for
H\coloneqq O\left(\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}\Omega\mathrm{diag}\left(r_{j},-r_{j}\right)\right)O^{\rm T}=H^{\rm T}
where we used that
O
is orthogonal symplectic so
\Omega_{n}O=O\Omega_{n}
.

Next, consider the case when the representation
S
is passive.
Then, according to Proposition
17
,
V^{\rm T}
is invariant, and as a consequence
\sqrt{V^{\rm T}V}
and
O
are also invariant.
Therefore, we can write
O=e^{-\Omega H_{1}}
and
\sqrt{V^{\rm T}V}=e^{-\Omega H_{2}}
, for some invariant
H_{1},H_{2}
.
Then,
\hat{H}_{1},\hat{H}_{2}
are both invariant under
g\mapsto\hat{S}(g)
, and so is
\hat{V}=e^{i\hat{H}_{1}}e^{i\hat{H}_{2}}
.

If the representation
g\mapsto S(g)=RO(g)R^{-1}
is squeezed passive for a symplectic Gaussian unitary
\hat{R}
and passive representation
g\mapsto O(g)
, then
R^{-1}VR
is invariant under
g\mapsto O(g)
, so there exist
\hat{R}^{\dagger}\hat{H}_{1}\hat{R}
and
\hat{R}^{\dagger}\hat{H}_{2}\hat{R}
that are invariant under
g\mapsto\hat{O}(g)
such that
\hat{R}^{\dagger}\hat{V}\hat{R}=\exp(i\hat{R}^\dag\hat{H}_1\hat{R})\exp(i\hat{R}^\dag\hat{H}_2\hat{R})
is also invariant.
Therefore,
\hat{H}_{1},\hat{H}_{2}
and
\hat{V}=e^{i\hat{H}_{1}}e^{i\hat{H}_{2}}
are invariant under
g\mapsto\hat{S}(g)
.

Statement
(iv)
in the case of equal input/output dimensions follows directly from the fact that Gaussian channel
\hat{\mathcal{E}}
is uniquely specified by
\boldsymbol{\xi},X,Y
and their transformation rules listed in Table
1
.
The assumption of squeezed passive representation guarantees, due to Proposition
3
, that the representations admit invariant states, which is relevant in the direct part, i.e. when the channel is assumed covariant.

To prove statement
(iv)
in the general case where input and output dimensions may not be equal, let
\hat{\rho}
be a Gaussian quantum state with displacement vector
\boldsymbol{d}
and covariance matrix
\sigma
.
Consider the action of group element
g\in G
followed by the channel,

\begin{split}&\boldsymbol{d}\xrightarrow{\hat{S}_{A}(g)}S_{A}(g)\boldsymbol{d}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{E}}XS_{A}(g)\boldsymbol{d}+\boldsymbol{\xi}\eqqcolon\boldsymbol{d}_{1}^{\prime}\,,\text{ and }\\
&\sigma\xrightarrow{\hat{S}_{A}(g)}S_{A}(g)\sigma S_{A}(g)^{\rm T}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{E}}XS_{A}(g)\sigma S_{A}(g)^{\rm T}X^{\rm T}+Y\eqqcolon\sigma_{1}^{\prime}\,.\end{split}

(159)

Now consider the action of the channel followed by group element
g\in G
,

\begin{split}&\boldsymbol{d}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{E}}X\boldsymbol{d}+\boldsymbol{\xi}\xrightarrow{\hat{S}_{B}(g)}S_{B}(g)X\boldsymbol{d}+S_{B}(g)\boldsymbol{\xi}\eqqcolon\boldsymbol{d}_{2}^{\prime}\,,\text{ and }\\
&\sigma\xrightarrow{\mathcal{E}}X\sigma X^{\rm T}+Y\xrightarrow{\hat{S}_{B}(g)}S_{B}(g)X\sigma X^{\rm T}S_{B}(g)^{\rm T}+S_{B}(g)YS_{B}(g)^{\rm T}\eqqcolon\sigma_{2}^{\prime}\,.\end{split}

(160)

Assume that
\mathcal{E}
is covariant according to Definition
16
.
Then, we must have
\boldsymbol{d}_{1}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{d}_{2}^{\prime}
for arbitrary initial
\boldsymbol{d}
, leading to the conditions
S_{B}(g)\boldsymbol{\xi}=\boldsymbol{\xi}
and
XS_{A}(g)=S_{B}(g)X
.
We must also have
\sigma_{1}^{\prime}=\sigma_{2}^{\prime}
for arbitrary initial
\sigma
, which leads to the condition
S_{B}(g)YS_{B}(g)^{\rm T}=Y
.

Conversely, assume that the conditions in Eq.(
157
) hold.
Then,
\boldsymbol{d}_{1}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{d}_{2}^{\prime}
and
\sigma_{1}^{\prime}=\sigma_{2}^{\prime}
for an arbitrary initial quantum state with displacement vector
\boldsymbol{d}
and covariance matrix
\sigma
, so the Gaussian quantum channel
\mathcal{E}
is covariant according to Definition
16
.
∎

It is clear that if either Hamiltonian
\hat{H}
or state
\hat{\rho}=e^{i\beta\hat{H}}/{\rm{tr}}[e^{i\beta\hat{H}}]
at any temperature
\beta^{-1}>0
is invariant, then the other one is as well.
Moreover, if any one of the
(1,1)
–tensors,
\Omega H
or
\sigma\Omega^{-1}=i\coth\left(\frac{1}{2}i\Omega H\right)
is invariant, then the other one is as well.
As a corollary of Proposition
18
, proving one of the above invariances either in phase space or in Hilbert space, guarantees the rest.

B.3
Irrep decomposition of squeezed passive representations

Let
G
be a symmetry group with representation
\hat{U}(g)
for all
g\in G
in Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}\cong L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
of a bosonic system.
Under the action of a representation of
G
, the Hilbert space admits a decomposition of the form

L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cong\bigoplus_{\lambda}\mathbb{C}^{d_{\lambda}}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{\lambda}\,,

(161)

where there may be arbitrarily many inequivalent irreps
\lambda
, while
\mathbb{C}^{d_{\lambda}}
and
\mathcal{M}_{\lambda}
can be finite or infinite dimensional subspaces.
To characterize the asymmetry of general systems, we would need to understand the asymmetry in all these, potentially infinite, irreps.

However, the CV systems under consideration are described by a finite number
n
of modes in phase space.
A passive operator
\hat{U}
has an associated symplectic matrix
S\overset{\boldsymbol{a}}{=}U\oplus U^{*}
according to Eq.(
144
).
