Since passive representations do not mix creation and annihilation operators, the phase space decomposes identically within the block of creation operators and within the block of annihilation operators.
Therefore, we introduce a decomposition of symmetry in the linear span of annihilation operators, which is isomorphic to
\mathbb{C}^{n}
,

\mathbb{C}^{n}\cong\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\mathbb{C}^{d_{\lambda}}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{\lambda}\cong\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\mathbb{C}^{d_{\lambda}}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m(\lambda)}\,,

(162)

where
\Lambda
is the set of inequivalent irreps,
\mathbb{C}^{d_{\lambda}}
is the
d_{\lambda}
–dimensional invariant subspace of irrep
\lambda
, and
\mathcal{M}_{\lambda}
is the subspace associated with all
m(\lambda)
multiplicities of
\lambda
.
According to this decomposition, by a proper change of basis,
U(g)
decomposes in irreps as

U(g)=\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\Lambda}U_{\lambda}(g)\otimes I_{m(\lambda)}\,,\quad\text{for all }g\in G\,.

(163)

Consider a
(1,0)
–tensor
\boldsymbol{d}\overset{\boldsymbol{a}}{=}\boldsymbol{\alpha}\oplus\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{*}
in phase space.
Invariance under representation
S\overset{\boldsymbol{a}}{=}U\oplus U^{*}
means that
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=U(g)\boldsymbol{\alpha}
for all
g\in G
.
This occurs if and only if
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
contains non-zero components within the trivial subrepresentation
\lambda_{\rm trivial}
, which is a 1-dimensional irrep.
Therefore,
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
is invariant if and only if

\boldsymbol{\alpha}=\boldsymbol{0}\oplus\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\lambda_{\rm trivial}}\,,

(164)

where
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\lambda_{\rm trivial}}
is an arbitrary complex vector of
m(\lambda_{\rm trivial})
components in the multiplicity space of
\lambda_{\rm trivial}
, and
\boldsymbol{0}
is the zero vector of
n-m(\lambda_{\rm trivial})
in the compliment space
\Lambda\setminus\{\lambda_{\rm trivial}\}
.

In order to fully characterize invariance of larger phase space tensors under passive representations, we need to introduce the notion of a dual irrep.
For any irrep
\lambda\in\Lambda
, its dual
\lambda^{*}\in\Lambda^{*}\coloneqq\{\mu^{*}:\mu\in\Lambda\}
is defined such that irrep
U_{\lambda^{*}}
is equivalent to
U^{*}_{\lambda}
, i.e. there exists a matrix
B_{\lambda}
such that

U_{\lambda^{*}}(g)^{*}=B_{\lambda}^{-1}U_{\lambda}(g)B_{\lambda}\,,

(165)

for all
g\in G
.
Dual irreps have equal dimensions,
d_{\lambda^{*}}=d_{\lambda}
, so there exists a bijection between the bases of their corresponding invariant subspaces, but the corresponding multiplicity spaces are unconstrained.
Since
U_{\lambda}(g),U_{\lambda^{*}}(g)
are unitary,
B_{\lambda}
can in general be chosen to be a multiple of a unitary matrix
Fulton and Harris 2013
.

We can provide a classification of irreps in terms of their duals:

(1)

Irrep
\lambda\in\Lambda
is called
self-dual
if
\lambda^{*}\cong\lambda
, i.e. there exists an invertible matrix
B_{\lambda}
such that
U^{*}_{\lambda}(g)=B_{\lambda}^{-1}U_{\lambda}(g)B_{\lambda}
for all
g\in G
.
One can check using Schur’s lemma that, for self-dual
\lambda
,
B_{\lambda}
is either symmetric or anti-symmetric, and, additionally, if
B_{\lambda}
is symmetric, then
U_{\lambda}
is equivalent to an irrep with real matrix elements
Fulton and Harris 2013
;
Bouchard 2025
.

If
B_{\lambda}
is symmetric, the self-dual irrep
\lambda
is called
real
.
We denote the set of real irreps by
\Lambda_{\rm real}
.
Any symmetry that is reducible over the reals has real irreps, i.e.
U^{*}_{\lambda}(g)=U_{\lambda}(g)
for all
g\in G
, with the permutation group
\operatorname{S}_{n}
serving as an example.
The adjoint representation of a simple compact Lie group is also always a real irrep
Fulton and Harris 2013
;
Hall 2013
.

If
B_{\lambda}
is anti-symmetric, the self-dual irrep
\lambda
is called
pseudo-real
.
We denote the set of pseudo-real irreps by
\Lambda_{\rm pseu}
.
A prominent example of a pseudo-real irrep is
\operatorname{SU}(2)
with total angular momentum
1/2
in which case
U^{*}_{\lambda}(g)=YU_{\lambda}(g)Y
for all
g\in G
, where Pauli matrix
Y
is anti-symmetric.

(2)

Irrep
\lambda\in\Lambda
is called
complex
if
\lambda^{*}\not\cong\lambda
.
It is then relevant whether its inequivalent dual irrep
\lambda^{*}
belongs to the set of inequivalent irreps
\Lambda
.
We denote the irreps with no dual in
\Lambda
, and the irreps with an inequivalent dual pair, respectively, as

\Lambda_{\rm nd}\coloneqq\Lambda\setminus\Lambda^{*}\,,\quad\quad\Lambda_{\rm pd}\coloneqq(\Lambda\cap\Lambda^{*})\setminus(\Lambda_{\rm real}\sqcup\Lambda_{\rm pseu})\,.

(166)

The
\operatorname{U}(1)
symmetry is an important example of a symmetry with complex irreps.
Specifically,
\Lambda
contains inequivalent dual irreps of
\operatorname{U}(1)
if the representation of
\operatorname{U}(1)
is generated by a charge operator that contains charges of equal magnitude but opposite signs.
Additionally, the defining representation of
\operatorname{SU}(3)
is a complex irrep, i.e. is not equivalent to its complex conjugate.

According to this characterization, the set of inequivalent irreps naturally partitions in four disjoint subsets,

\Lambda=\Lambda_{\rm nd}\sqcup\Lambda_{\rm real}\sqcup\Lambda_{\rm pseu}\sqcup\Lambda_{\rm pd}\,.

(167)

Let us now consider an invariant
(1,1)
–tensor represented by blocks
F,G
according to Eq.(
145
).
Our goal is to decompose
F
and
G
into the irreps induced by representation
U
.
Recall that
(2,0)
–tensors and
(0,2)
–tensors can be converted into
(1,1)
–tensor by appropriate contractions with the symplectic form.

The invariance condition for component
F
as given in Eq.(
146
) is
F=U(g)FU(g)^{\dagger}
.
Denoting by
F_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}
the component of
F
that maps irrep
\lambda^{\prime}
to irrep
\lambda
, Schur’s lemma requires that

F_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}\propto I_{d_{\lambda}}\delta_{\lambda^{\prime}\lambda}\,,

(168)

for all
\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}\in\Lambda
.
This decomposition is independent of the nature of the dual irrep
\lambda^{*}
.
Accounting for multiplicities,
F
takes the following explicit form,

F=\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\Lambda}I_{d_{\lambda}}\otimes F_{\lambda}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\Lambda\\
s,\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\end{subarray}}F_{\lambda,\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}\outerproduct{\lambda, s, \alpha}{\lambda, s, \alpha'}\,,

(169)

where index
s=1,\dots,d_{\lambda}
labels a basis for irrep
\lambda
of dimension
d_{\lambda}
, and indices
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}=1,\dots,m(\lambda)
label a basis in the multiplicity space of irrep
\lambda
of dimension
m(\lambda)
.
This fully characterizes an invariant
(1,1)
–tensor in the case of
G=0
, which may correspond to absence of squeezing in an appropriate context.

The invariance condition for component
G
as given in Eq.(
146
) is
G=U(g)GU(g)^{\rm T}
.
Denoting by
G_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}
the component of
G
that maps irrep
\lambda^{\prime}
to irrep
\lambda
, Schur’s lemma requires that

G_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}\propto\begin{cases}0\,,&\text{for }\lambda\in\Lambda_{\rm nd}\,,\\
B_{\lambda}\delta_{\lambda^{\prime}\lambda}\,,&\text{for }\lambda\in\Lambda_{\rm real}\sqcup\Lambda_{\rm pseu}\,,\\
B_{\lambda}\delta_{\lambda^{\prime}\lambda^{*}}\,,&\text{for }\lambda\in\Lambda_{\rm pd}\,.\end{cases}

(170)

Block
B_{\lambda}
is diagonal, symmetric and unitary for each real irrep
\lambda\in\Lambda_{\rm real}
, and diagonal, anti-symmetric and unitary for each pseudo-real irrep
\lambda\in\Lambda_{\rm pseu}
.
For each inequivalent dual pair
\lambda,\lambda^{*}\in\Lambda_{\rm pd}
, unitary block
B_{\lambda}=B_{\lambda^{*}}^{\rm T}
is off-diagonal with respect to the irrep bases of
\lambda
and
\lambda^{*}
.
Accounting for multiplicities,
G
takes the following explicit form,

G=\hskip-12.0pt\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\in\Lambda_{\rm real}\sqcup\Lambda_{\rm pseu}\\
s,s^{\prime},\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\hskip-15.0ptB_{\lambda,s,s^{\prime}}G_{\lambda,\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}\outerproduct{\lambda, s, \alpha}{\lambda, s', \alpha'}\hskip 5.0pt+\hskip-6.0pt\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda,\lambda^{*}\in\Lambda_{\rm pd}\\
s,s^{\prime},\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\hskip-7.0pt\left(B_{\lambda,s,s^{\prime}}G_{\lambda,\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}\outerproduct{\lambda, s, \alpha}{\lambda^*, s', \alpha'}+B_{\lambda,s,s^{\prime}}^{\rm T}G_{\lambda^{*},\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}\outerproduct{\lambda^*, s', \alpha'}{\lambda, s, \alpha}\right)\,,

(171)

where
s,s^{\prime}=1,\dots,d_{\lambda}
,
\alpha=1,\dots,m(\lambda)
, and
\alpha^{\prime}=1,\dots,m(\lambda^{*})
.
Note that for a self-dual irrep
\lambda
,
m(\lambda^{*})=m(\lambda)
.

We summarize the above analysis in the following statement, where we provide a full characterization of the irrep decompositions of blocks
F
and
G
under passive representations.

Proposition 19
.

Let
G
be a symmetry group with passive representation
g\mapsto S(g)\overset{\boldsymbol{a}}{=}U(g)\oplus U(g)^{*}
in phase space.

There exists a basis, called the irrep basis, such that the unitary
U(g)
decomposes in irreps as

U(g)=\left(\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\Lambda\setminus\Lambda_{\rm pd}}U_{\lambda}(g)\otimes I_{m(\lambda)}\right)\oplus\left(\bigoplus_{\lambda,\lambda^{*}\in\Lambda_{\rm pd}}\begin{pmatrix}U_{\lambda}\otimes I_{m(\lambda)}&0\\[5.0pt]
0&U_{\lambda^{*}}\otimes I_{m(\lambda^{*})}\end{pmatrix}\right)\,,

(172)

where every inequivalent irrep
\lambda\in\Lambda
has dimension
d_{\lambda}
, and multiplicity
m(\lambda)
.

Every invariant
(1,0)
–tensor
\boldsymbol{d}\overset{\boldsymbol{a}}{=}\boldsymbol{\alpha}\oplus\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{*}
is expressed in the irrep basis as

\boldsymbol{\alpha}=\boldsymbol{0}\oplus\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\lambda_{\rm trivial}}\,,

(173)

where
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\lambda_{\rm trivial}}
is an arbitrary vector in the trivial irrep
\lambda_{\rm trivial}
, and
\boldsymbol{0}
is the zero vector in
\Lambda\setminus\{\lambda_{\rm trivial}\}
.

Every invariant
(1,1)
–tensor
\begin{pmatrix}F&G\\
G^{*}&F^{*}\end{pmatrix}
and
(2,0)
–tensor
\begin{pmatrix}G&F\\
F^{*}&G^{*}\end{pmatrix}
are represented by blocks
F
and
G
expressed in the irrep basis as

F=\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\Lambda}I_{d_{\lambda}}\otimes F_{\lambda}\,,\quad\quad G=\left(\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{\rm real}\sqcup\Lambda_{\rm pseu}}B_{\lambda}\otimes G_{\lambda}\right)\oplus\left(\bigoplus_{\lambda,\lambda^{*}\in\Lambda_{\rm pd}}\begin{pmatrix}0&B_{\lambda}\otimes G_{\lambda}\\[5.0pt]
B_{\lambda}^{\rm T}\otimes G_{\lambda^{*}}&0\end{pmatrix}\right)\,,

(174)

where
F_{\lambda},G_{\lambda}
and
G_{\lambda^{*}}
are complex matrices of appropriate dimensions and
B_{\lambda}
are determined by the symmetry conditions
U_{\lambda^{*}}(g)=B_{\lambda}^{-1}U_{\lambda}(g)B_{\lambda}
for all
\lambda
such that
\lambda^{*}\in\Lambda
.

Note that this decomposition can be readily employed when dealing with squeezed passive representations of the form
R(U(g)\oplus U(g)^{*})R^{-1}
, where
R
is a symplectic matrix that may contain squeezing, as equivalent representations have the same irrep decomposition up to a basis change by
R
.

B.4
Irrep decomposition of invariant Gaussian symplectic Gaussian unitaries and quantum states

Proposition
19
has direct implications on the structure of invariant symplectic and covariance matrices under a squeezed passive representation
g\mapsto R(U(g)\oplus U(g)^{*})R^{-1}
of a symmetry group
G
, with symplectic
R
, and in particular on the entanglement between modes.
We focus on passive representation
g\mapsto U(g)\oplus U(g)^{*}
and recall that the invariant unitaries and states of the squeezed passive representation can be obtained by the invariant states of the passive representation via a basis transformation by
\hat{R}
.

Any symplectic Gaussian unitary
\hat{S}
cannot correlate modes carrying irreps of different type among the four types that appear in Eq.(
167
).
The symplectic matrix
S\overset{\boldsymbol{a}}{=}\begin{pmatrix}F&G\\
G^{*}&F^{*}\end{pmatrix}
associated with
\hat{S}
is expressed in the irrep basis according to Eq.(
174
), where, in order to satisfy the symplectic matrix constraints
FF^{\dagger}-GG^{\dagger}=I
and
FG^{\rm T}-GF^{\rm T}=0
, we require that

(i)

F_{\lambda}
is a unitary matrix for all
\lambda\in\Lambda_{\rm nd}
,

(ii)

F_{\lambda}F_{\lambda}^{\dagger}-G_{\lambda}G_{\lambda}^{\dagger}=I_{m(\lambda)}
for all
\lambda\in\Lambda\setminus\Lambda_{\rm nd}
,

(iii)

F_{\lambda}G_{\lambda}^{\rm T}-G_{\lambda}F_{\lambda}^{\rm T}=0
for all
\lambda\in\Lambda_{\rm real}
,

(iv)

F_{\lambda}G_{\lambda}^{\rm T}+G_{\lambda}F_{\lambda}^{\rm T}=0
for all
\lambda\in\Lambda_{\rm pseu}
,

(v)

F_{\lambda}G_{\lambda^{*}}^{\rm T}-G_{\lambda}F_{\lambda^{*}}^{\rm T}=0
for all
\lambda,\lambda^{*}\in\Lambda_{\rm pd}
.

Analogously, any invariant state
\hat{\rho}
does not contain correlations between modes carrying irreps of different type among the four types that appear in Eq.(
167
).
Concretely,

\hat{\rho}={\rm{tr}}_{\Lambda\setminus\Lambda_{\rm nd}}[\hat{\rho}]\otimes{\rm{tr}}_{\Lambda\setminus\Lambda_{\rm real}}[\hat{\rho}]\otimes{\rm{tr}}_{\Lambda\setminus\Lambda_{\rm pseu}}[\hat{\rho}]\otimes{\rm{tr}}_{\Lambda\setminus\Lambda_{\rm pd}}[\hat{\rho}]\,.

(175)

The symmetry necessitates entanglement between inequivalent dual irreps, and allows for arbitrary entanglement within multiplicity subspaces, but all other irrep subspaces are uncorrelated with each other.

Consider the decomposition of
U(g)
in the irrep basis given in Eq.(
172
).
The displacement vector
\boldsymbol{d}\overset{\boldsymbol{a}}{=}\boldsymbol{\alpha}\oplus\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{*}
of invariant state
\hat{\rho}
is expressed in the irrep basis via Eq.(
173
), meaning that it is non-zero only on modes where the symmetry acts trivially.
The covariance matrix
\sigma\overset{\boldsymbol{a}}{=}\begin{pmatrix}G&F\\
F^{*}&G^{*}\end{pmatrix}
of
\hat{\rho}
is expressed in the irrep basis according to Eq.(
174
), where, in order to satisfy the covariance matrix constraints
F=F^{\dagger}
and
G=G^{\rm T}
, we require that

(i)

F_{\lambda}
is a
(m(\lambda)\times m(\lambda))
Hermitian matrix for all
\lambda\in\Lambda
,

(ii)

G_{\lambda}
is a
(m(\lambda)\times m(\lambda))
complex symmetric matrix for all
\lambda\in\Lambda_{\rm real}
,

(iii)

G_{\lambda}
is a
(m(\lambda)\times m(\lambda))
complex anti-symmetric matrix for all
\lambda\in\Lambda_{\rm pseu}
,

(iv)

G_{\lambda}=G_{\lambda^{*}}^{\rm T}
is a
(m(\lambda)\times m(\lambda^{*}))
complex matrix, for pair
\lambda,\lambda^{*}\in\Lambda_{\rm pd}
.

We can therefore decompose the covariance matrix in the irrep basis as

\sigma=\left(\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{\rm nd}}\sigma_{\lambda}\right)\oplus\left(\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{\rm real}\sqcup\Lambda_{\rm pseu}}\sigma_{\lambda}\right)\oplus\left(\bigoplus_{\lambda,\lambda^{*}\in\Lambda_{\rm pd}}\sigma_{\lambda}\right)\,,

(176)

where the restriction of
\sigma
within each disjoint irrep subspace takes different forms.

For
\lambda\in\Lambda_{\rm nd}
, the invariance of
\sigma
ensures that there is no squeezing, so

\sigma_{\lambda}\overset{\boldsymbol{a}}{=}\begin{pmatrix}0&I_{d_{\lambda}}\otimes F_{\lambda}\\
I_{d_{\lambda}}\otimes F_{\lambda}^{*}&0\end{pmatrix}\,.

(177)

For
\lambda\in\Lambda_{\rm real}\sqcup\Lambda_{\rm pseu}
, squeezing is generally allowed,

\sigma_{\lambda}\overset{\boldsymbol{a}}{=}\begin{pmatrix}B_{\lambda}\otimes G_{\lambda}&I_{d_{\lambda}}\otimes F_{\lambda}\\
I_{d_{\lambda}}\otimes F_{\lambda}^{*}&B_{\lambda}^{*}\otimes G_{\lambda}^{*}\end{pmatrix}\,,

(178)

For inequivalent dual pairs
\lambda,\lambda^{*}\in\Lambda_{\rm pd}
, entanglement between the irrep subspaces is also generally present,

\sigma_{\lambda}\overset{\boldsymbol{a}}{=}\begin{pmatrix}0&I_{d_{\lambda}}\otimes F_{\lambda}&B_{\lambda}\otimes G_{\lambda}&0\\[2.0pt]
I_{d_{\lambda}}\otimes F_{\lambda}^{*}&0&0&B_{\lambda}^{*}\otimes G_{\lambda}^{*}\\[2.0pt]
B_{\lambda}^{\rm T}\otimes G_{\lambda}^{\rm T}&0&0&I_{d_{\lambda}}\otimes F_{\lambda_{*}}\\[2.0pt]
0&B_{\lambda}^{\dagger}\otimes G_{\lambda}^{\dagger}&I_{d_{\lambda}}\otimes F_{\lambda^{*}}^{*}&0\end{pmatrix}\,.

(179)

B.5
Examples of passive representation decompositions

For the sake of concreteness, we provide examples of Gaussian operators that are invariant under specific symmetries of interest.
Using Proposition
19
, we can also immediately derive invariant tensors in phase space.

Trivial symmetry.

In the case of no symmetry, the trivial representation corresponds to
\Lambda=\{\lambda_{\rm trivial}\}
, where
\lambda_{\rm trivial}
is a 1-dimensional, self-dual irrep with multiplicity equal to the number of total modes of the system,
m(\lambda)=n
.

\operatorname{SU}(2)
symmetry.

Recall the Schwinger representation
\hat{S}(U):U\in\operatorname{SU}(2)
from Eq.(
28
).
This is a pseudo-real irrep satisfying
U^{*}=\Omega^{\rm T}U\Omega
.
An
\operatorname{SU}(2)
–invariant covariance matrix
\sigma
on 2 modes takes the form

\sigma\overset{\boldsymbol{a}}{=}\begin{pmatrix}0&F_{1}I_{2}\\
F_{1}I_{2}&0\end{pmatrix}\,,

(180)

where
I_{2}
is the
(2\times 2)
identity matrix, and
F_{1}>1
is a real number.
Recall that in the case of a pseudo-real irrep, the non-passive block is
B_{\lambda}\otimes G_{\lambda}
for an anti-symmetric matrix
G_{\lambda}
.
Here,
B_{\lambda}=\Omega
and
G_{\lambda}
is a 1-dimensional anti-symmetric matrix, so it must equal 0.
Hence, the only 2–mode
\operatorname{SU}(2)
–invariant Gaussian states are two uncorrelated modes at equal temperature.
Similarly, one finds that the only 2–mode
\operatorname{SU}(2)
–invariant Gaussian unitary is the identity operator.

A less trivial example arises when we consider the group
\operatorname{SU}(2)
on 6 modes, with representation

U=U(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)\otimes I_{3}\,.

(181)

Therefore, the 6–mode
\operatorname{SU}(2)
–invariant covariance matrix
\sigma
takes the form

\displaystyle\sigma\overset{\boldsymbol{a}}{=}\begin{pmatrix}\Omega\otimes G_{3}&I_{2}\otimes F_{3}\\
I_{2}\otimes F_{3}^{*}&\Omega\otimes G_{3}^{*}\end{pmatrix}\,,

(182)

where
F_{3}\geq I_{3}
is a
(3\times 3)
Hermitian matrix and
G_{3}
is a
(3\times 3)
anti-symmetric matrix.

\operatorname{U}(1)
symmetry.

Consider
\operatorname{U}(1)
symmetry on 3 modes, with representation
\hat{U}(t)=e^{it\hat{H}}
, where
\hat{H}=\omega\hat{a}_{1}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{1}+\omega\hat{a}_{2}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{2}-\omega\hat{a}_{3}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{3}
for some rational
\omega>0
.
The phase space representation
S(t)\overset{\boldsymbol{a}}{=}U(t)\oplus U^{*}(t)
, with

U(t)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc|c}&&0\\
\lx@intercol\hfil\smash{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$e^{i\omega t}I_{2}$}}\hfil\lx@intercol\vrule\lx@intercol&0\\
\hline\cr&&\\[-10.00002pt]
0&0&e^{-i\omega t}\end{array}\right)\,,

(183)

in the irrep basis, contains two distinct 1-dimensional irreps
U_{\lambda}(t)=e^{i\omega t}
and
U_{\lambda^{*}}(t)=e^{-i\omega t}
, which are dual to each other.
Irrep
\lambda
has multiplicity 2.

For any invariant matrix, we can directly use our analysis to express components
F
and
G
in the irrep basis,

\displaystyle F=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc|c}&&0\\
\lx@intercol\hfil\smash{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$F_{2}$}}\hfil\lx@intercol\vrule\lx@intercol&0\\
\hline\cr&&\\[-10.00002pt]
0&0&F_{1}\end{array}\right),\quad\quad G

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc|c}0&0&\\
0&0&\lx@intercol\hfil\smash{\raisebox{6.0pt}{$G_{1}$}}\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\hline\cr&&\\[-10.00002pt]
\lx@intercol\hfil G_{1}^{\rm T}\hfil\lx@intercol\vrule\lx@intercol&0\end{array}\right)\,,

where
F_{2}
is a
(2\times 2)
Hermitian matrix,
F_{1}
is a real number, and
G_{1}
is a
(2\times 1)
complex matrix.

\operatorname{S}_{3}
and
\mathbb{Z}_{3}
symmetries and composite representations.

Consider an interaction Hamiltonian
\hat{H}_{AB}^{\rm int}
between system
A
with
n_{A}
modes and representation
g\mapsto S_{A}(g)\overset{\boldsymbol{a}}{=}U_{A}(g)\oplus U_{A}(g)^{*}
, and system
B
with
n_{B}
modes and representation
g\mapsto S_{B}(g)\overset{\boldsymbol{a}}{=}U_{B}(g)\oplus U_{B}(g)^{*}
.
The transformation
\hat{H}_{AB}^{\rm int}\mapsto(\hat{S}_{A}\otimes\hat{S}_{B})\hat{H}_{AB}^{\rm int}(\hat{S}_{A}\otimes\hat{S}_{B})^{\dagger}
reads

\hat{H}_{AB}^{\rm int}=\sum_{j=1}^{n_{A}}\sum_{k=1}^{n_{B}}\left(F_{jk}\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{j}\hat{b}_{k}+G_{jk}\hat{a}_{j}\hat{b}_{k}\right)+\text{H.c.}\mapsto\sum_{j,j^{\prime}=1}^{n_{A}}\sum_{k,k^{\prime}=1}^{n_{B}}\left(F_{jk}U_{A,j^{\prime}j}U^{*}_{B,k^{\prime}k}\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{j}\hat{b}_{k}+G_{jk}U_{A,j^{\prime}j}U_{B,k^{\prime}k}\hat{a}_{j}\hat{b}_{k}\right)+\text{H.c.}\,.

(192)

Therefore,
\hat{H}_{AB}^{\rm int}
is invariant under the symmetry if
U_{A}(g)FU_{B}(g)^{\dagger}=F
and
U_{A}(g)GU_{B}(g)^{\rm T}=G
, for all
g\in G
.
These conditions can be vectorized to

(U_{A}(g)\otimes U_{B}(g)^{*}-I_{A}\otimes I_{B})\ket{{\rm vec}(F)}=(U_{A}(g)\otimes U_{B}(g)-I_{A}\otimes I_{B})\ket{{\rm vec}(G)}=0\,,

(193)

where
I_{A},I_{B}
denote the identity on systems
A,B
.
If
U_{B}(g)=U_{B}(g)^{*}
is real, the conditions for
F
and
G
are identical.

Consider the
\operatorname{S}_{3}
symmetry on the composite system highlighted in Fig.
1
, with elements
\{e,r,r^{2},t_{1},t_{2},t_{3}\}
, where
e,r,r^{2}
indicate rotations and
t_{1},t_{2},t_{3}
indicate two-element transpositions.
The standard 2-dimensional irreducible representation of
\operatorname{S}_{3}
is

\displaystyle O_{2}(e)=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\,,\quad O_{2}(r)=\begin{pmatrix}-\frac{1}{2}&-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\\[5.0pt]
\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}&-\frac{1}{2}\end{pmatrix}\,,\quad O_{2}(r^{2})=\begin{pmatrix}-\frac{1}{2}&\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\\[5.0pt]
-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}&-\frac{1}{2}\end{pmatrix}\,,

\displaystyle O_{2}(t_{1})=\begin{pmatrix}-1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\,,\quad O_{2}(t_{2})=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{2}&\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\\[5.0pt]
\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}&-\frac{1}{2}\end{pmatrix}\,,\quad O_{2}(t_{3})=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{2}&-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\\[5.0pt]
-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}&-\frac{1}{2}\end{pmatrix}\,.

(194)

whereas the 3-dimensional natural representation of
\operatorname{S}_{3}
is

\displaystyle O_{3}(e)=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix}\,,\quad O_{3}(r)=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&1\\
1&0&0\\
0&1&0\end{pmatrix}\,,\quad O_{3}(r^{2})=\begin{pmatrix}0&1&0\\
0&0&1\\
1&0&0\end{pmatrix}\,,

\displaystyle O_{3}(t_{1})=\begin{pmatrix}0&1&0\\
1&0&0\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix}\,,\quad O_{3}(t_{2})=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&0&1\\
0&1&0\end{pmatrix}\,,\quad O_{3}(t_{3})=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&1\\
0&1&0\\
1&0&0\end{pmatrix}\,.

(195)

We will ignore the self-interactions of each subsystem, and focus on determining the interaction parts
F_{\rm int}
and
G_{\rm int}
, where
[F_{\rm int}]_{jk}
and
[G_{\rm int}]_{jk}
are the coefficients of terms
\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger}\hat{b}_{k}
and
\hat{a}_{j}\hat{b}_{k}
, respectively.

First, suppose systems
A
and
B
have 3 modes, and representations
U_{A}=U_{B}=O_{3}
.
Then, solving Eq.(
193
) gives

F_{\rm int}=f_{1}\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix}+f_{2}\begin{pmatrix}0&1&1\\
1&0&1\\
1&1&0\end{pmatrix}\,,\quad\quad G_{\rm int}=g_{1}\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix}+g_{2}\begin{pmatrix}0&1&1\\
1&0&1\\
1&1&0\end{pmatrix}\,,

(196)

for
f_{1},f_{2},g_{1},g_{2}\in\mathbb{C}
.
Therefore, every invariant Hamiltonian interaction is of the form

\hat{H}_{AB}^{\rm int}=\sum_{k=1}^{3}\left(f_{1}\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{k}\hat{b}_{k}+g_{1}\hat{a}_{k}\hat{b}_{k}\right)+\sum_{k=1}^{3}\left(f_{2}\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{k}\hat{b}_{k+1}+f_{2}\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{k}\hat{b}_{k+2}+g_{2}\hat{a}_{k}\hat{b}_{k+1}+g_{2}\hat{a}_{k}\hat{b}_{k+2}\right)+\text{H.c.}\,,

(197)

where subscript addition is considered mod 3.
Suppose system
C
has 2 modes and representation
U_{C}=O_{2}
.
Then,

F_{\rm int}=f\begin{pmatrix}1&\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\\[5.0pt]
-1&\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\\[5.0pt]
0&-\frac{2}{\sqrt{3}}\end{pmatrix}\,,\quad\quad G_{\rm int}=g\begin{pmatrix}1&\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\\[5.0pt]
-1&\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\\[5.0pt]
0&-\frac{2}{\sqrt{3}}\end{pmatrix}\,,

(198)

for
f,g\in\mathbb{C}
, so every invariant Hamiltonian interaction is of the form

\hat{H}_{AC}^{\rm int}=f\left(\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{1}\hat{b}_{1}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{1}\hat{b}_{2}-\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{2}\hat{b}_{1}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{2}\hat{b}_{2}-\frac{2}{\sqrt{3}}\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{3}\hat{b}_{2}\right)+g\left(\hat{a}_{1}\hat{b}_{1}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\hat{a}_{1}\hat{b}_{2}-\hat{a}_{2}\hat{b}_{1}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\hat{a}_{2}\hat{b}_{2}-\frac{2}{\sqrt{3}}\hat{a}_{3}\hat{b}_{2}\right)+\text{H.c.}\,.

(199)

Considering the subgroup
\mathbb{Z}_{3}=\{e,r,r^{2}\}
on system
AC
leads to different invariant Hamiltonian interaction, with

F_{\rm int}=f_{1}\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\[5.0pt]
-\frac{1}{2}&\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\\[5.0pt]
-\frac{1}{2}&-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\end{pmatrix}+f_{2}\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\[5.0pt]
-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}&-\frac{1}{2}\\[5.0pt]
\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}&-\frac{1}{2}\end{pmatrix}\,,

(200)

for
f_{1},f_{2}\in\mathbb{C}
, so that all invariant terms of the form
[F_{\rm int}]_{jk}\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{j}\hat{b}_{k}
are

f_{1}\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{1}\hat{b}_{1}+f_{2}\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{1}\hat{b}_{2}+\left(-\frac{1}{2}f_{1}-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}f_{2}\right)\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{2}\hat{b}_{1}+\left(\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}f_{1}-\frac{1}{2}f_{2}\right)\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{2}\hat{b}_{2}+\left(-\frac{1}{2}f_{1}+\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}f_{2}\right)\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{3}\hat{b}_{1}+\left(-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}f_{1}-\frac{1}{2}f_{2}\right)\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{3}\hat{b}_{2}+\text{H.c.}\,.

(201)

Here, we are not considering the non-passive terms
G_{\rm int}
.

B.6
Local decomposition of U(1)–invariant Gaussian unitaries

We prove that U(1)–invariant Gaussian unitaries decompose to 2–local U(1)–invariant Gaussian unitaries.
See
2

Proof.

Recall the general
\operatorname{U}(1)
representation
\hat{O}(\theta)=\bigotimes_{j=1}^{n}e^{i\theta q_{j}\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{j}\hat{a}_{j}}
with integer charges
q_{j}\neq 0
.
A Hamiltonian
\hat{H}=-i\sum_{j,k}\left(F_{jk}\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{k}+G_{jk}\hat{a}_{j}\hat{a}_{k}\right)+\text{H.c.}
is invariant under
\hat{O}
if and only if
F_{jk}=e^{i(q_{k}-q_{j})}F_{jk}
and
G_{jk}=e^{i(q_{k}+q_{j})}G_{jk}
, so we can decompose any invariant Hamiltonian as

\displaystyle i\hat{H}=\sum_{j}F_{jj}\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{j}\ +\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j,k:j\neq k\\
q_{j}=q_{k}\end{subarray}}F_{jk}\left(\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{k}-\hat{a}_{j}\hat{a}_{k}^{\dagger}\right)+\sum_{q_{j}=-q_{k}}G_{jk}\left(\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{k}^{\dagger}-\hat{a}_{j}\hat{a}_{k}\right)\,.

(202)

The Lie algebra of invariant Hamiltonians is therefore spanned and hence generated by phase-shifters, beam-splitters between sectors of equal charge and 2–mode squeezers between sectors of opposite charge.
According to Proposition
18
, the group of
G
–invariant symplectic transformations is connected.
As a consequence, the Lie group of U(1)–invariant symplectic matrices is generated by exponentials of multiples of these generators.
∎

As an example, suppose we have two equal charges, i.e.
Q=q(I\oplus I)
.
Then, any invariant covariance matrix
\sigma
as given by Eq.(
44
) decomposes as

\displaystyle\sigma=(V_{\rm ps}(\phi)\hskip-1.0pt\oplus\hskip-1.0ptI)V_{\rm bs}(\phi^{\prime})\hskip-2.0pt\begin{pmatrix}\nu_{1}I&\hskip-4.0pt0\\
0&\hskip-4.0pt\nu_{2}I\end{pmatrix}\hskip-2.0ptV_{\rm bs}(\phi^{\prime})^{\rm T}(V_{\rm ps}(\phi)\hskip-1.0pt\oplus\hskip-1.0ptI)^{\rm T}\,,

for symplectic eigenvalues
\nu_{1},\nu_{2}>1
, and parameters
\phi=\tan^{-1}(\theta_{12})
, and
\phi^{\prime}=\frac{1}{2}\tan^{-1}\left(\frac{2a_{12}^{2}}{a_{11}-a_{22}}\right)
.

On the other hand, suppose that we have two opposite charges, i.e.
Q=q(I\oplus-I)
.
Then,
\sigma
decomposes as

\displaystyle\sigma=(V_{\rm ps}(\phi)\hskip-0.5pt\oplus\hskip-0.5ptI)V_{\rm 2sq}(r)\hskip-2.0pt\begin{pmatrix}\nu_{1}I&\hskip-4.0pt0\\
0&\hskip-4.0pt\nu_{2}I\end{pmatrix}\hskip-2.0ptV_{\rm 2sq}(r)^{\rm T}\hskip-1.5pt(V_{\rm ps}(\phi)\hskip-0.5pt\oplus\hskip-0.5ptI)^{\rm T}\,,

for symplectic eigenvalues
\nu_{1},\nu_{2}>1
, and parameters
\phi=-\tan^{-1}(\theta_{12})
, and
r=-\frac{1}{2}\tanh^{-1}\left(\frac{2a_{12}^{2}}{a_{11}+a_{22}}\right)
.

We highlight that in the case of equal charges, a beam-splitter was used for the decomposition, whereas in the case of opposite charges, a 2–mode squeezer was used instead.
It is natural to ask if there is a systematic way to decompose U(1)–invariant symplectic matrices on an arbitrary number of modes.
If all charges
q_{j}=q
are equal, then invariant symplectic matrices coincide with passive symplectic matrices, and there exists a known algorithm
Reck et al. 1994
that decomposes them into phase-shifters and beam-splitters.
In the context of symmetries, this algorithm hinges on the fact that the symplectic form
\Omega_{n}
is proportional to the charge
Q\Omega_{n}=q\Omega_{n}
, so the conditions for a matrix to be symplectic and U(1)–invariant coincide.
This reduces the problem into decomposing an
(n\times n)
unitary that mixes annihilation operators into
(2\times 2)
unitaries and phases.
If at least two charges have different signs, then the conditions for a matrix to be symplectic and U(1)–invariant no longer coincide, so the algorithm cannot be easily extended to the general case of
\operatorname{U}(1)
symmetry.
We leave the task of finding such an algorithm as an open question.

B.7
Squeezed passive representations as symplectic representations that admit invariant states

We prove that all symplectic representations that admit an invariant state are equivalent to a passive representation, and further admit a pure invariant state.
As noted in the main text, the first statement on compact groups is an immediate consequence of the Cartan-Iwasawa-Malcev theorem
Malcev 1945
;
Iwasawa 1949
;
Onishchik and Vinberg 2012
.
Here, we present a self-contained proof.
See
3

Proof.

We first prove statements (i) and (iii).
Compact groups always admit an invariant quantum state,

\sigma=r\int{\rm d}g\ S(g)S(g)^{\rm T}\,.

(203)

Indeed,
\sigma
is symmetric,
G
–invariant, i.e.
S(g)\sigma S(g)^{\rm T}=\sigma
for all
g\in G
, and satisfies the uncertainty relation
\sigma+i\Omega\geq 0
for sufficiently high
r>0
.

In both cases of a compact and non-compact group
G
, given a covariance matrix
\sigma
that remains invariant under the action of symmetry, we can obtain a covariance matrix
\sigma_{\infty}
of a pure state by applying the transformation
\lim_{\alpha\rightarrow\infty}\sigma_{\alpha}\coloneqq\coth(\alpha\coth^{-1}(\sigma i\Omega))i\Omega
.
Here, if
\sigma
is the covariance matrix of Gaussian state
e^{-\beta\hat{H}}/{\rm{tr}}[e^{-\beta\hat{H}}]
, then
\sigma_{\alpha}
is the covariance matrix of a Gaussian state with the same Hamiltonian
\hat{H}
and temperature
(\alpha\beta)^{-1}
.
Finally,
\sqrt{\sigma_{\infty}}
is a symplectic transformation as discussed in the proof of Proposition
6
.
Choosing
R=\sqrt{\sigma_{\infty}}
completes the proof.

We now prove statement (ii).
The symplectic transformation
Q=\widetilde{R}^{-1}R
satisfies
\widetilde{O}(g)=QO(g)Q^{-1}
.
Consider the Bloch-Messiah decomposition
Q=O_{2}DO_{1}
(according to Eq.(
132
)), where
D
is a positive-definite diagonal symplectic matrix, and
O_{1}
and
O_{2}
are orthogonal symplectic.
Define the orthogonal transformation
I(g)\coloneqq O_{1}O(g)O_{1}^{\rm T}
.
Then,

\widetilde{O}(g)=(O_{2}DO_{1})O(g)(O_{2}DO_{1})^{-1}=O_{2}DI(g)D^{-1}O_{2}^{\rm T}

(204)

is orthogonal if and only if
DI(g)D^{-1}
is orthogonal, i.e.

(DI(g)D^{-1})(DI(g)D^{-1})^{\rm T}=I\,,

(205)

or equivalently,

DI(g)D^{-2}I(g)^{\rm T}D=I\,,

(206)

where we have used the fact that
D
is diagonal, and therefore
D=D^{\rm T}
.
This equivalently means that
[D^{-2},I(g)]=0
, or, since
D
is positive-definite,
[D,I(g)]=0
, for all
g\in G
.
Together with Eq.(
204
), we get

\widetilde{O}(g)=O_{2}DI(g)D^{-1}O_{2}^{\rm T}=O_{2}I(g)O_{2}^{\rm T}=(O_{2}O_{1})O(g)(O_{2}O_{1})^{\rm T}\,,

(207)

where
T\coloneqq O_{2}O_{1}
is orthogonal and symplectic, concluding the proof.
∎

An important corollary of Proposition
3
is that symplectically equivalent representations have the same irrep multiplicities.

Proposition 20
.

Consider two
G
–invariant symplectic subspaces
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
with equal dimensions, symplectic forms
\Omega_{1}
and
\Omega_{2}
, and squeezed passive representations
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
, respectively.
Suppose there exists a symplectic intertwiner
V:F_{1}\rightarrow F_{2}
, such that
V^{\rm T}\Omega_{2}V=\Omega_{1}
, and
VS_{1}(g)=S_{2}(g)V
for all
g\in G
.
Then, for each irrep
\lambda
of group
G
in the associated unitary representation in the space of annihilation (or creation) operators, the multiplicity
m(\lambda)
, as defined by Eq.(
172
), is identical for
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
.

Conversely, if all multiplicities
m(\lambda)
are identical for two symplectic representations
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
, then the representations are equivalent up to a similarity transformation by a symplectic matrix
V
, i.e.
S_{2}(g)=V^{-1}S_{1}(g)V:g\in G
. If
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
are both symplectic and orthogonal, then
V
can also be chosen to be symplectic and orthogonal.

Proof.

Consider the decomposition
S_{1}(g)=R_{1}O_{1}(g)R_{1}^{-1}
for symplectic matrix
R_{1}
and orthogonal symplectic representation
O_{1}(g):g\in G
, with decomposition

O_{1}(g)\overset{\boldsymbol{a}}{=}U_{1}(g)\oplus U_{1}(g)^{*}\,.

(208)

A similar decomposition exists for
S_{2}(g)
, with
S_{2}(g)=R_{2}O_{2}(g)R_{2}^{-1}
, and
O_{2}(g)\overset{\boldsymbol{a}}{=}U_{2}(g)\oplus U_{2}(g)^{*}
.

Since
S_{2}(g)=VS_{1}(g)V^{-1}=(VR)O_{1}(g)(VR)^{-1}
, and
V
is symplectic, the second part of Proposition
3
implies that there exists an orthogonal symplectic transformation
\widetilde{O}
, such that

O_{2}(g)=\widetilde{O}O_{1}(g)\widetilde{O}^{\rm T}\,,

(209)

for all
g\in G
.
Writing this in the complex basis, we find that there exists a unitary transformation
\tilde{U}
such that

U_{2}(g)=\tilde{U}U_{1}(g)\tilde{U}^{\dagger}\,,

(210)

for all
g\in G
.
This implies that
U_{1}
and
U_{2}
are equivalent unitary representations, and thus have the same isotypic decomposition,

U_{2}(g)=\tilde{U}U_{1}(g)\tilde{U}^{\dagger}=\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\Lambda}U_{\lambda}(g)\otimes I_{m(\lambda)}\,,\quad\text{for all }g\in G\,.

(211)

In particular, this means that for each irrep
\lambda
, the multiplicity
m(\lambda)
is identical for representations
U_{1}
and
U_{2}
.
∎

B.8
U(1) symmetry breaking for Gaussian systems

In Gaussian systems,
\operatorname{U}(1)
symmetry breaking is equivalent to
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
symmetry breaking for some
p>2
.

Proposition 21
.

Let
\hat{O}(\theta)=\bigotimes_{j=1}^{n}\exp(i\theta q_j \hat{a}^\dag_j\hat{a}_j)
, with integer
q_{j}\neq 0
for
j=1,\dots,n
, be a representation of
\operatorname{U}(1)
on
n
modes.
A Gaussian Hamiltonian breaks the
\operatorname{U}(1)
symmetry if and only if it breaks a
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
symmetry for some integer
p>2\max_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}}|q_{j}|
.

Proof.

Since no mode carries the trivial representation, i.e.
q_{j}\neq 0
for all
j
, we can assume that there is no displacement, otherwise both symmetries are broken.

Any purely quadratic Hamiltonian on
n
modes takes the form

\hat{H}=\sum_{j,k=1}F_{jk}\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{k}+G_{jk}\hat{a}_{j}\hat{a}_{k}+\text{H.c.}\,.

(212)

For an arbitrary representation of
\operatorname{U}(1)
on
n
modes, as given by Eq.(
20
), the subgroup
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
admits subrepresentation

\hat{O}(r)=\bigotimes_{j=1}^{n}e^{ir\frac{2\pi}{p}q_{j}\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{j}\hat{a}_{j}}\,,\quad\text{for all }r\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}\,.

(213)

The Hamiltonian transforms under the action of
\operatorname{U}(1)
or
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
as

\begin{split}\hat{O}(\theta)\hat{H}\hat{O}(\theta)^{\dagger}=\sum_{j,k=1}F_{jk}e^{i\theta(q_{k}-q_{j})}\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{k}+G_{jk}e^{i\theta(q_{k}+q_{j})}\hat{a}_{j}\hat{a}_{k}+\text{H.c.}\,,\end{split}

(214)

where
\theta\in[0,2\pi)
if the symmetry is U(1), and
\theta\in\left\{r\frac{2\pi}{p}:r\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}\right\}
if the symmetry is
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
.
Operator
\hat{H}
breaks the symmetry if and only if any of the phases
e^{i\theta(q_{k}-q_{j})},e^{i\theta(q_{k}+q_{j})}
is non-zero for any permissible
\theta
.

In the case of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
symmetry with
p<\max\{q_{k}-q_{j},q_{j}+q_{k}\}
, there may be phases that are zero independently of
r
.
However, for
p>2\max_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}}|q_{j}|
, it is guaranteed that
p>q_{k}-q_{j}
and
p>q_{j}+q_{k}
, so
\hat{H}
has the same non-zero terms under the action of both
\operatorname{U}(1)
and
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
.
∎

This result applies to any symplectic Gaussian unitary generated by / any quantum state prepared as the thermal state of a second-order Hamiltonian that breaks the
\operatorname{U}(1)
symmetry.

B.9
Mode-coupling and entanglement in the presence of symmetries

We introduce a primitive that entangles systems in a symmetry-respecting manner.
Starting with a simple scenario, let
\hat{a}
and
\hat{b}
be the annihilation operators on two 1–mode systems
A
and
B
respectively.
Then, passive operations, such as the beam-splitter cannot create correlations from the vacuum state
\ket{0}^{\otimes 2}
.
On the other hand, the 2–mode squeezer
\hat{V}_{\rm{2sq}}(r)
, with
r\neq 0
, acts on the vacuum as

\hat{V}_{\rm{2sq}}(r)\ket{0}^{\otimes 2}=\sech{r}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}(-\tanh r)^{j}\ket{j}\otimes\ket{j}\,.

(215)

This state is known as the EPR state and it is entangled whenever
r\neq 0
, as its reduced 1–mode states are thermal states with non-zero von Neumann entropy (Eq.(
60
)).
We generalize the 2–mode squeezer
\hat{V}_{\rm{2sq}}(r)
to obtain an operator that entangles 2 systems of arbitrary numbers of modes, and we characterize the conditions on the squeezing correlation matrix
r
that ensure invariance of the operator under a general passive representation of a symmetry.

Proposition 22
.

Suppose that
A
and
B
are two bosonic systems of
n
modes, and let
G
be a symmetry group with squeezed passive representation
g\mapsto\hat{S}_{A}(g)=\hat{R}^{-1}\hat{O}_{A}(g)\hat{R}
in Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{A}
, where
\hat{R}
is a symplectic Gaussian unitary and
\hat{O}_{A}
has associated phase space representation
g\mapsto O_{A}(g)\overset{\boldsymbol{a}}{=}U(g)\oplus U(g)^{*}
.

Define the following symplectic Gaussian unitary,

\hat{V}_{\rm sq}(r)\coloneqq\hat{R}\ \exp\left(\sum_{j,k=1}^{n}r_{jk}\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{j}\hat{b}^{\dagger}_{k}-r_{jk}^{*}\hat{a}_{j}\hat{b}_{k}\right)\hat{R}^{-1}\,,

(216)

for any
(n\times n)
complex, invertible matrix
r
.
Then, operator
\hat{V}(r)
has associated symplectic matrix

V_{\rm sq}(r)\overset{\boldsymbol{a}}{=}R\begin{pmatrix}\cosh\sqrt{rr^{\dagger}}&0&0&{\displaystyle\frac{\sinh\sqrt{rr^{\dagger}}}{\sqrt{rr^{\dagger}}}\ r}\\
0&\left(\cosh\sqrt{rr^{\dagger}}\right)^{*}&{\displaystyle\left(\frac{\sinh\sqrt{rr^{\dagger}}}{\sqrt{rr^{\dagger}}}\ r\right)^{*}}&0\\
0&{\displaystyle\left(\frac{\sinh\sqrt{r^{\dagger}r}}{\sqrt{r^{\dagger}r}}\ r^{\dagger}\right)^{*}}&\left(\cosh\sqrt{r^{\dagger}r}\right)^{*}&0\\
{\displaystyle\frac{\sinh\sqrt{r^{\dagger}r}}{\sqrt{r^{\dagger}r}}\ r^{\dagger}}&0&0&\cosh\sqrt{r^{\dagger}r}\end{pmatrix}R^{-1}\,,

(217)

where the basis is
(\hat{a}_{1},\dots,\hat{a}_{n},\hat{a}_{1}^{\dagger},\dots,\hat{a}_{n}^{\dagger},\hat{b}_{1},\dots,\hat{b}_{n},\hat{b}_{1}^{\dagger},\dots,\hat{b}_{n}^{\dagger})
.

Moreover,
\hat{V}(r)
is invariant under the tensor representation
g\mapsto\hat{S}_{A}(g)\otimes\hat{S}_{B}(g)
if and only if
r=U_{A}(g)rU_{B}(g)^{\rm T}
for all
g\in G
.
In particular, if
\hat{S}_{B}(g)=\hat{S}^{*}_{A}(g)
, this condition becomes
[r,U_{A}(g)]=0
for all
g\in G
.

The correlation matrix
r
describes how operator
\hat{V}_{\rm sq}(r)
entangles the annihilation (creation) operators on system
A
with the annihilation (creation) operators on system
B
.
Operator
\hat{V}_{\rm sq}(r)
is generated by the Hamiltonian matrix
H_{\rm sq}(r)\overset{\boldsymbol{a}}{=}r^{*}\oplus r\oplus r^{*}\oplus r
and it is an
n
–mode generalization of the 2–mode squeezer
\hat{V}_{\rm{2sq}}(r)
, which can be obtained by setting
n=1
and
r\in\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

Throughout the proof, we assume that the representations
\hat{S}_{A}
and
\hat{S}_{B}
are passive, which can be achieved by simply changing the basis by
\hat{R}
.

First, we prove the invariance condition for operator
\hat{V}(r)
,

\begin{split}(\hat{S}_{A}(g)\otimes\hat{S}_{B}(g))\hat{V}_{\rm sq}(r)(\hat{S}_{A}(g)\otimes\hat{S}_{B}(g))^{\dagger}&=\exp\left(\sum_{j,k=1}^{n}\left(r_{jk}\left(\hat{S}_{A}(g)\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger}\hat{S}_{A}(g)^{\dagger}\right)\left(\hat{S}_{B}(g)\hat{b}_{k}^{\dagger}\hat{S}_{B}(g)^{\dagger}\right)-\text{H.c.}\right)\right)\\
&\overset{\boldsymbol{a}}{=}\exp\left(\sum_{j,k=1}^{n}\left(r_{jk}\sum_{j^{\prime},k^{\prime}=1}^{n}[U_{A}(g)]_{j^{\prime}j}\hat{a}_{j^{\prime}}^{\dagger}[U_{B}(g)]_{k^{\prime}k}\hat{b}_{k^{\prime}}^{\dagger}-\text{H.c.}\right)\right)\\
&\overset{\boldsymbol{a}}{=}\exp\left(\sum_{j^{\prime},k^{\prime}=1}^{n}\left(\left[U_{A}(g)rU_{B}(g)^{\rm T}\right]_{j^{\prime}k^{\prime}}\hat{a}_{j^{\prime}}^{\dagger}\hat{b}_{k^{\prime}}^{\dagger}-\text{H.c.}\right)\right)\\
&=\hat{V}_{\rm sq}(U_{A}(g)rU_{B}(g)^{\rm T})\,,\end{split}

(218)

so
[\hat{S}_{A}(g)\otimes\hat{S}_{B}(g),\hat{V}(r)]=0
if and only if
r=U_{A}(g)rU_{B}(g)^{\rm T}
, which holds if
S_{B}(g)\overset{\boldsymbol{a}}{=}\widetilde{S}_{A}(g)
and
[r,U_{A}(g)]=0
, for all
g\in G
.

Next, we derive the symplectic matrix
V_{\rm sq}(r)
associated to the operator
\hat{V}_{\rm sq}(r)
given in Eq.(
216
).
To this end, we evaluate the operator action on
\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}=(\hat{a}_{1},\dots,\hat{a}_{n},\hat{a}_{1}^{\dagger},\dots,\hat{a}_{n}^{\dagger},\hat{b}_{1},\dots,\hat{b}_{n},\hat{b}_{1}^{\dagger},\dots,\hat{b}_{n}^{\dagger})
, such that
\hat{V}_{\rm sq}(r)^{\dagger}\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\hat{V}_{\rm sq}(r)=V_{\rm sq}(r)\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}
.

We first derive the action of
\hat{V}(r)
on
\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}
by considering the singular value decomposition (SVD)

r=U^{\dagger}DW\,,

(219)

for some unitary matrices
U,W
and
D=\mathrm{diag}(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n})
, where
\lambda_{\ell}\geq 0
for
\ell=1,\dots,n
.
The symplectic Gaussian unitary
\hat{V}_{\rm sq}(r)
can be re-expressed as

\hat{V}_{\rm sq}(r)=\exp\left(\sum_{\ell}\lambda_{\ell}\left(\tilde{a}^{\dagger}_{\ell}\tilde{b}^{\dagger}_{\ell}-\tilde{a}_{\ell}\tilde{b}_{\ell}\right)\right)\,,

(220)

where the new annihilation operators can be expressed in terms of the original annihilation operators as

\begin{split}\tilde{a}_{\ell}&\coloneqq\sum_{k=1}^{n}U_{\ell k}\hat{a}_{k}\,,\\
\tilde{b}_{\ell}&\coloneqq\sum_{k=1}^{n}W^{*}_{\ell k}\hat{b}_{k}\,.\end{split}

(221)

The modes are locally decoupled in the new basis, so the action of
\hat{V}(r)
is independent on each pair of modes,

\begin{split}\hat{V}_{\rm sq}(r)^{\dagger}\tilde{a}_{\ell}\hat{V}_{\rm sq}(r)&=\cosh{\lambda_\ell}\ \tilde{a}_{\ell}+\sinh{\lambda_\ell}\ \tilde{b}^{\dagger}_{\ell}\,,\\
\hat{V}_{\rm sq}(r)^{\dagger}\tilde{b}_{\ell}\hat{V}_{\rm sq}(r)&=\cosh{\lambda_\ell}\ \tilde{b}_{\ell}+\sinh{\lambda_\ell}\ \tilde{a}^{\dagger}_{\ell}\,.\end{split}

(222)

This is the action of a 2–mode squeezer.

Converting back to the original basis using the transformations in Eq.(
221
),

\displaystyle\hat{V}_{\rm sq}(r)^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{\ell}\hat{V}_{\rm sq}(r)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\left(\left[U^{\dagger}\cosh{D}\ U\right]_{\ell k}\hat{a}_{k}+\left[U^{\dagger}\sinh{D}\ W\right]_{\ell k}\hat{b}^{\dagger}_{k}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\left(\left[\cosh{\sqrt{rr^\dag}}\right]_{k\ell}\ \hat{a}_{k}+\left[\left(\sinh{\sqrt{rr^\dag}}\right)(rr^{\dagger})^{-\frac{1}{2}}r\right]_{k\ell}\ \hat{b}^{\dagger}_{k}\right)\,,

(223)

\displaystyle\hat{V}_{\rm sq}(r)^{\dagger}\hat{b}_{\ell}\hat{V}_{\rm sq}(r)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\left(\left[W^{\dagger}\cosh{D}\ W\right]_{k\ell}\hat{b}_{k}+\left[U^{\dagger}\sinh{D}\ W\right]_{k\ell}\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{k}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\left(\left[\cosh{\sqrt{r^\dag r}}\right]^{*}_{\ell k}\ \hat{b}_{k}+\left[\left(\sinh{\sqrt{r^\dag r}}\right)(r^{\dagger}r)^{-\frac{1}{2}}r^{\dagger}\right]^{*}_{\ell k}\ \hat{a}^{\dagger}_{k}\right)\,,

(224)

where we have used that
(rr^{\dagger})^{-\frac{1}{2}}=({U^{\dagger}DW(U^{\dagger}DW)^{{\dagger}}})^{-1/2}=U^{\dagger}D^{-1}U
, which implies

U^{\dagger}W=[U^{\dagger}D^{-1}U]U^{\dagger}DW=(rr^{\dagger})^{-\frac{1}{2}}r\,.

(225)

The associated symplectic matrix
V_{\rm sq}(r)
provided in Eq.(
217
) can be read directly from Eq.(
223
) and Eq.(
224
) up to the basis change induced by
\hat{R}
.
∎

Appendix C
Resource theory of Gaussian asymmetry

Here, we provide proofs and derivations for Sections
IV
and
V
.

C.1
Transitivity of free unitaries on free pure states

We prove the existence of a free unitary that converts between any two free pure states in the resource theory of Gaussian asymmetry and provide an explicit form for it in terms of the states’ statistical moments.
See
6

Proof.

We first consider the passive representation
g\mapsto\hat{O}(g)
and make use of the irrep decomposition of an invariant state
\ket{\psi}
(see Section
B.4
) to find an invariant Gaussian unitary
\hat{V}
that maps the vacuum state
\ket{0}^{\otimes n}
to
\ket{\psi}
.

Let
S(g)\overset{\boldsymbol{a}}{=}U(g)\oplus U(g)^{*}
be the associated phase space representation.
Let
\boldsymbol{d}
and
\sigma
be the displacement vector and covariance matrix of state
\ket{\psi}
.
The displacement operator
\hat{D}_{\boldsymbol{d}}
is invariant as proven in Proposition
18
.
Due to Eq.(
132
), the covariance matrix of any pure state can be written as
\sigma\overset{\boldsymbol{r}}{=}SS^{\rm T}\overset{\boldsymbol{r}}{=}O\left(\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}\mathrm{diag}\left(z_{j}^{2},z_{j}^{-2}\right)\right)O^{\rm T}
, where
O
is orthogonal symplectic.
Then, we construct the matrix

V\coloneqq\sqrt{\sigma}\overset{\boldsymbol{r}}{=}O\left(\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}\mathrm{diag}\left(z_{j},z_{j}^{-1}\right)\right)O^{\rm T}\,,

(226)

which is invariant under
S
, symplectic, i.e.
\sqrt{\sigma}\Omega_{n}\sqrt{\sigma}^{\rm T}=\Omega_{n}
.
Then,
\hat{D}_{\boldsymbol{d}}\hat{V}
maps
\ket{0}^{\otimes n}
to
\ket{\psi}
.

For squeezed passive representations of the form
\hat{R}\hat{S}(g)\hat{R}^{\dagger}
, where
\hat{S}
is passive, we can simply use the transformation Table
1
, to deduce that an invariant symplectic matrix that sends
\hat{R}\ket{0}^{\otimes n}
to
\ket{\psi}
is
V=R\sqrt{R^{-1}\sigma R^{\rm-T}}R^{-1}
.

Applying first
\hat{V}_{1}^{\dagger}
to send
\ket{\psi_1}
to the vacuum, then
\hat{V}_{2}
to send the vacuum to
\ket{\psi_2}
, we obtain Eq.(
43
).
∎

C.2
Interconversion between types of asymmetry

Here we provide the proof of Proposition
7
on the convertibility between type–1 and type–2 asymmetry.
See
7

Proof.

As mentioned in the main text, both types of asymmetry remain distinct under Gaussian covariant channels, i.e. the output state contains each type of asymmetry, only if the input state contains it.

Consider a quantum state with zero type–1 asymmetry.
In such a case, the state’s first moment is non-zero only in the trivial irreps of the representation, thus the state respects the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
symmetry.
If a quantum channel acts on the state increasing its type–1 asymmetry, then the new first moment is non-zero in at least one non-trivial irrep, and the state no longer respects the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
symmetry.
Therefore, such a channel cannot be
G
–covariant.

To prove that a pure Gaussian state
\ket{\psi}
with non-zero type–1 asymmetry can generate type–2 asymmetry via a
G
–covariant operation, consider the passive representation
g\mapsto\hat{S}(g)
of a group
G
on
n
modes, with associated phase space representation
g\mapsto S(g)\overset{\boldsymbol{a}}{=}U(g)\oplus U(g)^{*}
.
Without loss of generality, we assume that it does not contain a trivial subrepresentation, otherwise we can increase type–2 asymmetry arbitrarily on modes with trivial symmetry.

Firstly, we can convert the covariance matrix
\sigma
of
\ket{\psi}
to the identity
I
by applying the
G
–invariant symplectic transformation
\sqrt{\sigma}
(see Proposition
6
).
Therefore, we can write
\ket{\psi}
as an
n
–mode coherent state
\ket{\psi}=\ket{\alpha}=\bigotimes_{j=1}^{n}\ket{\alpha_j}
, for vector
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n})
.
We then act on it with the non-Gaussian
G
–invariant unitary
\hat{U}\coloneqq\hat{I}-2\outerproduct{0}{0}
.
According to Eqs.(
96
,
97
), we can write the output covariance matrix in the complex basis as

\sigma\overset{\boldsymbol{a}}{=}\begin{pmatrix}G&F\\
F^{*}&G^{*}\end{pmatrix}\,,\quad\quad F=I+2(1-c^{2})\alpha\alpha^{\dagger}\,,\quad\quad G=2c(1-c)\alpha\alpha^{\rm T}\,.

(227)

Whenever the vector
\alpha
is not invariant under
g\mapsto U(g)
, the matrix
G\propto\alpha\alpha^{\rm T}
is also not invariant in the sense of Eq.(
147
), i.e.
U\alpha\neq\alpha
implies
UGU^{\rm T}\propto U\alpha(U\alpha)^{\rm T}\neq\alpha\alpha^{\rm T}
for any unitary
U
.
∎

In fact, if
\alpha
is arbitrarily high and the symmetry group
G
is compact, we can output any arbitrary state
\hat{\tau}
with arbitrarily high probability.
Define the measure-and-prepare operation
\mathcal{E}_{\hat{\tau}}:\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})\rightarrow\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
that acts on a state
\hat{\rho}
as

\mathcal{E}_{\hat{\tau}}(\hat{\rho})=\int{\rm d}g\ {\rm{tr}}[\hat{\rho}\outerproduct{\alpha_g}{\alpha_g}]\hat{S}(g)\hat{\tau}\hat{S}(g)^{\dagger}+{\rm{tr}}\left[\hat{\rho}\left(\hat{I}-\int{\rm d}g\ \outerproduct{\alpha_g}{\alpha_g}\right)\right]\outerproduct{0}{0}\,,

(228)

where
\hat{\tau}
is a fixed state, and
\ket{\alpha_g}\coloneqq\hat{S}(g)\ket{\alpha}=\ket{U(g)\alpha}
.
Choose
\hat{\rho}=\outerproduct{\alpha}{\alpha}
and label the POVM implicitly defined in Eq.(
228
) as
\{\hat{M}_{g}:g\in G\sqcup\{0\}\}
, with
g=0
corresponding to the preparation of the vacuum.
Then, the density function,

p(\alpha|g)=\begin{cases}|\innerproduct{\alpha}{\alpha_g}|^{2}\,,&g\in G\,,\\
1-\int{\rm d}g\ |\innerproduct{\alpha}{\alpha_g}|^{2}\,,&g=0\,,\end{cases}

(229)

satisfies
p(\alpha|g)\neq p(\alpha|g^{\prime})
for
g\neq g^{\prime}
, because the representation acts non-trivially on
\ket{\alpha}
, and it is uniquely maximized at the identity element
g=e
.
Consider the limit of high
\|\alpha\|_{2}
which, in the absence of a trivial representation, can be taken in any direction
|\alpha_{i}|
.
In this limit, and for
\hat{\rho}=\outerproduct{\alpha}{\alpha}
,
p
decays exponentially in
\|\alpha\|_{2}^{2}
tending to a sharp distribution around
g=e
(Dirac delta
\delta(g-e)
if
G
is continuous or Kronecker delta
\delta_{g,e}
if
G
is finite) due to the approximate orthogonality of coherent states, and channel
\mathcal{E}_{\hat{\tau}}
outputs state
\hat{\tau}
with arbitrarily high probability.
This holds for any input state with first moment defined by
\alpha\neq 0
.

In fact, the maximum likelihood estimator can be chosen
Newey and McFadden 1994
to estimate
e
.
Importantly, the density function
p
is continuous over the compact parameter space
G\sqcup\{0\}
, and identifiable, i.e.
g\neq g^{\prime}
implies
p(\alpha|g)\neq p(\alpha|g^{\prime})
, so the maximum likelihood estimator converges in probability to the unique maximum
g=e
for a sequence of input states with increasingly high
\|\alpha\|_{2}
.

C.3
First moments of reachable states under Gaussian covariant channels

We provide the proof of Proposition
8
which describes the complete set of first moments of states that are reachable from a fixed state under Gaussian covariant channels.
See
8

Proof.

First, we note that for a general covariant channel the displacement vector transforms as
\boldsymbol{d}_{B}=X\boldsymbol{d}_{A}+\boldsymbol{\xi}
, where
X
satisfies
XS_{A}(g)=S_{B}(g)X
for all
g\in G
, and
\boldsymbol{\xi}
is zero in all modes except the mode carrying the trivial representation. It follows that the projection of
\boldsymbol{d}_{B}
to the trivial modes is fully unrestricted and can be independent of the input. Therefore, in the following, we focus on modes carrying non-trivial representations of symmetry and find the condition for the existence of an invariant
X
satisfying
\boldsymbol{d}_{B}=X\boldsymbol{d}_{A}+\boldsymbol{\xi}
.

Consider input and output representations

K_{A}S_{A}(g)K_{A}^{-1}=\bigoplus_{\mu\in M_{A}}U_{\mu}\otimes I_{m(\mu)}\,,\quad\quad K_{B}S_{B}(g)K_{B}^{-1}=\bigoplus_{\nu\in M_{B}}U_{\nu}\otimes I_{m^{\prime}(\nu)}\,,

(230)

where
K_{A},K_{B}
are defined according to Eq.(
46
),
m(\mu)
and
m^{\prime}(\nu)
are the multiplicities of irrep
\mu
in the input irrep decomposition and of irrep
\nu
in the output irrep decomposition, respectively.

Given a complex matrix
X
and
g\in G
, we have
XS_{A}(g)=S_{B}(g)X
if and only if
X^{\prime}\coloneqq K_{B}^{-1}XK_{A}
satisfies

X^{\prime}\left(\bigoplus_{\mu\in M_{A}}U_{\mu}\otimes I_{m(\mu)}\right)=\left(\bigoplus_{\nu\in M_{B}}U_{\nu}\otimes I_{m^{\prime}(\nu)}\right)X^{\prime}\,,

(231)

which leads to the following form for
X^{\prime}
according to Schur’s lemma,

X^{\prime}=\bigoplus_{\mu\in M_{A}\cap M_{B}}I_{d_{\mu}}\otimes X^{\prime}_{\mu}\,,

(232)

for some
(m^{\prime}(\mu)\times m(\mu))
matrices
X^{\prime}_{\mu}
.

Defining
\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}_{A}\coloneqq K_{A}\boldsymbol{d}_{A}
and
\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}_{B}\coloneqq K_{B}\boldsymbol{d}_{B}
, we now determine the necessary and sufficient condition for existence of
X^{\prime}
in the form given in Eq.(
232
), which satisfies (i)
X^{\prime}\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}_{A}=\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}_{B}
, and (ii)
X=K_{B}X^{\prime}K^{-1}_{A}
is real.

First, note that there exists
X^{\prime}
in the above form satisfying
X^{\prime}\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}_{A}=\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}_{B}
if and only if for all
\mu\in M_{A}\cap M_{B}
, there exists
X^{\prime}_{\mu}
such that

(I_{d_{\mu}}\otimes X^{\prime}_{\mu})\Pi_{\mu}\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}_{A}=\Pi_{\mu}\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}_{B}\,.

(233)

According to Lemma
23
, such
X_{\mu}
exists if and only if

{\rm supp}\left({\rm{tr}}_{\mathcal{N}_{\mu}}[\Pi_{\mu}\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}_{B}{\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}_{B}}^{{\dagger}}\Pi_{\mu}]\right)\subseteq{\rm supp}\left({\rm{tr}}_{\mathcal{N}_{\mu}}[\Pi_{\mu}\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}_{A}{\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}_{A}}^{{\dagger}}\Pi_{\mu}]\right)\,,

(234)

for all
\mu\in M_{A}\cap M_{B}
, or, equivalently,

{\rm supp}\left(T^{(\mu)}\left(\boldsymbol{d}_{B}\boldsymbol{d}_{B}^{{\dagger}}\right)\right)\subseteq{\rm supp}\left(T^{(\mu)}\left(\boldsymbol{d}_{A}\boldsymbol{d}_{A}^{\dagger}\right)\right)\,,

(235)

for all
\mu\in M_{A}\cap M_{B}
, where

T^{(\mu)}(\cdot)\coloneqq{\rm{tr}}_{\mathcal{N}_{\mu}}[\Pi_{\mu}K(\cdot)K^{\dagger}\Pi_{\mu}]\,.

(236)

This proves the necessity of the condition in Eq.(
48
).
To prove the sufficiency of this condition, consider a set of
\{X^{\prime}_{\mu}\}
satisfying Eq.(
233
) for all
\mu\in M_{A}\cap M_{B}
, and consider the corresponding
X^{\prime}
obtained from Eq.(
232
).
Then,
X=K_{B}X^{\prime}K^{-1}_{A}
satisfies the desired equation
X\boldsymbol{d}_{A}=\boldsymbol{d}_{B}
.
However, in general, this
X
will not be real.
To show that
X
can be chosen to be real, first we note that, because
\boldsymbol{d}_{A}
and
\boldsymbol{d}_{B}
are real, then
X^{*}
, the complex conjugate of
X
also satisfies this equation. Furthermore, because
S_{A}
and
S_{B}
are real, it also respects the symmetry. It follows that
\tfrac{1}{2}(X+X^{*})
is a
G
–invariant real matrix satisfying
\tfrac{1}{2}(X+X^{*})\boldsymbol{d}_{A}=\boldsymbol{d}_{B}
. This proves the necessity and sufficiency of the condition in Eq.(
48
).
∎

We prove a standard result for bipartite vectors, needed in the proof of Proposition
8
.

Lemma 23
.

Given bipartite vectors
\ket{\psi},\ket{\psi'}\in\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B}
, there exists a linear operator
N_{B}
acting on
\mathcal{H}_{B}
such that
(I_{A}\otimes N_{B})\ket{\psi}=\ket{\psi'}
if and only if

{\rm supp}\left({\rm{tr}}_{B}[\outerproduct{\psi'}{\psi'}]\right)\subseteq{\rm supp}\left({\rm{tr}}_{B}[\outerproduct{\psi}{\psi}]\right)\,.

(237)

Proof.

Arbitrary bipartite vectors
\ket{\psi}
and
\ket{\psi'}
can be written as
\ket{\psi}=\left(\sqrt{M_{A}}\otimes U_{B}\right)\sum_{k}\ket{k}\otimes\ket{k}
and
\ket{\psi'}=\left(\sqrt{M^{\prime}_{A}}\otimes U^{\prime}_{B}\right)\sum_{k}\ket{k}\otimes\ket{k}
, where
M_{A}={\rm{tr}}_{B}[\ket{\psi}\bra{\psi}]
and
M^{\prime}_{A}={\rm{tr}}_{B}[\ket{\psi'}\bra{\psi'}]
, and
U_{B},U^{\prime}_{B}
are arbitrary unitaries.
It follows that there exists an operator
N_{B}
acting on
\mathcal{H}_{B}
such that
(I_{A}\otimes N_{B})\ket{\psi}=\ket{\psi'}
, if and only if
N_{B}U_{B}\sqrt{M_{A}}^{\rm T}=U^{\prime}_{B}\sqrt{M^{\prime}_{A}}^{\rm T}
, or, equivalently,
\tilde{N}_{B}\coloneqq U^{\prime{\dagger}}_{B}N_{B}U_{B}
satisfies
\sqrt{M_{A}}\tilde{N}_{B}^{\rm T}=\sqrt{M^{\prime}_{A}}
.
Such
\tilde{N}_{B}
exists only if the support of
M^{\prime}_{A}
is contained in
M_{A}
, which can be seen, e.g. by noting that
M^{\prime}_{A}=\sqrt{M_{A}}\tilde{N}_{B}^{\rm T}\tilde{N}^{*}_{B}\sqrt{M_{A}}
.
Conversely, suppose this condition is satisfied.
Then, by defining
M_{A}^{-1}
to be the inverse of
M_{A}
on its support and zero in the orthogonal subspace, we find
\tilde{N}_{B}^{\rm T}=\sqrt{M^{-1}_{A}}\sqrt{M^{\prime}_{A}}
satisfies the above equation. This completes the proof.
∎

C.4
SU(2)–covariant channels

Consider the passive irreducible representation of
\operatorname{SU}(2)
given by

U\mapsto S(U)\overset{\boldsymbol{a}}{=}U\oplus U^{*}=(I\oplus\Omega^{\rm T})(U\oplus U)(I\oplus\Omega)\,,

(238)

where
\Omega=\begin{pmatrix}0&I\\
-I&0\end{pmatrix}
throughout this section.
Suppose a channel characterized by its action on the phase space via Eq.(
7
), is covariant under input and output representation
S
.
Then, its displacement vector
\boldsymbol{\xi}
must be 0 because representation
S
does not contain the trivial irrep, while matrices
X,Y
must satisfy

[X,S(U)]=0\,,\quad S(U)YS(U)^{\rm T}=Y\,,

(239)

for all
U\in\mathrm{SU}(2)
.
The conditions in Eq.(
239
) are equivalent to

\left[(I\oplus-\Omega)X(I\oplus\Omega),U\oplus U\right]=0\,,\quad[(I\oplus-\Omega)Y(\Omega\oplus I),U\oplus U]=0\,,

(240)

for all
U\in\operatorname{SU}(2)
, where we have used
U^{\rm T}=\Omega U^{\dagger}\Omega^{\rm T}=-\Omega U^{\dagger}\Omega
.
Expressing
X\overset{\boldsymbol{a}}{=}\begin{pmatrix}X_{1}&X_{2}\\
X_{3}&X_{4}\end{pmatrix}
and
Y\overset{\boldsymbol{a}}{=}\begin{pmatrix}Y_{1}&Y_{2}\\
Y_{3}&Y_{4}\end{pmatrix}
in block form, the conditions in Eq.(
239
) are further equivalent to

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}U&0\\
0&U\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}X_{1}&X_{2}\Omega\\
\Omega X_{3}&\Omega X_{4}\Omega\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}U^{\dagger}&0\\
0&U^{\dagger}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}X_{1}&X_{2}\Omega\\
\Omega X_{3}&\Omega X_{4}\Omega\end{pmatrix}\,,

(241)

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}U&0\\
0&U\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}Y_{1}\Omega&Y_{2}\\
\Omega Y_{3}\Omega&\Omega Y_{4}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}U^{\dagger}&0\\
0&U^{\dagger}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}Y_{1}\Omega&Y_{2}\\
\Omega Y_{3}\Omega&\Omega Y_{4}\end{pmatrix}\,.

(242)

Since
U
is an irrep of
\operatorname{SU}(2)
, Schur’s lemma implies that

X_{1},X_{4},Y_{2},Y_{3}\propto I\,,\quad X_{2},X_{3},Y_{1},Y_{4}\propto\Omega\,,

(243)

where the proportionality constants are any complex numbers provided that
Y=Y^{\rm T}
and that
X
and
Y
are of the form of Eq.(
128
) to ensure that they are real in the real basis, i.e.

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle\overset{\boldsymbol{a}}{=}\begin{pmatrix}aI&b\Omega\\
b^{*}\Omega&a^{*}I\end{pmatrix}\overset{\boldsymbol{r}}{=}e^{\phi\Omega}\begin{pmatrix}x_{+}I&x_{-}Ze^{\theta\Omega}\\
-x_{-}Ze^{\theta\Omega}&x_{+}I\end{pmatrix}\,,

(244)

\displaystyle Y

\displaystyle\overset{\boldsymbol{a}}{=}\begin{pmatrix}0&yI\\
yI&0\end{pmatrix}\overset{\boldsymbol{r}}{=}yI_{4}\,,

(245)

where
a,b\in\mathbb{C}
, and
y\in\mathbb{R}
are arbitrary, and
x_{+}\coloneqq|a|,x_{-}\coloneqq|b|,\phi\coloneqq-\arg(a),\theta\coloneqq\arg(b)-\arg(a)
.
This form for
X
is equivalent to Eq.(
52
).
The channel uncertainty relation
Y+i(\Omega\oplus\Omega-X(\Omega\oplus\Omega)X^{\rm T})\geq 0
takes the form

\begin{pmatrix}yI+i(1-x_{+}^{2}+x_{-}^{2})\Omega&2ix_{+}x_{-}(Z\Omega)e^{(\theta-\phi)\Omega)}\\
-2ix_{+}x_{-}e^{-(\theta-\phi)\Omega)}(Z\Omega)&yI+i(1-x_{+}^{2}+x_{-}^{2})\Omega\end{pmatrix}\geq 0\,,

(246)

which holds if and only if

y\geq\sqrt{\left((x_{+}-1)^{2}+x_{-}^{2}\right)\left((x_{+}+1)^{2}+x_{-}^{2}\right)}\,.

(247)

C.5
Relative entropy of asymmetry on 1 mode

Consider
\operatorname{U}(1)
symmetry on 1 mode, with representation
\phi\mapsto\hat{V}_{\rm ps}(\phi)
, and uniform distribution
p(\phi)=\tfrac{1}{2\pi}
.
We can calculate the relative entropy of asymmetry given in Eq.(
V.1
) of a coherent state
\ket{\alpha}\coloneqq D_{\alpha}\ket{0}
as follows,

\displaystyle\Gamma_{p}(\outerproduct{\alpha}{\alpha})

\displaystyle\overset{(1)}{=}S_{v}\left(\frac{1}{2\pi}\int{\rm d}\phi\ \hat{V}_{\rm ps}(\phi)\outerproduct{\alpha}{\alpha}\hat{V}_{\rm ps}(\phi)^{\dagger}\right)-S_{v}(\outerproduct{\alpha}{\alpha})

\displaystyle\overset{(2)}{=}S_{v}\left(\frac{1}{2\pi}\int{\rm d}\phi\ e^{i\phi(j-k)}e^{-|\alpha|^{2}}\sum_{j,k=0}^{\infty}\frac{\alpha^{j}\alpha^{*k}}{\sqrt{j!k!}}\outerproduct{j}{k}\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(3)}{=}S_{v}\left(e^{-|\alpha|^{2}}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{|\alpha|^{2k}}{k!}\outerproduct{k}{k}\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(4)}{=}|\alpha|^{2}\log\frac{e}{|\alpha|^{2}}+e^{-|\alpha|^{2}}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{|\alpha|^{2k}\log(k!)}{k!}\overset{|\alpha|\gg 0}{=}\log(\sqrt{2\pi e} |\alpha|)+O(|\alpha|^{-2})\,,

(248)

where
(1)
follows from the monotone definition in Eq.(
V.1
),
(2)
from the fact that pure states have zero entropy (e.g. because they have unit symplectic eigenvalues), and from the decomposition of a coherent state
\ket{\alpha}
in the Fock basis given in Eq.(
136
),
(3)
from explicit calculation of the integral, and
(4)
from the definition of the von Neumann entropy given in Eq.(
60
).
The asymptotic behavior of
\Gamma_{p}(\outerproduct{\alpha}{\alpha})
corresponds to the well-known Poisson distribution.

The Holevo asymmetry monotone of a 1–mode squeezed state
\ket{r}\coloneqq V_{\rm 1sq}(r)\ket{0}
is

\displaystyle\Gamma_{p}(\outerproduct{r}{r})

\displaystyle\overset{(1)}{=}S_{v}\left(\frac{1}{2\pi}\int{\rm d}\phi\ \hat{V}_{\rm ps}(\phi)\outerproduct{r}{r}\hat{V}_{\rm ps}(\phi)^{\dagger}\right)-S_{v}(\outerproduct{r}{r})

(249)

\displaystyle\overset{(2)}{=}S_{v}\left(\frac{1}{2\pi}\int{\rm d}\phi\ e^{i2\phi(j-k)}\frac{1}{\cosh(r)}\sum_{j,k=0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{\tanh(r)}{2}\right)^{j+k}\frac{\sqrt{(2j)!(2k)!}}{j!k!}\outerproduct{2j}{2k}\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(3)}{=}S_{v}\left(\sech(r)\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{\tanh(r)}{2}\right)^{2k}\binom{2k}{k}\outerproduct{2k}{2k}\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(4)}{=}-\log(\sech(r))-\sinh|r|\log(\frac{\tanh|r|}{2})-\sech(r)\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{\tanh(r)}{2}\right)^{2k}\binom{2k}{k}\log{\binom{2k}{k}}

\displaystyle\overset{|r|\gg 0}{=}2\log(e)\ |r|+\log(2^{-5}\sqrt{\pi}e^{2-\gamma/2})+o(1)\,,

(250)

where steps
(1)-(3)
follow similar justifications as for coherence.

Below, we provide a detailed derivation of step
(4)
and the asymptotic behavior of
\Gamma_{p}(\outerproduct{r}{r})
, which is not well-known, as dephased squeezed states don’t correspond to a known distribution, unlike dephased coherence states that correspond to the Poisson distribution.
The entropy of a dephased squeezed state is

\displaystyle S_{v}\left(\sech(r)\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{\tanh(r)}{2}\right)^{2k}\binom{2k}{k}\outerproduct{2k}{2k}\right)

(251)

\displaystyle\overset{(1)}{=}

\displaystyle-\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\sech(r)\left(\frac{\tanh(r)}{2}\right)^{2k}\binom{2k}{k}\log\left(\sech(r)\left(\frac{\tanh(r)}{2}\right)^{2k}\binom{2k}{k}\right)

(252)

\displaystyle\overset{(2)}{=}

\displaystyle-\sqrt{1-4x}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}x^{k}\binom{2k}{k}\left(\frac{1}{2}\log(1-4x)+k\log(x)+\log{\binom{2k}{k}}\right)

(253)

\displaystyle\overset{(3)}{=}

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\log(1-4x)-\frac{2x\log(x)}{1-4x}-\sqrt{1-4x}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}x^{k}\binom{2k}{k}\log{\binom{2k}{k}}\,.

(254)

where
(1)
follows from the definition of entropy in Eq.(
60
),
(2)
follows by simply expanding the logarithm into three terms, and setting
x=\left(\frac{\tanh(r)}{2}\right)^{2}
, so
\sech^{2}(r)=1-4x
, while
(3)
follows from evaluating the first two sums.
This leaves us with the third sum, which diverges as
|r|\rightarrow\infty
(
x\rightarrow\frac{1}{4}^{-}
), and is dominated by terms with high
k
.
We provide an estimate of the sum to leading order,

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}x^{k}\binom{2k}{k}\log{\binom{2k}{k}}

(255)

\displaystyle\overset{(1)}{\approx}\

\displaystyle\frac{2\log(2)}{\sqrt{\pi}}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}(4x)^{k}k^{1/2}-\frac{\log(\pi)}{2\sqrt{\pi}}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}(4x)^{k}k^{-1/2}-\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\pi}}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}(4x)^{k}k^{-1/2}\log(k)

(256)

\displaystyle\overset{(2)}{\approx}\

\displaystyle\frac{2\log(2)}{\sqrt{\pi}}\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}k\ e^{-zk}k^{1/2}-\frac{\log(\pi)}{2\sqrt{\pi}}\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}k\ e^{-zk}k^{-1/2}-\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\pi}}\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm d}k\ e^{-zk}k^{-1/2}\log(k)

(257)

\displaystyle\overset{(3)}{\approx}\

\displaystyle\frac{2\log(2)}{\sqrt{\pi}z^{3/2}}\Gamma\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)-\frac{\log(\pi)}{2\sqrt{\pi}z^{1/2}}\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)-\frac{\log(z)}{2\sqrt{\pi}z^{1/2}}\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)+\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\pi}z^{1/2}}\Gamma^{\prime}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)

(258)

\displaystyle\overset{(4)}{\approx}\

\displaystyle\frac{\log(2)}{(1-4x)^{3/2}}-\frac{\log(\pi)}{2(1-4x)^{1/2}}-\frac{\log(1-4x)}{2(1-4x)^{1/2}}-\frac{\gamma\log(e)+2\log(2)}{2(1-4x)^{1/2}}\,,

(259)

where in
(1)
we use Stirling’s approximation,

\frac{n!}{\sqrt{2\pi n}\left(\frac{n}{e}\right)^{n}}=1+o(1)\,,

(260)

in
(2)
we approximate the sums by integrals and set
z=-\ln(4x)
(
\approx 1-4x
around
x=1/4
), in
(3)
we evaluate the integrals, using the Gamma function
\Gamma
and its derivative
\Gamma^{\prime}
, and in
(4)
we evaluate the terms, where
\gamma\approx 0.577
is the Euler–Mascheroni constant.

Combining Eq.(
254
) with Eq.(
259
), we obtain

\displaystyle S_{v}\left(\sech(r)\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{\tanh(r)}{2}\right)^{2k}\binom{2k}{k}\outerproduct{2k}{2k}\right)\overset{|r|\gg 0}{\approx}\ 2\log(e)\ |r|+\log(2^{-5}\sqrt{\pi}e^{2-\gamma/2})\,.

(261)

One can similarly calculate that the 2–mode squeezed state
\ket{r}_{\rm EPR}
defined in Eq.(
215
),
under
\operatorname{U}(1)
representation
\theta\mapsto e^{i\theta\left(q_{1}\hat{a}_{1}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{1}+q_{2}\hat{a}_{2}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{2}\right)}
with integer charges
q_{1}\neq-q_{2}
, results in

\displaystyle\Gamma(\outerproduct{r}{r}_{\rm EPR})

\displaystyle=\cosh^{2}(r)\log\left(\cosh^{2}(r)\right)-\sinh^{2}(r)\log\left(\sinh^{2}(r)\right)

\displaystyle\hskip-4.0pt\overset{r\gg 0}{=}\log\langle n\rangle-\log{4}\,,

(262)

where
\langle n\rangle=\langle\hat{a}_{1}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{1}+\hat{a}_{2}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{2}\rangle=2\sinh^{2}(r)
.
Since
\outerproduct{r}{r}_{\rm EPR}
can be obtained by 1–mode squeezed states via beam-splitters,

V_{\rm{2sq}}(r)=V_{\rm{bs}}(\pi/4)\big(V_{\rm{ps}}(\pi/2)\oplus I\big)\big(V_{\rm{1sq}}(r)\oplus V_{\rm{1sq}}(r)\big)\big(V_{\rm{ps}}(\pi/2)\oplus I\big)^{\rm T}V_{\rm{bs}}(\pi/4)^{\rm T}\,,

(263)

we can confirm the sub-additivity of the relative entropy of asymmetry,

\Gamma(\outerproduct{r}{r}_{\rm EPR})=\Gamma(\outerproduct{r}{r}\otimes\outerproduct{r}{r})<2\Gamma(\outerproduct{r}{r})\,,

(264)

where the equality follows for
q_{1}=q_{2}
ensuring that the beam-splitter operations are phase-covariant, and the inequality follows from Eq.(
62
) and Eq.(
262
).

C.6
Gaussian asymmetry monotone based on the Petz-Rényi divergence

Consider a state
\hat{\rho}=e^{-\beta H}/Z_{\sigma}
as the thermal state of second-order Hamiltonian
\hat{H}
at temperature
\beta^{-1}
, where we denote its displacement vector and covariance matrix by
\boldsymbol{d}
and
\sigma
, respectively, and its partition function by

Z_{\sigma}\coloneqq{\rm{tr}}[e^{-\beta H}]=\sqrt{{\rm{det}}[(\sigma+i\Omega)/2]}\,.

(265)

Then, the state
\hat{\rho}^{\alpha}/{\rm{tr}}[\hat{\rho}^{\alpha}]
with
\alpha>0
, i.e. the thermal state of Hamiltonian
\hat{H}
at temperature
(\alpha\beta)^{-1}
, has first moment
\boldsymbol{d}
and covariance matrix given by

\sigma_{\alpha}i\Omega\coloneqq\coth(\alpha\ {\rm coth}^{-1}(\sigma i\Omega))\,,\quad\text{with }\sigma_{1}\equiv\sigma\,.

(266)

For Gaussian states
\hat{\rho}
, with displacement vector
\boldsymbol{d}
and covariance matrix
\sigma
, and
\hat{\rho}^{\prime}
, with displacement vector
\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}
and covariance matrix
\sigma^{\prime}
, the Petz-Rényi
\alpha
–divergence, with
\alpha\in(0,1)
, has been calculated
Seshadreesan et al. 2018
explicitly in terms of the moments,

\displaystyle D_{\alpha}(\hat{\rho}\|\hat{\rho}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\alpha-1}\log Q_{\alpha}(\hat{\rho}\|\hat{\rho}^{\prime})\,,\quad\text{where}

(267)

\displaystyle Q_{\alpha}(\hat{\rho}\|\hat{\rho}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\frac{Z_{\sigma_{\alpha}}Z_{\sigma^{\prime}_{1-\alpha}}}{Z_{\sigma}^{\alpha}Z_{\sigma^{\prime}}^{1-\alpha}\sqrt{\rule{0.0pt}{8.61108pt}{\rm{det}}[(\sigma_{\alpha}+\sigma^{\prime}_{1-\alpha})/2]}}\exp(-(\bmd-\bmd')^{\rm T}(\sigma_\alpha+ \sigma'_{1-\alpha})^{-1}(\bmd-\bmd'))\,.

(268)

If
\hat{\rho}^{\prime}=\hat{S}\hat{\rho}\hat{S}^{\dagger}
for some symplectic Gaussian unitary
\hat{S}
, then
\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}=S\boldsymbol{d}
and
\sigma^{\prime}=S\sigma S^{\rm T}
.
Therefore, the exponent in Eq.(
268
) becomes
\boldsymbol{d}^{\rm T}(I-S)^{\rm T}\left(\sigma_{\alpha}+S\sigma_{1-\alpha}S^{\rm T}\right)^{-1}(I-S)\boldsymbol{d}
, giving the expression of
f_{\alpha,g}^{(1)}(\hat{\rho})
in Eq.(
70
).

To obtain the expression of
f_{\alpha,g}^{(2)}(\hat{\rho})
in Eq.(
71
), first note that
Z_{S\sigma S^{\rm T}}=Z_{\sigma}
because
{\rm{det}}[S]=1
for symplectic
S
, so

Z_{\sigma}^{\alpha}Z_{S\sigma S^{\rm T}}^{1-\alpha}=Z_{\sigma}\,.

(269)

Then, we can calculate

\displaystyle\frac{Z_{\sigma_{\alpha}}Z_{\sigma_{1-\alpha}}}{Z_{\sigma}}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{{\rm{det}}[(\sigma_{\alpha}+i\Omega)(\sigma+i\Omega)^{-1}(\sigma_{1-\alpha}+i\Omega)/2]}=\sqrt{{\rm{det}}[(I+\sigma_{\alpha}i\Omega)(I+\sigma i\Omega)^{-1}(I+\sigma_{1-\alpha}i\Omega)i\Omega/2]}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{{\rm{det}}[(\sigma_{\alpha}+\sigma_{1-\alpha})/2]}\,,

(270)

where the first step follows by properties of the determinant, and in the third step we used Eq.(
266
) and the well-known trigonometric formula

\frac{\Big(1+\coth(a\coth^{-1}x)\Big)\Big(1+\coth((1-a)\coth^{-1}x)\Big)}{1+x}=\coth(a\coth^{-1}x)+\coth((1-a)\coth^{-1}x)\,,

(271)

which holds whenever
|x|>1
.
Inserting Eq.(
270
) into Eq.(
268
), we can identify the expression of
f_{\alpha,g}^{(2)}(\hat{\rho})
in Eq.(
71
) as minus the logarithm of the fraction in front of the exponential in Eq.(
268
).

C.7
Second derivative of Gaussian asymmetry monotone

We derive the expressions for
F_{Q}^{(1)},F_{Q}^{(2)}
in Eqs.(
77
,
78
), when the group is generated by the family of Gaussian unitaries
\hat{S}(t)=e^{i\widehat{Q}t}
with associated symplectic matrices
S(t)=e^{-\Omega Qt}
for
t\in\mathbb{R}
.

We first focus on the first moment quantity, and obtain the zeroth, first, and second derivatives of
f_{1/2,t}^{(1)}(\hat{\rho})=\boldsymbol{d}^{\rm T}(I-S(t))^{\rm T}\left(\sigma_{1/2}+S(t)\sigma_{1/2}S(t)^{\rm T}\right)^{-1}(I-S(t))\boldsymbol{d}
at
t=0
,

\displaystyle f_{1/2,0}^{(1)}(\hat{\rho})

\displaystyle=0\,,

(272)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\left.f_{1/2,t}^{(1)}(\hat{\rho})\right|_{t=0}

\displaystyle=0\,,

(273)

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}\left.f_{1/2,t}^{(1)}(\hat{\rho})\right|_{t=0}

\displaystyle=-\boldsymbol{d}^{\rm T}Q\Omega\sigma^{-1}\Omega Q\boldsymbol{d}\eqqcolon F_{Q}^{(1)}(\hat{\rho})\,,

(274)

where the zeroth and first derivatives are 0 because all terms contain a factor of
\left.(I-S(t))\right|_{t=0}=0
.
Then, the quantity
f_{1/2}^{(1)}
takes the form,

\displaystyle f_{1/2,t}^{(1)}(\hat{\rho})=\sum_{k=0}^{2}\frac{t^{k}}{k!}\left.\frac{d^{k}f_{1/2,t}^{(1)}(\hat{\rho})}{dt^{k}}\right|_{t=0}+O(t^{3})=\frac{t^{2}}{2}F_{Q}^{(1)}(\hat{\rho})+O(t^{3})\,.

(275)

We now turn to the type–2 monotone.
Let
A(t)\coloneqq\left(\sigma_{1/2}+e^{-\Omega Qt}\sigma_{1/2}e^{Q\Omega t}\right)/2
.
We find that

\displaystyle A(0)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{1/2}\,,

(276)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\left.A(t)\right|_{t=0}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(-\Omega Q\sigma_{1/2}+\sigma_{1/2}Q\Omega\right)\,,

(277)

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}\left.A(t)\right|_{t=0}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\Omega Q\Omega Q\sigma_{1/2}-2\Omega Q\sigma_{1/2}Q\Omega+\sigma_{1/2}Q\Omega Q\Omega\right)\,.

(278)

Using Jacobi’s derivative formulas,

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\log{\rm{det}}[A]={\rm{tr}}\left[A^{-1}\frac{d}{dt}A\right]\,,\quad\quad\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}\log{\rm{det}}[A]={\rm{tr}}\left[A^{-1}\frac{d^{2}A}{dt^{2}}\right]-{\rm{tr}}\left[\left(A^{-1}\frac{dA}{dt}\right)^{2}\right]\,,

(279)

we can obtain the zeroth, first, and second derivatives of
f_{1/2,t}^{(2)}(\hat{\rho})\coloneqq-\frac{1}{2}\log\left({\rm{det}}[\sigma_{1/2}]\right)+\frac{1}{2}\log\left({\rm{det}}[A(t)]\right)
at
t=0
,

\displaystyle f_{1/2,0}^{(2)}(\hat{\rho})

\displaystyle=0\,,

(280)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\left.f_{1/2,t}^{(2)}(\hat{\rho})\right|_{t=0}

\displaystyle=0\,,

(281)

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}\left.f_{1/2,t}^{(2)}(\hat{\rho})\right|_{t=0}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}{\rm{tr}}\Big[\Omega Q\Omega Q-\sigma_{1/2}^{-1}\Omega Q\sigma_{1/2}Q\Omega\Big]\eqqcolon F_{Q}^{(2)}(\hat{\rho})\,.

(282)

Then, the monotone
f_{1/2}^{(2)}
takes the form,

\displaystyle f_{1/2,t}^{(2)}(\hat{\rho})=\sum_{k=0}^{2}\frac{t^{k}}{k!}\left.\frac{d^{k}f_{1/2,t}^{(2)}(\hat{\rho})}{dt^{k}}\right|_{t=0}+O(t^{3})=\frac{t^{2}}{2}F_{Q}^{(2)}(\hat{\rho})+O(t^{3})\,.

(283)

Appendix D
Conservation laws in closed Gaussian dynamics

D.1
Invariant normal mode decomposition in the presence of a passive conserved charge

Here, we prove Theorem
12
, an extension of Williamson’s theorem in the presence of conserved charges.
See
12

Note that the conditions
[\Omega M,\Omega Q]=[Q,\Omega]=0
are equivalent to
[M,\Omega Q]=[Q,\Omega]=0
.
The theorem is a corollary of the following lemma, which is of independent interest.

Lemma 24
.

Let
B
be a
(2n\times 2n)
real symmetric positive-definite matrix such that

[B,\Omega_{n}Q]=0\,,

(284)

where
Q=\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}q_{j}I_{2}
,
q_{1},\dots,q_{n}\in\mathbb{R}
.
Let
A
be defined by

A\coloneqq B\Omega_{n}B\,.

(285)

There exists a
(2n\times 2n)
orthogonal matrix
O
such that

OAO^{\rm T}=\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}a_{j}\Omega_{1}\,,\quad\quad[O,\Omega_{n}Q]=0\,,

(286)

for real positive
a_{1},\dots,a_{n}
.

Using this lemma, we now prove Theorem
12
via an argument similar to the proof of the standard Williamson’s theorem, as presented in Ref.
Serafini et al. 2020
.

Proof.

(Theorem
12
)
For clarity, we use
\Omega_{n}
to denote symplectic form on
n
modes.

The direct statement follows because, for any symplectic matrix
S
,

\left[\Omega_{n}M,\Omega_{n}Q\right]=S^{\rm T}S^{-\rm T}\left[\Omega_{n}M,\Omega_{n}Q\right]S^{\rm T}S^{-\rm T}=S^{\rm T}\left[\Omega_{n}SMS^{\rm T},\Omega_{n}SQS^{\rm T}\right]S^{-\rm T}=0\,.

(287)

To prove the converse, first off, assume that
Q=\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}q_{j}I_{2}
.
Matrix
M^{-1/2}
is real symmetric and well-defined because
M
is positive-definite.
Consider the anti-symmetric matrix

A\coloneqq M^{-1/2}\Omega_{n}M^{-1/2}\,.

(288)

In Lemma
24
, we show that for any matrix
M
satisfying the assumptions of the lemma,
A=M^{-1/2}\Omega_{n}M^{-1/2}
can be block-diagonalized using an orthogonal transformation that commutes with
Q\Omega_{n}
, i.e. there exists a
(2n\times 2n)
orthogonal matrix
O
such that

\displaystyle OAO^{\rm T}

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}\nu_{j}^{-1}\Omega_{1}\,,

(289)

\displaystyle[O,Q\Omega_{n}]

\displaystyle=0\,,

(290)

for some real positive
\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{n}
.
Let
D\coloneqq\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}\nu_{j}I_{2}
.
Then, the matrix
S\coloneqq D^{1/2}OM^{-1/2}
is symplectic, because

\displaystyle S\Omega_{n}S^{\rm T}=D^{1/2}\left(\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}\nu_{j}^{-1}\Omega_{1}\right)D^{-1/2}=\Omega_{n}\,,

(291)

where we used Eq.(
289
).
Furthermore, it brings
M
to the canonical form in Eq.(
113
),

\displaystyle SMS^{\rm T}=D=\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}\nu_{j}I_{2}\,,

(292)

and satisfies the invariance condition of Eq.(
114
).
This follows from the fact that
O
,
D
, and
M
all commute with
Q\Omega_{n}=\Omega_{n}Q
, which in turn implies
S\coloneqq D^{1/2}OM^{-1/2}
commutes with
\Omega_{n}Q=Q\Omega_{n}
, and so

SQS^{\rm T}=SQ\Omega_{n}S^{-1}\Omega_{n}^{\rm T}=Q\Omega_{n}\Omega_{n}^{\rm T}=Q\,,

(293)

where the first identity follows from
S\Omega_{n}S^{\rm T}=\Omega_{n}
, and the second from the fact that
S
commutes with
Q\Omega_{n}
.

This proves the claim for the special case
Q=\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}q_{j}I_{2}
. Next, we generalize the result to arbitrary symmetric
Q
that commutes with
\Omega_{n}
.
Let
Q
be any
(2n\times 2n)
real symmetric matrix such that
\left[Q,\Omega_{n}\right]=0
, or, equivalently,
\left[Q\Omega_{n},\Omega_{n}\right]=0
.
Using Lemma
24
for the special case of
B=I_{2n}
and thus
A=\Omega_{n}
, we find a
(2n\times 2n)
orthogonal matrix
O
such that

O\Omega_{n}O^{\rm T}=\Omega_{n}\,,\quad\quad OQ\Omega_{n}O^{\rm T}=\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}q_{j}\Omega_{1}\,,

(294)

which, in particular, implies that
O
is symplectic.

Then, condition
[M,Q\Omega_{n}]=0
is equivalent to
[\widetilde{M},\widetilde{Q}\Omega_{n}]=0
, where
\widetilde{Q}\coloneqq\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}q_{j}I_{2}
is block-diagonal and
\widetilde{M}\coloneqq OMO^{\rm T}
.
Hence, according to the above argument, there exists a symplectic matrix
\widetilde{S}
such that

\displaystyle\widetilde{S}\widetilde{M}\widetilde{S}^{\rm T}=\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}\nu_{j}I_{2}\,,\quad\quad\quad\quad\widetilde{S}\widetilde{Q}\widetilde{S}^{\rm T}=\widetilde{Q}\,,

(295)

and choosing
S=\widetilde{S}O
concludes the proof.
∎

The charge operator
\widehat{Q}=\sum_{j}q_{j}\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{j}
partitions the modes into sectors of constant charge
q
in the sense that a strictly positive purely quadratic Hamiltonian
\hat{H}
commuting with
\widehat{Q}
can be decomposed, due to Theorem
12
, as

\hat{H}=\sum_{q\in\text{Eig}(Q)}\sum_{\alpha}\omega_{q,\alpha}\ \hat{b}^{\dagger}_{q,\alpha}\hat{b}_{q,\alpha}\,,

(296)

for some frequencies
\omega_{q,\alpha}>0
with a multiplicity index
\alpha
, where each creation operator is expressed in terms of the original operators
\{\hat{a}_{j}\}
as

\displaystyle\hat{b}_{|q|,\alpha}^{{\dagger}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j:q_{j}=|q|}r^{+}_{j,\alpha}\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{j}+\sum_{j:q_{j}=-|q|}s^{+}_{j,\alpha}\hat{a}_{j}\,,

(297)

\displaystyle\hat{b}_{-|q|,\alpha}^{{\dagger}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j:q_{j}=|q|}r^{-}_{j,\alpha}\hat{a}_{j}+\sum_{j:q_{j}=-|q|}s^{-}_{j,\alpha}\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{j}

(298)

for some constants
r^{\pm}_{j,\alpha},s^{\pm}_{j,\alpha}
.
In particular,
\hat{b}_{q}^{\dagger}
contains terms
a^{\dagger}_{j}
when
q_{j}
and
q
have the same sign, and terms
a_{j}
when
q_{j}
and
q
have opposite sign.
Therefore,
\hat{b}_{q}^{{\dagger}}
shifts the charge operator
\widehat{Q}
by
q\hat{I}
,

[\widehat{Q},\hat{b}^{\dagger}_{q,\alpha}]=q\hat{b}_{q,\alpha}^{\dagger}\,,

(299)

so that
\hat{b}^{\dagger}_{q,\alpha}\hat{b}_{q,\alpha}
stays within the sector of charge
q
.

Theorem
12
also implies a symmetric decoupling for symplectic matrices.
Suppose symplectic matrix
V=e^{H\Omega_{n}}
is generated by a Hamiltonian matrix
H
such that the assumption in Eq.(
115
) holds.
Then, there exists a symplectic matrix
S
which simultaneously diagonalizes
Q
by congruence,
SQS^{\rm T}=\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}q_{j}I_{2}
and
V
by similarity,
