SVS^{-1}=e^{SHS^{\rm T}\Omega_{n}}=\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}e^{\nu_{j}\Omega_{1}}\,,\quad\nu_{j}\in\mathbb{R}\,.

(300)

Proof of Lemma
24

Proof.

(Lemma
24
)
First, we note that since
B
is symmetric,
A
is anti-symmetric.
For any
q>0
, let

Q_{q}=\bigoplus_{j:|q_{j}|=q}q_{j}\Omega_{1}

(301)

be the restriction of
\Omega_{n}Q
to the subspace
\{j:|q_{j}|=q\}
.
Note that
Q_{q}
can be written as a polynomial of
Q\Omega_{n}
.
To see this, observe that
(Q\Omega_{n})^{2}=-\sum_{j}q_{j}^{2}I_{2}
, which implies that the projector
\bigoplus_{j:|q_{j}|=q}I_{2}
can be expressed as a polynomial of
(Q\Omega_{n})^{2}
.
Then,
Q_{q}=Q\Omega_{n}\bigoplus_{j:|q_{j}|=q}I_{2}
can also be expressed as a polynomial of
Q\Omega_{n}
.

Since
\Omega_{n}Q
and
A
commute and are both anti-symmetric, according to Lemma
25
, there exists an orthogonal matrix
O
such that

OAO^{\rm T}=\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}a_{j}\Omega_{1}\,,\quad\quad[O,\Omega_{n}Q]=0\,,

(302)

where
a_{j}
are not necessarily positive.
Then,

\left(OAO^{\rm T}\right)Q_{q}=\left(\bigoplus_{j:|q_{j}|=q}a_{j}\Omega_{1}\right)\bigoplus_{j:|q_{j}|=q}q_{j}\Omega_{1}=-\bigoplus_{j:|q_{j}|=q}a_{j}q_{j}I_{2}\,.

(303)

Furthermore, since
Q_{q}
can be expressed as a polynomial of
\Omega_{n}Q=Q\Omega_{n}
, and
O
commutes with
\Omega_{n}Q
, it also commutes with
Q_{q}
which, in turn, implies that
OAO^{\rm T}Q_{q}=OAQ_{q}O^{\rm T}
.

Hence, the eigenvalues of
OAO^{\rm T}Q_{q}=OAQ_{q}O^{\rm T}
are equal to the eigenvalues of
AQ_{q}
.
We conclude that the non-zero eigenvalues of
-AQ_{q}
are exactly the multi-set

\{a_{j}q_{j}:|q_{j}|=q\}\,,

with an additional multiplicity 2 for all elements.

Next, we show that the number of positive and negative eigenvalues of
-AQ_{q}
are indeed equal to the number of positive and negative elements of the multi-set

\{q_{j}:|q_{j}|=q\}

with an additional multiplicity 2.

To show this, we note that, by assumption,
B
commutes with
\Omega_{n}Q
, and therefore it also commutes with
Q_{q}
, which in turn implies that
A\coloneqq B\Omega_{n}B
satisfies

\displaystyle AQ_{q}=B\Omega_{n}BQ_{q}=B\Omega_{n}Q_{q}B=-B\left(\bigoplus_{j:|q_{j}|=q}q_{j}I_{2}\right)B\,.

(304)

Since
B
is positive, it is symmetric and full-rank.
Then, applying Sylvester’s law of inertia, the number of positive and negative eigenvalues of
-AQ_{q}
is equal to the number of positive and negative eigenvalues of
\bigoplus_{j:|q_{j}|=q}q_{j}I_{2}
, which are twice the number of positive and negative elements of multi-set
\{q_{j}:|q_{j}|=q\}
.

In summary, by looking at the eigenvalues of
-AQ_{q}
, we found that the number of positive and negative elements of multi-set
\{q_{j}:|q_{j}|=q\}
is equal to the number of positive and negative elements of multi-set
\{a_{j}q_{j}:|q_{j}|=q\}
.
This means that, for all
j
appearing in this sector, either
a_{j}
are all positive, or there exists an even number of
j
for which
a_{j}
is negative.
Furthermore, for exactly half of such
j
, denoted by
S_{+}
,
q_{j}
is positive, and for the other half, denoted by
S_{-}
,
q_{j}
is negative.

Based on this observation, we can construct another orthogonal transformation
\widetilde{O}
, which ensures that
a_{j}
are positive. Recall that the projector
\bigoplus_{j:|q_{j}|=q}I_{2}
can be expressed as a polynomial of
(Q\Omega_{n})^{2}
. Since
O
commutes with
Q\Omega_{n}
, it also commutes with this projector and therefore it is block-diagonal with respect to subspaces
\{j:|q_{j}|=q\}
.
Let
O_{q}
be the component of
O
in the subspace
\{j:|q_{j}|=q\}
.
Then, define

\widetilde{O}_{q}=\text{SWAP}_{\pm}\left(\bigoplus_{j\in S_{+}\sqcup S_{-}}X(j)\right)O_{q}\eqqcolon W_{q}O_{q}\,,

(305)

where
X(j)
is the Pauli
X
operator on mode
j
, and
\text{SWAP}_{\pm}
, exchanges the modes in
S_{+}
and
S_{-}
(recall that they have equal size).
With this definition, we obtain

\displaystyle W_{q}Q_{q}W_{q}^{\rm T}

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}j:|q_{j}|=q,\\
q\notin S_{+}\sqcup S_{-}\end{subarray}}q_{j}\Omega_{1}\oplus\left(\text{SWAP}_{\pm}\left(\bigoplus_{j\in S_{+}\sqcup S_{-}}q_{j}X(j)\Omega_{1}X(j)\right)\text{SWAP}_{\pm}\right)

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}j:|q_{j}|=q,\\
q\notin S_{+}\sqcup S_{-}\end{subarray}}q_{j}\Omega_{1}\oplus(-1)\left(\text{SWAP}_{\pm}\left(\bigoplus_{j\in S_{+}\sqcup S_{-}}q_{j}\Omega_{1}\right)\text{SWAP}_{\pm}\right)

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}j:|q_{j}|=q,\\
q\notin S_{+}\sqcup S_{-}\end{subarray}}q_{j}\Omega_{1}\oplus\bigoplus_{j\in S_{+}\sqcup S_{-}}q_{j}\Omega_{1}=Q_{q}\,,

(306)

where the second equality follows from the fact that
X(j)q_{j}\Omega_{1}X(j)=-q_{j}\Omega_{1}
.

Then, the orthogonal transformation
\widetilde{O}=\bigoplus_{q\geq 0}\widetilde{O}_{q}=WO
, where
W\coloneqq\bigoplus_{q}W_{q}
commutes with
Q\Omega_{n}
, i.e.

\widetilde{O}Q\Omega\widetilde{O}^{\rm T}=WOQ\Omega O^{\rm T}W^{\rm T}=WQ\Omega W^{\rm T}=\bigoplus_{q\geq 0}W_{q}Q_{q}W_{q}^{\rm T}=\bigoplus_{q\geq 0}Q_{q}=Q\Omega\,.

(307)

Furthermore, it brings
A
to the form required in Eq.(
302
) with positive
a_{j}
, i.e.

\widetilde{O}A\widetilde{O}^{\rm T}=WOAO^{\rm T}W^{\rm T}=W\left(\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}a_{j}\Omega_{1}\right)W^{\rm T}=\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}|a_{\pi(j)}|\Omega_{1}\,,

(308)

where
\pi
is an unspecified permutation.
The case of
q=0
corresponds to the standard canonical decomposition of an anti-symmetric matrix within that subspace.
∎

Lemma 25
.

Consider a set of real anti-symmetric matrices
\{A_{1},\dots,A_{N}\}
that pairwise commute.
There exists a real orthogonal matrix
O
that simultaneously brings them into canonical form,

OA_{j}O^{\rm T}=\left(\bigoplus_{k=1}^{{\rm rank}(A_{j})/2}a_{jk}\Omega_{1}\right)\oplus 0_{{\rm dim}(A_{j})-{\rm rank}(A_{j})}\,,\quad\quad\text{for all }j=1,\dots,N\,,

(309)

where
\{\pm ia_{jk}:k=1,\dots,{\rm rank}(A_{j})/2\}
are the eigenvalues of
A_{j}
and
0_{n}
is the
(n\times n)
zero matrix.

Proof.

For
N=1
, the statement reduces to the regular canonical decomposition of anti-symmetric matrix
A_{1}
.
Consider the case of
N=2
.
Since
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
commute and are anti-symmetric, matrices
A_{1}A_{2}=A_{2}A_{1}
,
A_{1}^{2}
and
A_{2}^{2}
are all symmetric and pairwise commute.
Therefore, the algebra generated by
A_{1}A_{2}
,
A_{1}^{2}
, and
A_{2}^{2}
is commutative and decomposes the vector space
\mathcal{V}
into orthogonal subspaces,
\mathcal{V}=\bigoplus_{k}\mathcal{V}_{k}
, such that
A_{1}A_{2}
,
A_{1}^{2}
, and
A_{2}^{2}
are constant over each
\mathcal{V}_{k}
, and for each distinct pair of subspaces
\mathcal{V}_{k},\mathcal{V}_{k^{\prime}}
, at least one of
A_{1}A_{2}
,
A_{1}^{2}
, and
A_{2}^{2}
takes different values between
\mathcal{V}_{k}
and
\mathcal{V}_{k^{\prime}}
.

Let
\Pi_{k}
be the projector to
\mathcal{V}_{k}
.
Since matrices
A_{1}A_{2}
,
A_{1}^{2}
, and
A_{2}^{2}
are all symmetric, and
\Pi_{k}
lives in the algebra generated by these matrices, it is also symmetric.

Now since
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
commute with
A_{1}^{2},A_{2}^{2}
, and
A_{1}A_{2}
, they also commute with all projectors
\Pi_{k}
.
Then,

\Pi_{k}A_{1}\Pi_{k}=\Pi_{k}A_{1}=A_{1}\Pi_{k}\,,

(310)

are anti-symmetric and commute with
\Pi_{k}A_{2}\Pi_{k}
, for all
k
.

Since
A^{2}_{1}
is constant over subspace
\mathcal{V}_{k}
, the symmetric positive operator
-\Pi_{k}A^{2}_{1}\Pi_{k}=a_{1,k}^{2}\Pi_{k}
has a non-negative eigenvalue
a_{1,k}^{2}
.
Then, the eigenvalues of
A_{1}\Pi_{k}
are all in the form
\pm ia_{1,k}
.
The fact that
\Pi_{k}A_{1}\Pi_{k}
is anti-symmetric implies that there exists an orthogonal transformation on
\mathcal{V}_{k}
that brings
\Pi_{k}A_{1}\Pi_{k}
to block-diagonal form

O(\Pi_{k}A_{1}\Pi_{k})O^{\rm T}=ia_{1,k}\Pi_{k}\bigoplus_{j}c_{j}\Omega_{1}\,,

(311)

where
c_{j}\in\{-1,1\}
.

Now recall that
O(\Pi_{k}A_{2}\Pi_{k})O^{\rm T}
is also anti-symmetric and restricted to
\mathcal{V}_{k}
.
Furthermore,

\Pi_{k}A_{1}A_{2}\Pi_{k}=b\Pi_{k}\,,

(312)

for some real number
b
.
This implies that

O(\Pi_{k}A_{1}\Pi_{k})O^{\rm T}O(\Pi_{k}A_{2}\Pi_{k})O^{\rm T}=O(\Pi_{k}A_{1}A_{2}\Pi_{k})O^{\rm T}=b\Pi_{k}\,,

(313)

and, consequently,

O(\Pi_{k}A_{2}\Pi_{k})O^{\rm T}=b\Pi_{k}O(\Pi_{k}A_{1}\Pi_{k})^{-1}O^{\rm T}=iba_{1,k}^{-1}\Pi_{k}\bigoplus_{j}c_{j}\Omega_{1}\,,

(314)

where the inverse is defined on the support of
\Pi_{k}
. This means that the same orthogonal transformation
O
also brings
A_{2}
to block-diagonal form.

Extending the proof to
N>2
is straightforward and involves first bringing
A_{1}
to its canonical form in each subspace as in Eq.(
311
), then repeating the step in Eq.(
313
)
N-1
times for each matrix
A_{j}
,
j=2,\dots,N
.
∎

D.2
Conditions for state interconversion in the presence of a passive conserved charge

See
11

Proof.

On modes that carry the trivial representation, i.e.
q=0
,
\sigma_{1}\Pi_{0}
and
\sigma_{2}\Pi_{0}
are related by a symplectic transformation if and only if they have equal symplectic eigenvalues, i.e. Eq.(
106
) holds.
Hence, we now focus on Eq.(
107
).

We first prove the necessity of Eq.(
107
) when
|q|>0
.
The conditions
[M,\Omega_{n}Q]=[Q,\Omega_{n}]=0
imply that

[M,Q^{2}]=-[M,\Omega_{n}Q\Omega_{n}Q]=0\,,

(315)

for any matrix
M
.
Considering the decomposition

Q^{2}=\sum_{q\in{\rm Eig}(Q)}q^{2}(\Pi_{q}+\Pi_{-q})\,,

(316)

we also get
[M,\Pi_{q}+\Pi_{-q}]=0
, for all
q\in{\rm Eig}(Q)
.
Similarly,
SQS^{\rm T}=Q
implies that
S
commutes with
Q\Omega_{n}
, and hence with
\Pi_{q}+\Pi_{-q}
.

We now assume that there exists symplectic matrix
S
such that
SQS^{\rm T}=Q
and
S\sigma_{1}S^{\rm T}=\sigma_{2}
.
In particular, this implies that
[S,Q\Omega_{n}]=0
, leading to

\sigma_{2}Q\Omega_{n}=S\sigma_{1}S^{\rm T}Q\Omega_{n}=S\sigma_{1}Q\Omega_{n}S^{\rm T}\,,

(317)

where the second equality follows from the facts that
[Q,\Omega_{n}]=0
and
Q=Q^{\rm T}
which imply that
[S^{\rm T},Q\Omega_{n}]=0
.
Right-multiplying Eq.(
317
) with
\Omega_{n}(\Pi_{q}+\Pi_{-q})
, we obtain

\sigma_{2}Q(\Pi_{q}+\Pi_{-q})=S\sigma_{1}Q(\Pi_{q}+\Pi_{-q})S^{-1}\,,

(318)

where in the right-hand side we again used the fact that
S
commutes with
Q\Omega_{n}
, and hence with
Q^{2}=-(Q\Omega_{n})^{2}
, which in turn implies it commutes with
\Pi_{q}+\Pi_{-q}
.
Equivalently this can be written as

q\sigma_{2}(\Pi_{q}-\Pi_{-q})=qS\sigma_{1}(\Pi_{q}-\Pi_{-q})S^{-1}\,,

(319)

Hence,
\sigma_{1}(\Pi_{q}-\Pi_{-q})
and
\sigma_{2}(\Pi_{q}-\Pi_{-q})
have equal ordinary eigenvalues when
q\neq 0
, so Eq.(
107
) holds for all
k
.

Now we proceed to show the converse.
According to Theorem
12
, there exist symplectic matrices
S_{1},S_{2}
such that

\displaystyle S_{1}\sigma_{1}S_{1}^{\rm T}=\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}\nu_{j}I_{2}\,,\quad\quad S_{2}\sigma_{2}S_{2}^{\rm T}=\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}\widetilde{\nu}_{j}I_{2}\,,\quad\quad S_{1}QS_{1}^{\rm T}=S_{2}QS_{2}^{\rm T}=\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}q_{j}I_{2}\,,

(320)

where
\nu_{j},\widetilde{\nu}_{j}\geq 0
(in fact
\nu_{j},\widetilde{\nu}_{j}\geq 1
), and

{\rm SympEig}(Q)\coloneqq\{q_{j}:1,\dots,n\}={\rm Eig}(Q)\,.

(321)

The fact that the symplectic and ordinary eigenvalues of
Q
are identical is a consequence of
[\Omega_{n},Q]=0
and can be seen, e.g. using Theorem
12
with
M=I_{2n}
.
The equations in Eq.(
320
) imply that

\displaystyle S_{1}\sigma_{1}\Omega S_{1}^{-1}=\bigoplus_{j}\nu_{j}\Omega_{1}\,,\quad\quad S_{1}Q\Omega S_{1}^{-1}=\bigoplus_{j}q_{j}\Omega_{1}\,.

(322)

Using the fact that
\Pi_{q}+\Pi_{-q}
can be written as a polynomial of
Q^{2}=-(Q\Omega)^{2}
, the latter equation implies that

\displaystyle S_{1}(\Pi_{q}+\Pi_{-q})S_{1}^{-1}=\bigoplus_{j:|q_{j}|=|q|}I_{2}\,.

(323)

Consider
q\sigma_{1}(\Pi_{q}-\Pi_{-q})=\sigma_{1}Q(\Pi_{q}+\Pi_{-q})
.
Sandwiching this between
S_{1}
and
S_{1}^{-1}
we obtain

\displaystyle qS_{1}\sigma_{1}(\Pi_{q}-\Pi_{-q})S^{-1}_{1}

\displaystyle=S_{1}\left(\sigma_{1}Q(\Pi_{q}+\Pi_{-q})\right)S_{1}^{-1}=-S_{1}\left(\sigma_{1}\Omega Q\Omega(\Pi_{q}+\Pi_{-q})\right)S_{1}^{-1}

(324)

\displaystyle=-\left(\bigoplus_{j}\nu_{j}\Omega_{1}\right)\left(\bigoplus_{j:|q_{j}|=q}q_{j}\Omega_{1}\right)=\bigoplus_{j:|q_{j}|=q}\nu_{j}q_{j}I_{2}

(325)

\displaystyle=|q|\bigoplus_{j:|q_{j}|=|q|}\nu_{j}\frac{q_{j}}{|q|}I_{2}\,,

(326)

where in the second line we used Eq.(
322
) and in the third line we assumed
q\neq 0
.
We conclude that, for
|q|>0
,
\sigma_{1}(\Pi_{q}-\Pi_{-q})
is diagonalizable and its positive and negative eigenvalues are, respectively,
\{\nu_{j}:q_{j}=|q|\}
and
\{-\nu_{j}:q_{j}=-|q|\}
, with an additional multiplicity of 2, and similarly for
\sigma_{2}(\Pi_{q}-\Pi_{-q})
.
Therefore, the assumption of the theorem that
{\rm Eig}(\sigma_{1}(\Pi_{q}-\Pi_{-q}))={\rm Eig}(\sigma_{2}(\Pi_{q}-\Pi_{-q}))
implies that for
|q|>0
,

\displaystyle\{\nu_{j}:q_{j}=|q|\}=\{\widetilde{\nu}_{j}:q_{j}=|q|\}\quad\text{and}\quad\{-\nu_{j}:q_{j}=-|q|\}=\{-\widetilde{\nu}_{j}:q_{j}=-|q|\}\,.

(327)

Consider the set of modes
\{j:q_{j}=q\}
where the diagonal matrix
S_{1}QS_{1}^{\rm T}=S_{2}QS_{2}^{\rm T}
takes the value
q\neq 0
.
We have shown that on these modes,
S_{1}\sigma_{1}S_{1}^{\rm T}
and
S_{2}\sigma_{2}S_{2}^{\rm T}
are diagonal and, up to a possible permutation of modes, have an identical set of diagonal elements.
Therefore, by simply reordering these modes, we can convert
S_{1}\sigma_{1}S_{1}^{\rm T}
to
S_{2}\sigma_{2}S_{2}^{\rm T}
.
That is, there exists an orthogonal symplectic transformation
O=\bigoplus_{q}O_{q}
, where
O_{q}
permutes modes appropriately within
\{j:q_{j}=q\}
and commutes with
S_{1}QS_{1}^{\rm T}
, such that
OS_{1}\sigma_{1}S_{1}^{\rm T}O^{\rm T}=S_{2}\sigma_{2}S_{2}^{\rm T}
.
This, in turn, implies that the matrix
S\coloneqq S^{-1}_{2}OS_{1}
is symplectic, and satisfies
\sigma_{2}=S\sigma_{1}S^{\rm T}
and
Q=SQS^{\rm T}
.
∎

Appendix E
Invariant Gaussian purification

In this section, we provide the proof of Lemma
14
, which we split into two parts, first proving the existence of an invariant Gaussian purification, and then showing that all such purifications are equivalent up to application of a Gaussian unitary on the ancillary system.

E.1
A standard form for invariant Gaussian purifications

Here we provide the proof of the invariant Gaussian purification as stated in Lemma
14
, which is a slight modification of the original Gaussian purification by Holevo and Werner
Holevo and Werner 2001
.

Lemma 26
.

Consider a system with a finite number of bosonic modes and Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{A}
equipped with symplectic Gaussian representation
\hat{S}_{A}(g):g\in G
of a group
G
on
\mathcal{H}_{A}
.

For any Gaussian state
\hat{\rho}_{A}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{A})
which is invariant under
\hat{S}_{A}
, i.e.
\hat{S}_{A}(g)\hat{\rho}_{A}\hat{S}_{A}(g)^{\dagger}=\hat{\rho}_{A}
for all
g\in G
, there exists a Gaussian pure state
\ket{\psi}_{A\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\in\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
where ancilla
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
has Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\cong\mathcal{H}_{A}
and is equipped with representation
\hat{S}_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}(g)\coloneqq\hat{S}^{*}_{A}(g):g\in G
, such that
\ket{\psi}_{A\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
is a purification of
\hat{\rho}_{A}
,

\hat{\rho}_{A}={\rm{tr}}_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}[\outerproduct{\psi}{\psi}_{A\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}]\,,

(328)

and is invariant, i.e.
\ket{\psi}_{A\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}=(\hat{S}_{A}(g)\otimes\hat{S}_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}(g))\ket{\psi}_{A\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
for all
g\in G
.

Assuming
\boldsymbol{d}
and
\sigma
are the displacement vector and covariance matrix of state
\hat{\rho}_{A}
, respectively, then,
\ket{\psi}
can be chosen to have displacement vector
\boldsymbol{d}_{\psi}=\boldsymbol{d}\oplus\boldsymbol{0}
, and covariance matrix given in basis
\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}=\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}_{A}\oplus\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
by

\sigma_{\psi}=\begin{pmatrix}\sigma&\hskip-8.0pt-\sqrt{-\sigma\Omega\sigma\Omega-I}\ \Omega Z_{A}\\[5.0pt]
Z_{A}\Omega\sqrt{-\Omega\sigma\Omega\sigma-I}&\hskip-8.0ptZ_{A}\sigma Z_{A}\end{pmatrix}\,,

(329)

where
\boldsymbol{0}
is the zero vector on
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
,
Z_{A}\coloneqq(I_{A}\otimes Z)
, and
\Omega
is the symplectic form on
A
.

Proof.

The displacement vector
\boldsymbol{d}_{\psi}
is invariant under any choice of
S_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
provided that
\boldsymbol{d}
is invariant under
S_{A}
.

Regarding the covariance matrix, we first note that
\sigma_{\psi}
is a well-defined covariance matrix, for any covariance matrix
\sigma
on system
A
.
Write
\Sigma\coloneqq-\sigma\Omega\sigma\Omega-I
, and note that
\Sigma\geq 0
according to Ineq.(
135
).
Then, the square root of
\Sigma
and its transpose
\sqrt{\Sigma^{\rm T}}=\sqrt{\Sigma}^{\rm T}
are well-defined.
Therefore,
\sigma_{\psi}
is a well-defined, symmetric matrix.

The Schur complement
\sigma_{\psi}/\sigma
of the top left block
\sigma>0
is

\displaystyle\sigma_{\psi}/\sigma=-Z_{A}\sigma Z_{A}-\left(Z_{A}\Omega\sqrt{\Sigma^{\rm T}}\right)\sigma^{-1}\left(-\sqrt{\Sigma}\Omega Z_{A}\right)=(Z_{A}\Omega)\sigma^{-1}(Z_{A}\Omega)^{\rm T}>0\,.

(330)

This implies that
\sigma_{\psi}>0
by the Schur complement condition
Johnson and Horn 1985
, and
{\rm{det}}[\sigma_{\psi}]={\rm{det}}[\sigma]\ {\rm{det}}\left[(Z_{A}\Omega)\sigma^{-1}(Z_{A}\Omega)^{\rm T}\right]=1
.

Moreover, all symplectic eigenvalues of
\sigma_{\psi}
are exactly equal to 1, i.e.
-\sigma_{\psi}(\Omega\oplus\Omega)\sigma_{\psi}(\Omega\oplus\Omega)-I=0
, which can by shown be explicit calculation,

\displaystyle-\sigma_{\psi}(\Omega\oplus\Omega)\sigma_{\psi}(\Omega\oplus\Omega)-I=-\left(\begin{pmatrix}\sigma&-\sqrt{-\sigma\Omega\sigma\Omega-I}\ \Omega Z_{A}\\[5.0pt]
Z_{A}\Omega\sqrt{-\Omega\sigma\Omega\sigma-I}&Z_{A}\sigma Z_{A}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\Omega&0\\[5.0pt]
0&\Omega\end{pmatrix}\right)^{2}-\begin{pmatrix}I&0\\[5.0pt]
0&I\end{pmatrix}=0\,.

(331)

Therefore,
\sigma_{\psi}
is the covariance matrix of a pure state and it satisfies the uncertainty relation,
\sigma_{\psi}+i(\Omega\oplus\Omega)\geq 0
.

The invariance of
\sigma_{\psi}
under representation
g\mapsto S_{A}(g)\oplus S_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}(g)=S_{A}(g)\oplus\widetilde{S}_{A}(g)
follows directly,

\displaystyle(S_{A}(g)\oplus\widetilde{S}_{A}(g))\hskip-2.0pt\begin{pmatrix}\sigma&\hskip-20.0pt-\sqrt{-\sigma\Omega\sigma\Omega-I}\ \Omega Z_{A}\\[5.0pt]
Z_{A}\Omega\sqrt{-\Omega\sigma\Omega\sigma-I}&\hskip-20.0ptZ_{A}\sigma Z_{A}\end{pmatrix}\hskip-2.0pt(S_{A}(g)\oplus\widetilde{S}_{A}(g))^{\rm T}\hskip-2.0pt=\hskip-2.0pt\begin{pmatrix}\sigma&\hskip-20.0pt-\sqrt{-\sigma\Omega\sigma\Omega-I}\ \Omega Z_{A}\\[5.0pt]
Z_{A}\Omega\sqrt{-\Omega\sigma\Omega\sigma-I}&\hskip-20.0ptZ_{A}\sigma Z_{A}\end{pmatrix}\hskip-2.0pt\,,

(332)

where we have made use of the invariance of
\sigma
under
S_{A}
, the fact that
S_{A}(g)
is symplectic, and the definition of the time-reversed representation,
Z_{A}S_{A}(g)Z_{A}
.
∎

In Fig.
5
of the main text, we illustrate a procedure that prepares the purification in Eq.(
329
), which we describe here for the case of a passive representation.
As usual, performing a symplectic basis change generalizes the result to any squeezed passive representation.
Assume system
A
has
n
modes, and consider the normal mode decomposition of the covariance matrix of
\hat{\rho}
,

\sigma\overset{\boldsymbol{a}}{=}V\begin{pmatrix}0&D\\
D&0\end{pmatrix}V^{\rm T}\,,

(333)

for some symplectic matrix
V
and diagonal
D\geq I\equiv I_{n}
.

(i)

Prepare the vacuum on both subsystems
A
and
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
, whose covariance matrix is expressed in basis
\hat{\boldsymbol{a}}_{A}\oplus\hat{\boldsymbol{a}}_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
as

\sigma_{0}\overset{\boldsymbol{a}}{=}\begin{pmatrix}0&I&0&0\\
I&0&0&0\\
0&0&0&I\\
0&0&I&0\end{pmatrix}\,.

(334)

(ii)

Apply the symplectic transformation
V_{\rm sq}(r)
from Proposition
22
on
\sigma_{0}
with

r=\frac{1}{2}\cosh^{-1}{D}\,,

(335)

which is well-defined for
D\geq I
.

V_{\rm sq}(r)
represents
n
2–mode squeezers coupling each mode in
A
with a mode in
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
, leading to the covariance matrix

\sigma_{1}=V_{\rm sq}(r)\sigma_{0}V_{\rm sq}(r)^{\rm T}\overset{\boldsymbol{a}}{=}\begin{pmatrix}0&D&\sqrt{D^{2}-I}&0\\
D&0&0&\sqrt{D^{2}-I}\\
\sqrt{D^{2}-I}&0&0&D\\
0&\sqrt{D^{2}-I}&D&0\end{pmatrix}\,.

(336)

(iii)

Apply the symplectic transformation
V\oplus V\Omega
on
\sigma_{1}
, to obtain

\sigma_{2}=(V\oplus V\Omega)\sigma_{1}(V\oplus V\Omega)^{\rm T}=\begin{pmatrix}\sigma&-\sqrt{-\sigma\Omega\sigma\Omega-I}\ \Omega\\[5.0pt]
\Omega\sqrt{-\Omega\sigma\Omega\sigma-I}&\sigma\end{pmatrix}\,,

(337)

where we have used that
\sqrt{-\sigma\Omega\sigma\Omega-I}=V\big(\sqrt{D^{2}-I}\oplus\sqrt{D^{2}-I}\big)V^{-1}
.

Finally, we apply
Z_{A}
on the second subsystem to restore the symplectic form
\Omega\oplus\Omega
, resulting in the purification of Eq.(
329
).
We can obtain any other
G
–invariant purification of
\hat{\rho}
by acting on
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
with a
G
–invariant Gaussian unitary.

E.2
Equivalence of Gaussian purifications up to local Gaussian operations

Here, we prove that all
G
–invariant Gaussian purifications of a
G
–invariant Gaussian state are equivalent up to a Gaussian unitary on the environment.
In the absence of symmetry constraints, this result has been shown previously
Botero and Reznik 2003
.
Since our proof relies on this special case, we include it here.

Lemma 27
.

Consider a pair of systems
A
and
B
with finite numbers of bosonic modes and Hilbert spaces
\mathcal{H}_{A}
and
\mathcal{H}_{B}
.
Suppose two pure Gaussian states
\ket{\psi_1},\ket{\psi_2}\in\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B}
have the same reduced state on

A
, i.e.
{\rm{tr}}_{B}[\outerproduct{\psi_1}{\psi_1}_{AB}]={\rm{tr}}_{B}[\outerproduct{\psi_2}{\psi_2}_{AB}]
.
Then, there exists a Gaussian unitary
\hat{U}_{B}
acting on
B
that transforms
\ket{\psi_1}_{AB}
to
\ket{\psi_2}_{AB}
, i.e.
\ket{\psi_2}_{AB}=(\hat{I}_{A}\otimes\hat{U}_{B})\ket{\psi_1}_{AB}
.

To reach this result, first note that the first moments of
\ket{\psi_k}_{AB}
are of the form
\boldsymbol{d}_{A}\oplus\boldsymbol{d}_{k,B}
,
k=1,2
, ensuring that the restriction on
A
is the same.
Their covariance matrices
\sigma_{k,AB}
,
k=1,2
, are related by
\sigma_{2,AB}=(I_{A}\oplus V_{B})\sigma_{1,AB}(I_{A}\oplus V_{B})^{\rm T}
for some symplectic matrix
V_{B}
acting on
B

Botero and Reznik 2003
;
Serafini et al. 2020
.
Therefore, we can relate the two purifications as follows,

\ket{\psi_2}_{AB}=(\hat{I}_{A}\otimes\hat{D}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{2,B}-\boldsymbol{d}_{1,B}}\hat{V}_{B})\ket{\psi_1}_{AB}\eqqcolon(\hat{I}_{A}\otimes\hat{U}_{B})\ket{\psi_1}_{AB}\,.

(338)

Next, we show how this result extends in the presence of symmetry constraints.

Lemma 28
.

Consider a pair of systems
A
and
B
with finite numbers of bosonic modes, Hilbert spaces
\mathcal{H}_{A}
and
\mathcal{H}_{B}
, and symplectic Gaussian representations of a group
G
, denoted by
\hat{S}_{A}(g):g\in G
and
\hat{S}_{B}(g):g\in G
, respectively.

Then, two pure Gaussian states
\ket{\psi_1},\ket{\psi_2}\in\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B}
that are
G
–invariant, i.e.
(\hat{S}_{A}(g)\otimes\hat{S}_{B}(g))\ket{\psi_k}=\ket{\psi_k}
for
k=1,2
and all
g\in G
, have the same reduced state on
A
, i.e.
{\rm{tr}}_{B}[\outerproduct{\psi_1}{\psi_1}_{AB}]={\rm{tr}}_{B}[\outerproduct{\psi_2}{\psi_2}_{AB}]
, if and only if there exists a
G
–invariant Gaussian unitary
\hat{U}_{B}
acting on
B
that transforms
\ket{\psi_1}_{AB}
to
\ket{\psi_2}_{AB}
, i.e.

\ket{\psi_2}_{AB}=(\hat{I}_{A}\otimes\hat{U}_{B})\ket{\psi_1}_{AB}\,,\qquad[\hat{U}_{B},\hat{S}_{B}(g)]=0\text{ for all }g\in G\,.

(339)

Proof.

The necessity of the equality of reduced states is obvious.
Here, we prove the converse.
From Lemma
27
we know that there exists, a possibly symmetry-breaking, Gaussian unitary
\hat{U}_{B}
acting on
B
that converts
\ket{\psi_1}
to
\ket{\psi_2}
.
In the following, we prove that
\hat{U}_{B}
can be chosen to be
G
–invariant.

The overall unitary can be realized via
\hat{U}_{B}=\hat{D}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{2,B}-\boldsymbol{d}_{1,B}}\hat{V}_{B}
, where
\hat{V}_{B}
is a purely symplectic Gaussian unitary, and
\boldsymbol{d}_{k,B}
is the restriction of the displacement vector
\boldsymbol{d}_{k}
of state
\ket{\psi_k}
on
B
for
k=1,2
.
Since both states
\ket{\psi_1},\ket{\psi_2}
are
G
–invariant, their displacement vectors
\boldsymbol{d}_{1},\boldsymbol{d}_{2}
are also
G
–invariant, which in turn implies that
\hat{D}_{\boldsymbol{d}_{2,B}-\boldsymbol{d}_{1,B}}
is invariant under
S_{B}
.
Without loss of generality, we can thus assume that the displacement vectors of
\ket{\psi_1}
and
\ket{\psi_2}
are zero and proceed to show that the symplectic Gaussian unitary
\hat{V}_{B}
can also be chosen to be
G
–invariant.

The fact that there exists a symplectic Gaussian unitary
\hat{V}_{B}
such that
\ket{\psi_2}_{AB}=(\hat{I}_{A}\otimes\hat{V}_{B})\ket{\psi_1}_{AB}
implies the existence of a symplectic matrix
V_{B}
such that

\sigma_{2}=(I\oplus V_{B})\sigma_{1}(I\oplus V_{B})^{\rm T}\,.

(340)

With respect to this direct sum decomposition, the covariance matrix can be written as

\sigma_{k}=\begin{pmatrix}\sigma_{k,A}&\sigma_{k,BA}^{\rm T}\\
\sigma_{k,BA}&\sigma_{k,B}\end{pmatrix}\,,\ k=1,2\,,

(341)

with
\sigma_{k,BA}:A\rightarrow B
mapping from the subspace associated with modes in
A
to the subspace associated with modes in
B
.
The invariance of
\sigma_{k}
under representation
g\mapsto S_{A}(g)\oplus S_{B}(g)
implies that

S_{B}(g)\sigma_{k,BA}S_{A}(g)^{\rm T}=\sigma_{k,BA}\,,

(342)

for
k=1,2
and all
g\in G
.
Then,

\displaystyle S_{B}(g)V_{B}S_{B}(g)^{-1}\sigma_{1,BA}

\displaystyle=S_{B}(g)V_{B}\sigma_{1,BA}S_{A}(g)^{\rm T}=S_{B}(g)\sigma_{2,BA}S_{A}(g)^{\rm T}=\sigma_{2,BA}

\displaystyle=V_{B}\sigma_{1,BA}\,,

(343)

for all
g\in G
, where the first and third equalities follow from the invariance of
\sigma_{1,BA}
and
\sigma_{2,BA}
, respectively, as given in Eq.(
342
), while the second and fourth equalities follow from Eq.(
340
).

As a consequence,
S_{B}(g)V_{B}S_{B}(g)^{-1}
and
V_{B}
are equal in the image of
\sigma_{1,BA}
, denoted by
B_{c}
, i.e. for all
g\in G
,

S_{B}(g)V_{B}\Pi_{c}=V_{B}S_{B}(g)\Pi_{c}\,,

(344)

where
\Pi_{c}
is any, possibly non-symmetric, projector to the image of
\sigma_{1,BA}
, i.e. any matrix satisfying the properties (i)
\Pi_{c}^{2}=\Pi_{c}
, (ii)
\Pi_{c}\boldsymbol{d}\in B_{c}
for all
\boldsymbol{d}\in B
, and (iii)
\Pi_{c}\boldsymbol{d}=\boldsymbol{d}
for all
\boldsymbol{d}\in B_{c}
, which, in particular, imply
\Pi_{c}\sigma_{1,BA}=\sigma_{1,BA}
.
Moreover, left-multiplying both sides of Eq.(
342
) with
\Pi_{c}
we find that

\Pi_{c}S_{B}(g)\Pi_{c}\sigma_{1,BA}S_{A}(g)^{\rm T}=\Pi_{c}\sigma_{1,BA}=\sigma_{1,BA}=S_{B}(g)\Pi_{c}\sigma_{1,BA}S_{A}(g)^{\rm T}

(345)

for all
g\in G
, which implies that, for all
g\in G
,

S_{c}(g)\coloneqq\Pi_{c}S_{B}(g)\Pi_{c}=S_{B}(g)\Pi_{c}

(346)

or, equivalently,
(I-\Pi_{c})S_{B}(g)\Pi_{c}=0
.
Therefore, the subspace
B_{c}
is invariant under the action of the symmetry representation
S_{c}(g):g\in G
.
Defining
W_{c}\coloneqq V_{B}\Pi_{c}
and combining Eq.(
346
) with Eq.(
344
), we get

W_{c}S_{c}(g)=S_{B}(g)W_{c}\,,

(347)

for all
g\in G
, i.e.
W_{c}
is the intertwiner for representations
S_{c}(g):g\in G
and
S_{B}(g):g\in G
.
It also satisfies

W^{\rm T}_{c}\Omega_{B}W_{c}=\Pi_{c}^{\rm T}V_{B}^{\rm T}\Omega_{B}V_{B}\Pi_{c}=\Pi_{c}^{\rm T}\Omega_{B}\Pi_{c}\eqqcolon\Omega_{c}\,.

(348)

While
\Omega_{c}
is always an anti-symmetric matrix, it may, in general, vanish or have rank smaller than the dimension of
B_{c}
.
However, this cannot occur when
\sigma_{k}
is the covariance matrix of a pure state (note that the assumption of state purity has not been used up to this point).
According to Refs.
Botero and Reznik 2003
;
Serafini et al. 2020
, in this case, the image
B_{c}
of
\sigma_{BA}
is a subspace that respects the symplectic structure.

More precisely, the covariance matrices
\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{2}
induce decompositions of the
(2n_{B})
–dimensional space
B
as

B=B_{c}\oplus B_{u}=B^{\prime}_{c}\oplus B^{\prime}_{u}\,,

(349)

where
B_{c}
and
B_{u}
are symplectic subspaces that are symplectically orthogonal, with
B_{c}
the image of
\sigma_{1,BA}
.
With respect to the decomposition
(A\oplus B_{c})\oplus B_{u}
,
\sigma_{1}
takes the form

\sigma_{1}=\omega_{1}\oplus\nu_{1}\,,

(350)

where
\omega_{1}
is restricted to subspace
A\oplus B_{c}
and
\nu_{1}
is restricted to
B_{u}
.
In particular,
B_{c}
corresponds to the modes that are correlated with system
A
for the state
\ket{\psi_1}
.

Similarly,
B^{\prime}_{c}
and
B^{\prime}_{u}
are symplectically orthogonal symplectic subspaces, with
B^{\prime}_{c}
being the image of
\sigma_{2,BA}
. With respect to the decomposition
(A\oplus B^{\prime}_{c})\oplus B^{\prime}_{u}
,
\sigma_{2}
takes the form

\sigma_{2}=\omega_{2}\oplus\nu_{2}\,.

(351)

In the above, we established that the
G
–invariant symplectic intertwiner
W_{c}\coloneqq V_{B}\Pi_{c}
maps
B_{c}
to
B^{\prime}_{c}
while preserving the symplectic form.
Choosing
\Pi_{c}
to be the symplectic projector to
B_{c}
,
W_{c}=V\Pi_{c}
maps
\sigma_{1}
to
\omega_{2}
, i.e.

(I_{A}\oplus W_{c})\sigma_{1}(I_{A}\oplus W_{c})^{\rm T}=(I_{A}\oplus V_{B}\Pi_{c})\sigma_{1}(I_{A}\oplus V_{B}\Pi_{c})^{\rm T}=(I_{A}\oplus V_{B}\Pi_{c})\omega_{1}(I_{A}\oplus V_{B}\Pi_{c})^{\rm T}=\omega_{2}\,.

(352)

We now proceed to show that there exists a full
G
–invariant symplectic transformation acting on
B
that maps
\sigma_{1}
to
\sigma_{2}
.
Firstly, as shown in Lemma
29
, any such intertwiner
W_{c}
that preserves the symplectic form can be extended to a full
G
–invariant symplectic transformation
W_{B}
satisfying

W_{B}\Pi_{c}=W_{c}=V_{B}\Pi_{c}\,,\qquad W_{B}^{\rm T}\Omega_{B}W_{B}=\Omega_{B}\,,\qquad W_{B}S_{B}(g)=S_{B}(g)W_{B}\qquad\text{for all }g\in G\,.

(353)

Since
W_{B}
maps the subspace
B_{c}
to
B^{\prime}_{c}
, it should also map
B_{u}
, namely the symplectically orthogonal complement of
B_{c}
, to
B^{\prime}_{u}
, namely the symplectically orthogonal complement of
B^{\prime}_{c}
.
Furthermore, with respect to decomposition
(A\oplus B_{c})\oplus B_{u}
,
\sigma_{1}
is block-diagonal, hence
\tilde{\sigma}_{1}\coloneqq W_{B}\sigma_{1}W_{B}^{\rm T}
is also block-diagonal with respect to
(A\oplus B^{\prime}_{c})\oplus B^{\prime}_{u}
, i.e.

\tilde{\sigma}_{1}\coloneqq(I_{A}\oplus W_{B})\sigma_{1}(I_{A}\oplus W_{B})^{\rm T}=\omega_{2}\oplus\tilde{\nu}_{1}\,,

(354)

where
\tilde{\nu}_{1}
is restricted to
B^{\prime}_{u}
.
Roughly speaking, this means that state
\tilde{\sigma}_{1}
is equal to the target state
\sigma_{2}
, when restricted to the modes in
A
and modes of
B
that are correlated with
A
.
It is left to show that there exists a
G
–invariant symplectic transformation acting only on
B
which transforms
\tilde{\nu}_{1}
to
\nu_{2}
, hence transforming
\tilde{\sigma}_{1}
to
\sigma_{2}
.

The fact that
\tilde{\sigma}_{1}
and
\sigma_{2}
are covariance matrices of pure states immediately implies that
\tilde{\nu}_{1}
and
\nu_{2}
should also correspond to covariance matrices of pure states. This can be seen, e.g. by noting that, as covariance matrices of pure Gaussian states, all symplectic eigenvalues of
\tilde{\sigma}_{1}
and
\sigma_{2}
are 1.
Since
\tilde{\sigma}_{1}=\omega_{2}\oplus\tilde{\nu}_{1}
is block-diagonal with respect to decomposition
(A\oplus B^{\prime}_{c})\oplus B^{\prime}_{u}
, where subspaces
A\oplus B^{\prime}_{c}
and
B^{\prime}_{u}
are symplectic, its symplectic eigenvalues are the union of the symplectic eigenvalues of
\omega_{2}
and of
\tilde{\nu}_{1}
, which in turn, implies that all symplectic eigenvalues of
\tilde{\nu}_{1}
should be 1.
Similarly,
\sigma_{2}=\omega_{2}\oplus\nu_{2}
with respect to the same decomposition, so all symplectic eigenvalues of
\tilde{\nu}_{2}
should be 1.

Based on this observation and the fact that
B^{\prime}_{c}
and
B^{\prime}_{u}
are both
G
–invariant symplectic subspaces we construct a
G
–invariant symplectic transformation that maps
\tilde{\sigma}_{1}
to
\sigma_{2}
.

First, it is useful to apply a change of basis by a symplectic transformation
T
on
B
, such that the subspaces
TB^{\prime}_{c}
and
TB^{\prime}_{u}
are mutually orthogonal with respect to both the Euclidean inner product and the symplectic form.
In other words, they can be thought of as subspaces corresponding to independent bosonic modes.
This transformation brings the covariance matrices to the form

(I_{A}\oplus T)\tilde{\sigma}_{1}(I_{A}\oplus T)^{\rm T}=\kappa_{c}\oplus\kappa_{u}\,,\qquad(I_{A}\oplus T)\tilde{\sigma}_{2}(I_{A}\oplus T)^{\rm T}=\kappa_{c}\oplus\kappa^{\prime}_{u}\,,

(355)

where
\kappa_{u}
and
\kappa^{\prime}_{u}
are both pure state covariance matrices, and the direct sum on the right-hand sides is with respect to the orthogonal subspaces
A\oplus TB^{\prime}_{c}
and
TB^{\prime}_{u}
.

This transformation also brings the representation of symmetry to the form

\tilde{S}_{c}(g)\oplus\tilde{S}_{u}(g)\coloneqq TS_{B}(g)T^{-1}:g\in G\,,

(356)

The covariance matrices
\kappa_{u}
and
\kappa^{\prime}_{u}
are invariant under the representation
\tilde{S}_{u}
. Then, according to part (i) of Proposition
3
, the representation
\tilde{S}_{u}
is a squeezed passive representation, i.e. it is equivalent to an orthogonal symplectic representation, up to a similarity transformation by a symplectic matrix.

Since subspace
TB_{u}^{\prime}
corresponds to a collection of bosonic modes, we can apply Proposition
6
on subspace
TB_{u}^{\prime}
, which guarantees that there exists a symplectic transformation
\tilde{N}_{u}
acting on
TB_{u}^{\prime}
, such that (i) it commutes with
\tilde{S}_{u}(g)
for all
g\in G
, and (ii) it maps
\kappa_{u}
to
\kappa^{\prime}_{u}
, i.e.

\tilde{N}_{u}\kappa_{u}\tilde{N}_{u}^{\rm T}=\kappa^{\prime}_{u}\,.

(357)

We extend this to a symplectic transformation on
TB
by acting with the identity operator on
TB^{\prime}_{c}
, i.e. we consider

\tilde{N}_{B}\coloneqq I_{TB^{\prime}_{c}}\oplus\tilde{N}_{u}\,,

(358)

which clearly commutes with
\tilde{S}_{c}(g)\oplus\tilde{S}_{u}(g)
for all
g\in G
, and satisfies

(I_{A}\oplus\tilde{N}_{B})(\kappa_{c}\oplus\kappa_{u})(I_{A}\oplus\tilde{N}_{B})^{\rm T}=(\kappa_{c}\oplus\kappa^{\prime}_{u})\,.

(359)

Returning to the original basis, with respect to decomposition
B=B^{\prime}_{c}\oplus B^{\prime}_{u}
, we obtain the symplectic transformation

N_{B}=T^{-1}\tilde{N}_{B}T=T^{-1}(I_{TB^{\prime}_{c}}\oplus\tilde{N}_{u})T\,,

(360)

which acts on
B
, is
G
–invariant, i.e. it commutes with representation
S_{B}(g):g\in G
, and transforms
\tilde{\sigma}_{1}
to
\sigma_{2}
, i.e.

\left(I_{A}\oplus N_{B}\right)\tilde{\sigma}_{1}\left(I_{A}\oplus N_{B}\right)^{\rm T}=(I_{A}\oplus T^{-1})(\kappa_{c}\oplus\kappa^{\prime}_{u})(I_{A}\oplus T^{-1})^{\rm T}=\sigma_{2}\,.

(361)

where the first equality follows from Eqs.(
359
,
360
) and the second equality follows from Eq.(
355
).
Hence, the
G
–invariant symplectic transformation
I_{A}\oplus N_{B}W_{B}
acts non-trivially only on
B
and maps
\sigma_{1}
to
\sigma_{2}
, i.e.

(I_{A}\oplus N_{B}W_{B})\sigma_{1}(I_{A}\oplus N_{B}W_{B})^{\rm T}=\sigma_{2}\,,

(362)

concluding the proof.
∎

E.3
Completion of symplectic intertwiner

We now complete the proof of equivalence between invariant Gaussian purifications, by providing a procedure that completes a symplectic intertwiner, i.e. an intertwiner that preserves the symplectic form, into a full
G
–invariant symplectic transformation.

Lemma 29
.

Consider a system of
n
bosonic modes, with phase space
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
, and symplectic form

\Omega\equiv\Omega_{n}=\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}\,.

(363)

Let
S:G\rightarrow\operatorname{Sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
be a symplectic representation that admits a
G
–invariant state, i.e.

S(g)\Omega S(g)^{\rm T}=\Omega\,,\qquad S(g)\sigma S(g)^{\rm T}=\sigma\,,

(364)

for all
g\in G
and some covariance matrix
\sigma
.
Let
F_{\text{in}}
and
F_{\text{out}}
be
G
–invariant symplectic subspaces of equal dimension.
Suppose there exists an intertwiner
V:F_{\text{in}}\rightarrow F_{\text{out}}
preserving the symplectic form, i.e.

VS(g)=S(g)V\,,\qquad\Pi_{F_{\text{in}}}^{\rm T}V^{\rm T}\Omega V\Pi_{F_{\text{in}}}=\Pi_{F_{\text{in}}}^{\rm T}\Omega\Pi_{F_{\text{in}}}\,,

(365)

where
\Pi_{F_{\text{in}}}
is any projector onto
F_{\text{in}}
.
Then,
V
can be extended to a global
G
–invariant symplectic transformation
W\in\operatorname{Sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
, i.e.
W
satisfies

W^{\rm T}\Omega W=\Omega\,,\qquad WS(g)=S(g)W\,,\qquad W\Pi_{F_{\text{in}}}=V\,.

(366)

Proof.

For any symplectic subspace
F\subset\mathbb{R}^{2n}
, the restriction of the symplectic form
\Omega_{n}
to
F
is, by definition, non-degenerate.
It follows that

\mathbb{R}^{2n}=F\oplus F^{\perp}\,,

(367)

where
F^{\perp}
denotes the symplectic complement of
F
, i.e. the set of vectors that are symplectically orthogonal to
F
, and satisfies
\dim(F^{\perp})=2n-\dim(F)
.
Moreover, there exists a symplectic transformation
T
(i.e.
T^{\rm T}\Omega_{n}T=\Omega_{n}
) such that the subspaces

A=TF\,,\qquad B=TF^{\perp}\,,

(368)

are mutually orthogonal with respect to both the Euclidean inner product and the symplectic form
\Omega_{n}
.

It follows that, relative to the decomposition
\mathbb{R}^{2n}=A\oplus B
,
\Omega_{n}
takes the standard block-diagonal form

\Omega_{n}=\Omega_{a}\oplus\Omega_{n-a}\,,

(369)

where
2a=\dim(A)=\dim(F)
. In other words,
A
and
B
describe independent bosonic modes.

Under this change of basis, the original representation
S(g):g\in G
, is transformed to

\widetilde{S}(g)=TS(g)T^{-1}\,:g\in G\,.

(370)

The assumptions that
S(g)
leaves
F
invariant and
S(g)
is symplectic imply that
S(g)
also leaves
F^{\perp}
invariant. This, in turn, implies that
\widetilde{S}(g)
leaves both
A
and
B
invariant. Then, the restrictions of
\widetilde{S}(g)
to these subspaces are also symplectic representations of
G
, i.e.
\widetilde{S}(g)
and can be decomposed as

\widetilde{S}(g)=\widetilde{S}_{A}(g)\oplus\widetilde{S}_{B}(g)\,,

(371)

for all
g\in G
, where the direct sum is with respect to the decomposition
\mathbb{R}^{2n}=A\oplus B
.
Furthermore, the assumption that
S(g)
admits a
G
–invariant state implies that these restricted representations also have invariant states. Since
A
and
B
are both symplectic and orthogonal complements, i.e. they correspond to independent bosonic modes, we can apply part (i) of Proposition
3
to each of them. Thus, there exist symplectic transformations
R_{A}
and
R_{B}
acting on
A
and
B
, respectively, such that

\displaystyle O_{A}(g)=R_{A}\,\widetilde{S}_{A}(g)\,R_{A}^{-1}\,,\qquad O_{B}(g)=R_{B}\,\widetilde{S}_{B}(g)\,R_{B}^{-1}\,,

(372)

where
O_{A}(g)
and
O_{B}(g)
are orthogonal and symplectic for all
g\in G
.

In summary, there exists a symplectic transformation

K\coloneqq(R_{A}\oplus R_{B})\ T\,,

(373)

such that (i) it maps the subspaces
F
and
F^{\perp}
to fully independent sets of bosonic modes, and (ii) it transforms the representation
S(g)
into an orthogonal symplectic (passive) representation on each subsystem,

KS(g)K^{-1}=O(g)=O_{A}(g)\oplus O_{B}(g)\,,

(374)

where the direct sum in both Eq.(
373
) and Eq.(
374
) is taken with respect to
\mathbb{R}^{2n}=A\oplus B
.

Since
A
and
B
are orthogonal with respect to both Euclidean and symplectic forms, there exists a basis

(\boldsymbol{r}_{1},\boldsymbol{r}_{2},\dots,\boldsymbol{r}_{2a},\dots,\boldsymbol{r}_{2n})

where
\{\boldsymbol{r}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{r}_{2a}\}
and
\{\boldsymbol{r}_{2a+1},\dots,\boldsymbol{r}_{2n}\}
span
A
and
B
, respectively, and satisfy

\boldsymbol{r}_{2i}^{\mathrm{T}}\Omega_{n}\boldsymbol{r}_{2j}=\boldsymbol{r}_{2i-1}^{\mathrm{T}}\Omega_{n}\boldsymbol{r}_{2j-1}=0\,,\qquad\boldsymbol{r}_{2i}^{\mathrm{T}}\Omega_{n}\boldsymbol{r}_{2j-1}=\delta_{ij}\,,\quad i,j=1,\dots,n\,.

Based on this basis, one can define the complex basis
{\boldsymbol{a}}
, as

{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}=\frac{\boldsymbol{r}_{2j-1}+i\boldsymbol{r}_{2j}}{\sqrt{2}}\ \ \ \,,\ \ \ \ {\boldsymbol{a}}_{n+j}=\boldsymbol{a}_{j}^{*}=\frac{\boldsymbol{r}_{2j-1}-i\boldsymbol{r}_{2j}}{\sqrt{2}}\,,

(375)

for
j=1,\cdots,n
. Relative to this basis, the subspaces
A
and
B
correspond to basis elements

\{\boldsymbol{a}_{j},\boldsymbol{a}^{*}_{j}={\boldsymbol{a}}_{n+j}:j=1,\dots,a\}
, and
\{\boldsymbol{a}_{j},{\boldsymbol{a}}^{*}_{j}={\boldsymbol{a}}_{n+j}:j=a+1,\dots,n\}
, respectively.

Then, relative to this basis we obtain

KS(g)K^{-1}=O(g)\overset{\boldsymbol{a}}{=}U(g)\oplus U^{*}(g)\,,

(376)

where
U(g)
is a unitary transformation on
\mathbb{C}^{n}
, satisfying

U(g)=U_{A}(g)\oplus U_{B}(g)\,,

(377)

for all
g\in G
, and

O_{A}(g)\overset{\boldsymbol{a}}{=}U_{A}(g)\oplus U_{A}(g)^{*}\,,

(378)

where
U_{A}(g)
is a unitary transformation on
\mathbb{C}^{a}
, for all
g\in G
.
A similar decomposition yields
O_{B}(g)\overset{\boldsymbol{a}}{=}U_{B}(g)\oplus U_{B}(g)^{*}
, where
U_{B}(g)
is a unitary transformation on
\mathbb{C}^{n-a}
, for all
g\in G
.

Further decomposing into irreps of
G
, we obtain

U_{A}(g)=\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\Lambda}U_{\lambda}(g)\otimes I_{m_{A}(\lambda)}\,,

(379)

where
\Lambda
is the set of all irreps of
G
, and
m_{A}(\lambda)
denotes the multiplicity of irrep
\lambda
in
U_{A}(g)
.

In summary, we found

KS(g)K^{-1}=O(g)=O_{A}(g)\oplus O_{B}(g)\overset{\boldsymbol{a}}{=}U(g)\oplus U^{*}(g)\,,

(380)

where
U(g)=U_{A}(g)\oplus U_{B}(g)
. It follows that the multiplicity of
\lambda
in
U(g):g\in G
is

m(\lambda)=m_{A}(\lambda)+m_{B}(\lambda)\,,

(381)

where
m_{A}(\lambda)
and
m_{B}(\lambda)
are multiplicities of irrep
\lambda
in representations
U_{A}
and
U_{B}
, respectively.

Recall that the above decomposition and symplectic transformation
K
depend on the original symplectic decomposition
\mathbb{R}^{2n}=F\oplus F^{\perp}
. In particular, choosing different symplectic decompositions

\mathbb{R}^{2n}=F\oplus F^{\perp}
, will result in different multiplicities
m_{A}(\lambda)
and
m_{B}(\lambda)
. However, part (ii) of Proposition
3
guarantees that their sum
m(\lambda)=m_{A}(\lambda)+m_{B}(\lambda)
is independent of the decomposition.

Now we apply this to the subspaces
F_{\text{in}}
and
F_{\text{out}}
given in the statement of the lemma. Let
\mathbb{R}^{2n}=A_{\text{in}}\oplus B_{\text{in}}
and
\mathbb{R}^{2n}=A_{\text{out}}\oplus B_{\text{out}}
be the decompositions obtained from symplectic subspaces
F_{\text{in}}
and
F_{\text{out}}
, respectively, such that

\displaystyle K_{\text{in}}S(g)K^{-1}_{\text{in}}

\displaystyle=O_{A_{\text{in}}}(g)\oplus O_{B_{\text{in}}}(g)

(382a)

\displaystyle K_{\text{out}}S(g)K^{-1}_{\text{out}}

\displaystyle=O_{A_{\text{out}}}(g)\oplus O_{B_{\text{out}}}(g)\,,

(382b)

for all
g\in G
. Then, for all
\lambda\in\Lambda
,

m_{A_{\text{in}}}(\lambda)+m_{B_{\text{in}}}(\lambda)=m_{A_{\text{out}}}(\lambda)+m_{B_{\text{out}}}(\lambda)

(383)

where
m_{A_{\text{in}}}(\lambda)
is the multiplicity of irrep
\lambda
in

A_{\text{in}}
, the subspace obtained from
F_{\text{in}}
via Eq.(
368
), and
m_{A_{\text{out}}}(\lambda)
,
m_{A_{\text{out}}}(\lambda)
,
m_{B_{\text{out}}}(\lambda)
, are defined in a similar fashion.

To construct the extension of
G
–invariant symplectic intertwiner
V
to
W
, we consider the chain of transformations

F_{\text{in}}\oplus F^{\perp}_{\text{in}}\xlongrightarrow{K_{\text{in}}}A_{\text{in}}\oplus B_{\text{in}}\xlongrightarrow{\widetilde{W}}A_{\text{out}}\oplus B_{\text{out}}\xlongrightarrow{K^{-1}_{\text{out}}}F_{\text{out}}\oplus F^{\perp}_{\text{out}}\,,

(384)

where
\widetilde{W}
is a symplectic,
G
–invariant transformation defined below in Eq.(
394
). The corresponding representations are

S(g)\xlongrightarrow{K_{\text{in}}}O_{A_{\text{in}}}(g)\oplus O_{B_{\text{in}}}(g)\xlongrightarrow{\widetilde{W}}O_{A_{\text{out}}}(g)\oplus O_{B_{\text{out}}}(g)\xlongrightarrow{K^{-1}_{\text{out}}}S(g)\,,

(385)

To construct
\widetilde{W}
, first recall that, by assumption,
V:F_{\text{in}}\rightarrow F_{\text{out}}
is a symplectic intertwiner commuting with
S(g)
, i.e.

VS(g)\Pi_{F_{\text{in}}}=S(g)V\Pi_{F_{\text{in}}}\,,

(386)

which, in turn, implies

VK^{-1}_{\text{in}}(K_{\text{in}}S(g)K_{\text{in}}^{-1})\Pi_{A_{\text{in}}}=K^{-1}_{\text{out}}(K_{\text{out}}S(g)K_{\text{out}}^{-1})K_{\text{out}}VK^{-1}_{\text{in}}\Pi_{A_{\text{in}}}

(387)

or, equivalently,

\widetilde{V}O_{A_{\text{in}}}(g)=O_{A_{\text{out}}}(g)\widetilde{V}

(388)

for all
g\in G
, where

\widetilde{V}\coloneqq K_{\text{out}}VK^{-1}_{\text{in}}\,,

(389)

maps
A_{\text{in}}
to
A_{\text{out}}
, and preserves the symplectic form. That is,

O_{A_{\text{in}}}(g)\Pi_{A_{\text{in}}}=\widetilde{V}^{-1}O_{A_{\text{out}}}(g)\widetilde{V}\Pi_{A_{\text{in}}}

(390)

where
\widetilde{V}^{-1}
is defined on subspace
A_{\text{out}}
. Then, again part (ii) of Proposition
3
implies that the orthogonal symplectic representations
O_{A_{\text{in}}}
and
O_{A_{\text{out}}}
are equivalent, up to an orthogonal symplectic transformation, and therefore

m_{A_{\text{in}}}(\lambda)=m_{A_{\text{out}}}(\lambda)

(391)

for all
\lambda\in\Lambda
.
Combined with Eq.(
383
) we find that

m_{B_{\text{in}}}(\lambda)=m_{B_{\text{out}}}(\lambda)\,,

(392)

for all
\lambda\in\Lambda
.
By Proposition
20
, this immediately implies that unitary representations
U_{B_{\text{in}}}
and
U_{B_{\text{out}}}
are equivalent up to conjugation by a unitary, which, in turn implies that orthogonal symplectic representations
O_{B_{\text{in}}}
and
O_{B_{\text{out}}}
are equivalent up to an orthogonal symplectic transformation. That is, there exists an orthogonal symplectic intertwiner
Q:B_{\text{in}}\rightarrow B_{\text{out}}
, such that, for all
g\in G
,

QO_{B_{\text{in}}}(g)=O_{B_{\text{out}}}(g)Q\,.

(393)

Then, define the symplectic transformation
\widetilde{W}\in\operatorname{Sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
, as

\widetilde{W}=\widetilde{V}\oplus Q

(394)

where
\widetilde{V}:A_{\text{in}}\rightarrow B_{\text{in}}
and
Q:A_{\text{out}}\rightarrow B_{\text{out}}
. By definition, on subspace
A_{\text{in}}
,
\widetilde{W}
is equal to

\widetilde{V}
, i.e.

\widetilde{W}\Pi_{A_{\text{in}}}=\widetilde{V}\Pi_{A_{\text{in}}}\,.

(395)

Furthermore,
\widetilde{W}
is a
G
–invariant symplectic intertwiner, i.e. for all
g\in G
, it satisfies

\displaystyle\widetilde{W}\big(O_{A_{\text{in}}}(g)\oplus O_{B_{\text{in}}}(g)\big)

\displaystyle=(\widetilde{V}\oplus Q)\big(O_{A_{\text{in}}}(g)\oplus O_{B_{\text{in}}}(g)\big)

(396a)

\displaystyle=\big(O_{A_{\text{out}}}(g)\oplus O_{B_{\text{out}}}(g)\big)(\widetilde{V}\oplus Q)

(396b)

\displaystyle=\big(O_{A_{\text{out}}}(g)\oplus O_{B_{\text{out}}}(g)\big)\widetilde{W}\,.

(396c)

Finally, we go back to the original basis and define the symplectic transformation

W\coloneqq K_{\text{out}}^{-1}\widetilde{W}K_{\text{in}}\,.

(397)

Then, using Eq.(
389
) and Eq.(
397
), Eq.(
395
) can be rewritten as

K_{\text{out}}WK^{-1}_{\text{in}}\Pi_{A_{\text{in}}}=K_{\text{out}}VK^{-1}_{\text{in}}\Pi_{A_{\text{in}}}\,.

(398)

By multiplying from left with
K_{\text{out}}^{-1}
and from right with
K_{\text{in}}
, this implies

W\Pi_{F_{\text{in}}}=V\Pi_{F_{\text{in}}}\,,

(399)

i.e.
V
and
W
act identically on subspace
F_{\text{in}}
.
Furthermore, using Eq.(
382
), we find that the left-hand side of Eq.(
396
) is equal to

\displaystyle\widetilde{W}\big(O_{A_{\text{in}}}(g)\oplus O_{B_{\text{in}}}(g)\big)=\widetilde{W}K_{\text{in}}S(g)K^{-1}_{\text{in}}=K_{\text{out}}WS(g)K^{-1}_{\text{in}}\,,

(400)

and the right-hand side of Eq.(
396
) is equal to

\displaystyle\big(O_{A_{\text{out}}}(g)\oplus O_{B_{\text{out}}}(g)\big)\widetilde{W}=K_{\text{out}}S(g)K^{-1}_{\text{out}}\widetilde{W}=K_{\text{out}}S(g)WK^{-1}_{\text{in}}\,.

(401)

We conclude that the symplectic transformation
W
is
G
–invariant, i.e. it satisfies

S(g)W=WS(g)
for all
g\in G
, and
W\Pi_{F_{\text{in}}}=V\Pi_{F_{\text{in}}}
.
In summary, this map is symplectic,
G
–invariant and as shown in Eq.(
399
), its action on
F_{\text{in}}
is equal to the action of
V
. This completes the proof.
∎

Appendix F
Covariant Gaussian dilation

In this section, we derive the channel dilation as stated in Theorem
15
.
Our derivation works in both the discrete and continuous setting, and can be extended to hold in the presence of symmetries.
We start our discussion by motivating a rigorous definition of Gaussian channels (in the absence of symmetries) as completely Gaussianity-preserving CPTP operations.
We then prove that a channel is a (covariant) Gaussian channel if and only if it admits a (covariant) Gaussian Stinespring dilation.
We provide examples of our dilation on 1 mode and in a discrete setting.

F.1
Definition of Gaussian channels

Several recent works
Serafini 2017
;
Weedbrook et al. 2012
introduce Gaussian channels by automatically adopting their Gaussian dilation as a defining property.
We say that a quantum channel
\mathcal{E}:\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{A})\rightarrow\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{B})
admits a Gaussian dilation if there exists a Gaussian state
\hat{\tau}_{BE}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{B}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{E})
, where
E
is the environment, and a symplectic Gaussian unitary
\hat{V}_{ABE}
acting on the composite system
ABE
, such that

\mathcal{E}(\hat{\rho}_{A})={\rm{tr}}_{AE}\left[\hat{V}_{ABE}(\hat{\rho}_{A}\otimes\hat{\tau}_{BE})\hat{V}_{ABE}^{\dagger}\right]\,,

(402)

for all input states
\hat{\rho}_{A}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{A})
.
Proposition
31
shows any such dilation immediately implies that
\mathcal{E}
preserves the Gaussian character of quantum states and acts in phase space via matrices
X,Y
according to Eq.(
7
).

A more axiomatic approach
Demoen et al. 1977
;
Demoen et al. 1979
;
Lindblad 2000
;
Holevo and Werner 2001
;
Caruso et al. 2008
involves shifting the focus from the Schrödinger to the Heisenberg picture by defining the dual
\mathcal{E}^{\dagger}
of a channel
\mathcal{E}
via the implicit relation
{\rm{tr}}[\mathcal{E}(\rho)\hat{O}]={\rm{tr}}[\rho\mathcal{E}^{\dagger}(\hat{O})]
.
Then, one can define Gaussian channels as follows.

Definition 30
.

A bosonic quantum channel
\mathcal{E}:\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{A})\rightarrow\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{B})
is called a Gaussian channel if its dual channel
\mathcal{E}^{\dagger}
maps displacement operators to displacement operators according to

\mathcal{E}^{\dagger}(D_{\boldsymbol{d}})\overset{\boldsymbol{r}}{=}D_{X^{\rm T}\boldsymbol{d}}\ \exp(-\frac{1}{2}\bmd^{\rm T} Y \bmd+ i\bmd^{\rm T}\bmxi)\,,

(403)

for real vector
\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\mathbb{R}^{2n_{B}}
,
(2n_{B}\times 2n_{A})
real matrix
X
and
(2n_{B}\times 2n_{B})
real matrix
Y
, where
n_{A}
and
n_{B}
are the numbers of modes in systems
A
and
B
respectively.

From this definition, one can directly recover the channel uncertainty relation (Ineq.(
8
)) and the action of
\mathcal{E}
in phase space (Eq.(
7
))
Lindblad 2000
;
Demoen et al. 1977
.
The phase
f(\boldsymbol{d})\coloneqq\exp(-\frac{1}{2}\bmd^{\rm T} Y \bmd+ i\bmd^{\rm T}\bmxi)
is a Gaussian function of
\boldsymbol{d}
, so the characteristic function
Barnett and Radmore 2002
of output
\mathcal{E}(\rho)
is Gaussian whenever the characteristic function of input
\rho
is Gaussian.
As a consequence,
\mathcal{E}
sends Gaussian states to Gaussian states.
In fact,
\mathcal{E}
is completely Gaussianity-preserving, i.e. for any environment
E
,
(\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{E})(\hat{\rho}_{AE})
is Gaussian whenever
\hat{\rho}_{AE}
is Gaussian, where the phase space action of
\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{E}
is characterized by vector
\boldsymbol{\xi}\oplus 0
, and matrices
X\oplus I
and
Y\oplus 0

Serafini 2017
.

F.2
Covariant Gaussian Stinespring dilation

We first show that every covariant Gaussian dilation is a covariant Gaussian channel.

Proposition 31
.

Consider a pair of systems
A
and
B
with a finite number of bosonic modes, and Hilbert spaces
\mathcal{H}_{A}
and
\mathcal{H}_{B}
.
Suppose each system is equipped with a symplectic Gaussian representation of a group
G
that admits an invariant state, denoted as
\hat{S}_{A}(g):g\in G
, and
\hat{S}_{B}(g):g\in G
, respectively (Recall that such representations are characterized in Proposition
3
).

Let
\mathcal{E}:\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{A})\rightarrow\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{B})
be a linear map defined by the dilation

\mathcal{E}(\hat{\rho}_{A})={\rm{tr}}_{A\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\left[\hat{V}_{AB\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}(\hat{\rho}_{A}\otimes\hat{\tau}_{B\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu})\hat{V}_{AB\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}^{\dagger}\right]\,,\quad\text{ for all }\hat{\rho}_{A}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{A})\,,

(404)

where
\hat{\tau}_{B\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{B}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu})
is a Gaussian state which commutes with representation
\hat{S}_{B}\otimes\hat{S}_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
and
\hat{V}_{AB\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
is a symplectic Gaussian unitary which commutes with representation
\hat{S}_{A}\otimes\hat{S}_{B}\otimes\hat{S}_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
.
Then,
\mathcal{E}
is a covariant Gaussian channel under input/output representations
\hat{S}_{A},\hat{S}_{B}
, i.e.
\mathcal{E}(\hat{S}_{A}(g)(\cdot)\hat{S}_{A}(g)^{\dagger})=\hat{S}_{B}(g)\mathcal{E}(\cdot)\hat{S}_{B}(g)^{\dagger}
for all
g\in G
.

Furthermore, there exist real matrices
X,Y
that satisfy

(i)

consistency with dilation,
V(\sigma\oplus\sigma_{\tau})V^{\rm T}\bigg|_{B}=X\sigma X^{\rm T}+Y
, where
\sigma_{\tau}
is the covariance matrix of state
\hat{\tau}
;

(ii)

the uncertainty relation,
Y+i(\Omega_{B}-X\Omega_{A}X^{\rm T})\geq 0
, where
\Omega_{A},\Omega_{B}
are the symplectic forms on
A,B
;

(iii)

the covariance conditions,
S_{B}(g)XS_{A}(g)^{-1}=X
and
S_{B}(g)YS_{B}(g)^{\rm T}=Y
for all
g\in G
.

Proof.

The dilation defined in Eq.(
404
) is a Gaussian quantum channel as a composition of Gaussian quantum channels according to Definition
30
, and it is also covariant in the sense that

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(\hat{S}_{A}\hat{\rho}_{A}\hat{S}_{A}^{\dagger})

\displaystyle={\rm{tr}}_{A\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\left[\hat{V}_{AB\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}(\hat{S}_{A}\hat{\rho}_{A}\hat{S}_{A}^{\dagger}\otimes\hat{\tau}_{B\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu})\hat{V}_{AB\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}^{\dagger}\right]

\displaystyle={\rm{tr}}_{A\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\left[(\hat{S}_{A}\otimes\hat{S}_{B}\otimes\hat{S}_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu})\hat{V}_{AB\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}(\hat{\rho}_{A}\otimes\hat{\tau}_{B\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu})\hat{V}_{AB\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}^{\dagger}(\hat{S}_{A}\otimes\hat{S}_{B}\otimes\hat{S}_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu})^{\dagger}\right]

\displaystyle=\hat{S}_{B}{\rm{tr}}_{A\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\left[\hat{V}_{AB\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}(\hat{\rho}_{A}\otimes\hat{\tau}_{B\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu})\hat{V}_{AB\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}^{\dagger}\right]\hat{S}_{B}^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=\hat{S}_{B}\mathcal{E}(\hat{\rho}_{A})\hat{S}_{B}^{\dagger}\,.

(405)

The first and last equalities follow from the map definition in Eq.(
404
), the second equality follows from the invariance conditions on
\hat{V}_{AB\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
and
\hat{\tau}_{B\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
, and the third equality follows from properties of the partial trace.

Since
\mathcal{E}
is a Gaussian channel, it is fully characterized by two real matrices
X,Y
.
The dilation condition
(i)
follows directly by this equivalent characterization of the channel, the uncertainty relation
(ii)
follows from the Gaussianity of the channel, and the invariance conditions
(iii)
follow from the covariance of the channel according to statement
(iv)
in Proposition
18
.
∎

We now proceed to prove the converse of Proposition
31
, which informally states that every covariant Gaussian channel
\mathcal{E}:\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{A})\rightarrow\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{B})
admits a covariant dilation.
The dilation requires an ancilla of
2n_{B}
modes, where
n_{B}
is the number of modes in the output system
B
, prepared in a pure state.

See
15

Proof.

First, consider an ancillary system with the same number of modes as
A
, denoted by
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
.
Let
\ket{\psi}_{A\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
be an arbitrary Gaussian pure state on
A\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
such that its reduced state
\rho_{A}={\rm{tr}}_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\left[\outerproduct{\psi}{\psi}_{A\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\right]
on
A
is full rank.

Next, act with channel
\mathcal{E}
on subsystem
A
, to obtain the state

\hat{\tau}_{B\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}=(\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathcal{I})(\outerproduct{\psi}{\psi}_{A\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu})\,,

(406)

where
\mathcal{I}(\cdot)=\hat{I}_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}(\cdot)\hat{I}_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
is the identity channel on
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
.
Lemma
14
guarantees that
\hat{\tau}_{B\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
has a Gaussian purification

\ket{\Phi}_{A\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5muB\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\in\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\,,

(407)

such that the reduced state on
B\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
is
\hat{\tau}_{B\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
, i.e.

\hat{\tau}_{B\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}={\rm{tr}}_{A\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}[\ket{\Phi}\bra{\Phi}_{A\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5muB\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}]\,,

(408)

where
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
has, at most, the same number of modes as
B
.

Moreover, consider the Gaussian state

\ket{\Psi}_{A\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5muB\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}=\ket{\psi}_{A\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\otimes\ket{\eta}_{B\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\in\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\,,

(409)

where
\ket{\eta}_{B\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
is some pure state.
The reduced states of
\ket{\Phi}_{A\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5muB\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
and
\ket{\Psi}_{A\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5muB\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
on
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
are the same, so Lemma
14
states that there exists a Gaussian unitary
\hat{V}_{AB\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
on the complement system
AB\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
such that

\ket{\Phi}_{A\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5muB\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}=\left(\hat{I}_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\otimes\hat{V}_{AB\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\right)\ket{\Psi}_{A\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5muB\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\,.

(410)

Finally, define the linear map

\mathcal{F}(\cdot)={\rm{tr}}_{A\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\left[\hat{V}_{AB\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}((\cdot)\otimes\outerproduct{\eta}{\eta}_{B\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu})\hat{V}_{AB\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}^{\dagger}\right]\,,

(411)

and observe that

\displaystyle(\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{I})(\outerproduct{\psi}{\psi}_{A\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu})

\displaystyle={\rm{tr}}_{A\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\left[\left(\hat{I}_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\otimes\hat{V}_{AB\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\right)\left(\outerproduct{\psi}{\psi}_{A\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\otimes\outerproduct{\eta}{\eta}_{B\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\right)\left(\hat{I}_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\otimes\hat{V}_{AB\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\right)^{\dagger}\right]

\displaystyle={\rm{tr}}_{A\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\left[\left(\hat{I}_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\otimes\hat{V}_{AB\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\right)\outerproduct{\Psi}{\Psi}_{A\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5muB\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\left(\hat{I}_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\otimes\hat{V}_{AB\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\right)^{\dagger}\right]

\displaystyle={\rm{tr}}_{A\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\left[\outerproduct{\Phi}{\Phi}_{A\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5muB\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\right]

\displaystyle=\hat{\tau}_{B\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathcal{I})(\outerproduct{\psi}{\psi}_{A\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu})\,,

(412)

where all equalities follow from the definitions given earlier.
Since the reduced state
\hat{\rho}_{A}
of state
\ket{\psi}_{A\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
is full-rank, we conclude the equality of channels
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{F}
.
This can be seen, for instance, by noting that for any bounded operator
\hat{M}_{A}
on system
A
, there exists a bounded operator
\hat{N}_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
on system
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
such that

\hat{M}_{A}={\rm{tr}}_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\left[\left(\hat{I}\otimes\hat{N}_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\right)\outerproduct{\psi}{\psi}_{A\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\right]\,.

(413)

Namely, one can choose
\hat{N}=\hat{\rho}^{-1/2}\hat{M}^{\rm T}\hat{\rho}^{-1/2}
, where
\hat{M}^{\rm T}
is the transpose of
\hat{M}
with respect to an arbitrary orthonormal basis, e.g. the Fock basis.
Therefore, for all bounded operators
\hat{M}_{A}
,

\mathcal{E}(\hat{M}_{A})={\rm{tr}}_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\left[(\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathcal{I})(\outerproduct{\psi}{\psi}_{A\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu})\left(\hat{I}\otimes\hat{N}_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\right)\right]={\rm{tr}}_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\left[(\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{I})(\outerproduct{\psi}{\psi}_{A\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu})\left(\hat{I}\otimes\hat{N}_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\right)\right]=\mathcal{F}(\hat{M}_{A})\,,

(414)

where the first and third equalities follow from Eq.(
413
) and the second equality from Eq.(
412
).

Next, we argue that if
\mathcal{E}
is
G
–covariant, then all the steps in this construction can be chosen to respect the symmetry.
To obtain
G
–invariant purifications, we choose the symmetry representations on systems
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
and
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
to be the complex conjugate of the representations on
A
and
B
, respectively, i.e.

\hat{S}_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}=\hat{S}^{*}_{A}\,,\quad\quad\hat{S}_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}=\hat{S}^{*}_{B}\,,

(415)

where the complex conjugate is defined in the common eigenbasis of position operators.
Then, due to Lemma
28
, state
\ket{\psi}_{A\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
can be chosen to be
G
–invariant, i.e.

\left(\hat{S}_{A}(g)\otimes\hat{S}_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}(g)\right)\ket{\psi}_{A\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}=\ket{\psi}_{A\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\,,

(416)

for all
g\in G
, where the reduced state on
A
is full rank.
The
G
–covariance of
\mathcal{E}
implies that the output state
\hat{\tau}_{B\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
is also
G
–invariant, and therefore, by Lemma
28
, it has a
G
–invariant Gaussian purification
\ket{\Phi}_{A\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5muB\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
satisfying Eq.(
407
).

Consider the second purification
\ket{\Psi}_{A\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5muB\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
of the reduced state on
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
, provided in Eq.(
409
).
As we saw in Proposition
3
, since both
\hat{S}_{B}
and
\hat{S}_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
admit
G
–invariant states, they also admit
G
–invariant pure Gaussian states
\ket{\eta_1}_{B}
and
\ket{\eta_2}_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
such that

\hat{S}_{B}\ket{\eta_1}_{B}=\ket{\eta_1}_{B}\,,\quad\quad\hat{S}_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\ket{\eta_2}_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}=\ket{\eta_2}_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\,,

(417)

for all
g\in G
.
Choosing
\ket{\eta}_{B\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}=\ket{\eta_1}_{B}\otimes\ket{\eta_2}_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
thus gives another
G
–invariant Gaussian purification of
\ket{\Psi}_{A\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5muB\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
.

Finally, according to Lemma
28
, there exists a symplectic Gaussian unitary
\hat{V}_{AB\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
that commutes with the representation of symmetry on
AB\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
, i.e.
\hat{S}_{A}\otimes\hat{S}_{B}\otimes\hat{S}_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
, and converts one purification to the other, as described in Eq.(
410
).
Hence, Eq.(
411
) and Eq.(
412
) remain valid, so Eq.(
411
) provides a dilation of the
G
–covariant channel
\mathcal{E}
.
∎

Note that we are free to choose any (invariant) full-rank state
\hat{\rho}
to construct the dilation in the proof of Theorem
15
.
Consider the Stinespring dilation of a channel tensor product
\mathcal{E}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{E}_{2}:\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{A_{1}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{A_{2}})\rightarrow\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{B_{1}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B_{2}})
,

\displaystyle(\mathcal{E}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{E}_{2})((\cdot)_{A_{1}A_{2}})={\rm{tr}}_{A_{1}A_{2}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{1}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{2}}\left[\hat{V}_{A_{1}A_{2}B_{1}B_{2}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{1}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{2}}((\cdot)_{A_{1}A_{2}}\otimes\outerproduct{\eta}{\eta}_{B_{1}B_{2}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{1}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{2}})\hat{V}_{A_{1}A_{2}B_{1}B_{2}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{1}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{2}}^{\dagger}\right]\,,

(418)

Choosing the full-rank, tensor-product state
\hat{\rho}_{A_{1}}\otimes\hat{\rho}_{A_{2}}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{A_{1}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{A_{2}})
leads to a tensor product form for the unitary

\hat{V}_{A_{1}A_{2}B_{1}B_{2}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{1}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{2}}=\hat{V}^{(1)}_{A_{1}B_{1}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{1}}\otimes\hat{V}^{(2)}_{A_{2}B_{2}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{2}}\,.

(419)

The Stinespring dilation of a channel composition
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\circ\mathcal{E}
, where
\mathcal{E}:\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{A})\rightarrow\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{B})
and
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}:\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{B})\rightarrow\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{C})
, can be written as follows,

\displaystyle(\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\circ\mathcal{E})((\cdot)_{A})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\left({\rm{tr}}_{A\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\left[\hat{V}_{AB\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}(\hat{\rho}_{A}\otimes\outerproduct{\eta}{\eta}_{B\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu})\hat{V}_{AB\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}^{\dagger}\right]\right)

\displaystyle={\rm{tr}}_{B\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muC\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\left[\hat{V}_{BC\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muC\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}^{\prime}\left({\rm{tr}}_{A\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\left[\hat{V}_{AB\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}((\cdot)_{A}\otimes\outerproduct{\eta}{\eta}_{B\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu})\hat{V}_{AB\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}^{\dagger}\right]\otimes\outerproduct{\eta}{\eta}_{C\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muC\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\right)\hat{V}_{BC\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muC\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}^{\prime{\dagger}}\right]

\displaystyle={\rm{tr}}_{AD}\left[\hat{V}^{\prime\prime}_{ACD}((\cdot)_{A}\otimes\outerproduct{\eta}{\eta}_{CD})\hat{V}_{ACD}^{\prime\prime{\dagger}}\right]\,,

(420)

where the ancilla system is
D=B\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muC\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
and
\hat{V}^{\prime\prime}_{ACD}\coloneqq\hat{V}_{BC\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muC\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}^{\prime}\hat{V}_{AB\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
.

F.3
Phase-insensitive Gaussian channels on 1 mode

We use our dilation theorem to construct dilations for all 1--mode phase-insensitive channels
14
14

14

Throughout this section, every covariance matrix
\sigma_{\hat{\rho}}
of composite state
\hat{\rho}_{A_{1}\dots A_{n}}
is represented in the basis
\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}_{A_{j}}
.
.
Any such channel
\mathcal{E}:\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{A})\rightarrow\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{B})\cong\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{A})
, is defined by real matrices
X=xI
and
Y=yI
, where
y\geq|x^{2}-1|

Holevo 2007
;
Giovannetti et al. 2014
.
Pick the full-rank thermal density operator

\hat{\rho}_{A}=\frac{\exp(-\beta\hat{a}^\dag\hat{a})}{{\rm{tr}}[\exp(-\beta\hat{a}^\dag\hat{a})]}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{\bar{n}^{k}}{(\bar{n}+1)^{k+1}}\outerproduct{k}{k}\,,

(421)

with zero first moment and
\sigma=cI
, where we set
c\equiv\cosh{(2r)}=2\bar{n}+1
and
s\equiv\sinh{(2r)}=2\sqrt{\bar{n}(\bar{n}+1)}
, and
\bar{n}=(e^{\beta}-1)^{-1}
.
Consider the purification
\ket{\psi}_{A\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
of
\hat{\rho}_{A}
according to Eq.(
120
) with covariance matrix

\sigma_{\psi}=\begin{pmatrix}cI&sZ\\[5.0pt]
sZ&cI\end{pmatrix}\,,

(422)

where the symplectic form is
\Omega\oplus\Omega
.

Applying
\mathcal{E}
on the first subsystem, we get
\hat{\tau}_{B\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}=(\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathcal{I})(\outerproduct{\psi}{\psi}_{A\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu})
with covariance matrix

\displaystyle\sigma_{\tau}=(X\oplus I)\sigma_{\psi}(X\oplus I)^{\rm T}+(Y\oplus 0)=\begin{pmatrix}(cx^{2}+y)I&sxZ\\[5.0pt]
sxZ&cI\end{pmatrix}\,.

(423)

Purifying
\hat{\tau}_{B\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
according to Eq.(
120
), we get
\ket{\Phi}_{B\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5muA}
with covariance matrix

\displaystyle\sigma_{\Phi}=\begin{pmatrix}(cx^{2}+y)I&sxZ&uZ&tI\\[5.0pt]
sxZ&cI&-tI&vZ\\[5.0pt]
uZ&-tI&(cx^{2}+y)I&-sxZ\\[5.0pt]
tI&vZ&-sxZ&cI\end{pmatrix}\,,

(424)

where we have calculated the off-diagonal block
M
as follows,

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle=\sqrt{-\sigma_{\tau}(\Omega\oplus\Omega)\sigma_{\tau}(\Omega\oplus\Omega)-(I\oplus I)}=\sqrt{\begin{pmatrix}((cx^{2}+y)^{2}-s^{2}x^{2}-1)I&sx(c(1-x^{2})-y)Z\\[5.0pt]
sx(c(x^{2}-1)+y)Z&s^{2}(1-x^{2})I\end{pmatrix}}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}uI&tZ\\[5.0pt]
-tZ&vI\end{pmatrix}\,,

(425)

for parameters
u,v,t
defined as

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(Q+\frac{(cx^{2}+y)^{2}-c^{2}}{Q}\right)\,,

(426)

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(Q-\frac{(cx^{2}+y)^{2}-c^{2}}{Q}\right)\,,\text{ and}

(427)

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle=\frac{sx(c(1-x^{2})-y)}{Q}\,,\text{ where}

(428)

\displaystyle Q

\displaystyle=\sqrt{(cx^{2}+y)^{2}-2s^{2}x^{2}+s^{2}-1+2s\sqrt{((cx^{2}+y)^{2}-s^{2}x^{2}-1)(1-x^{2})+x^{2}(c(1-x^{2})-y)^{2}}}\,.

(429)

The state
\ket{\Psi}_{B\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5muA}=\ket{\psi}_{A\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\otimes\ket{0}^{\otimes 2}_{B\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
has covariance matrix

\displaystyle\sigma_{\Psi}=\begin{pmatrix}I&0&0&0\\
0&cI&0&sZ\\
0&0&I&0\\
0&sZ&0&cI\end{pmatrix}\,.

(430)

To construct the dilation, we need to find a symplectic matrix
V_{AB\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}
such that
(I_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\oplus V_{AB\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu})\sigma_{\Psi}(I_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muA\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\oplus V_{AB\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu})^{\rm T}=\sigma_{\Phi}
, which we first do separately for attenuator and amplifier channels on 1 mode.

Attenuators
\mathcal{E}^{\rm att}_{\alpha,\beta}
are characterized by
x=\cos{\alpha}
and
y=(2n_{\beta}+1)\sin^{2}{\alpha}
, where
n_{\beta}
is the average number of thermal excitations.
To construct its dilation, we can choose
\bar{n}=n_{\beta}
for the full-rank state that we define in Eq.(
421
), so that
y=\cosh(2r)\sin^{2}{\alpha}
.
Since the sign of
x
does not affect the definition of the channel, we can also choose
\alpha\in[0,\pi/2)
.
As a consequence,
Q=2\sinh(2r)\sin\alpha
, hence
u=v=\sinh(2r)\sin\alpha
,
t=0
, and

\displaystyle\sigma_{\Phi}=\begin{pmatrix}\cosh(2r)I&\sinh(2r)\cos\alpha Z&\sinh(2r)\sin\alpha Z&0\\[5.0pt]
\sinh(2r)\cos\alpha Z&\cosh(2r)I&0&\sinh(2r)\sin\alpha Z\\[5.0pt]
\sinh(2r)\sin\alpha Z&0&\cosh(2r)I&-\sinh(2r)\cos\alpha Z\\[5.0pt]
0&\sinh(2r)\sin\alpha Z&-\sinh(2r)\cos\alpha Z&\cosh(2r)I\end{pmatrix}\,,

(431)

Indeed, this covariance matrix
\sigma_{\Phi}
is equivalent to
\sigma_{\Psi}
, up to the symplectic matrix

V^{\rm att}_{\alpha,\beta}\coloneqq V_{AB\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}=V_{\rm bs}(-\alpha+\pi/2)_{AB}V_{\rm 2sq}\left(\frac{1}{2}\cosh^{-1}(2n_{\beta}+1)\right)_{B\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\,,

(432)

which is invariant under any symmetry representation
S_{A}(g)\oplus S_{B}(g)\oplus S_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}(g)
, provided that
S_{A}(g)=S_{B}(g)=ZS_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}(g)Z
for all
g\in G
.

Amplifiers
\mathcal{E}^{\rm amp}_{\alpha,\beta}
are characterized by
x=\cosh{\alpha}
and
y=(2n_{\beta}+1)\sinh^{2}{\alpha}
, where
n_{\beta}
is the average number of thermal excitations.
Similarly, we choose
\alpha\in[0,\pi/2)
and
n=n_{\beta}
, so that
y=\cosh(2r)\sinh^{2}{\alpha}
.
As a consequence,
Q=\cosh(2r)\sinh(2\alpha)
, hence
u=\cosh(2r)\sinh(2\alpha)
,
v=0
,
t=-\sinh(2r)\sinh\alpha
, and

\displaystyle\sigma_{\Phi}=\begin{pmatrix}\cosh(2r)\cosh(2\alpha)I&\sinh(2r)\cosh\alpha Z&\cosh(2r)\sinh(2\alpha)Z&-\sinh(2r)\sinh\alpha I\\[5.0pt]
\sinh(2r)\cosh\alpha Z&\cosh(2r)I&\sinh(2r)\sinh\alpha I&0\\[5.0pt]
\cosh(2r)\sinh(2\alpha)Z&\sinh(2r)\sinh\alpha I&\cosh(2r)\cosh(2\alpha)I&-\sinh(2r)\cosh\alpha Z\\[5.0pt]
-\sinh(2r)\sinh\alpha I&0&-\sinh(2r)\cosh\alpha Z&\cosh(2r)I\end{pmatrix}\,,

(433)

Indeed, this covariance matrix
\sigma_{\Phi}
is equivalent to
\sigma_{\Psi}
, up to the symplectic matrix

V^{\rm amp}_{\alpha,\beta}\coloneqq V_{AB\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}=V_{\rm 2sq}(\alpha)_{B\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}V_{\rm bs}(\pi/2)_{AB}V_{\rm 2sq}\left(\frac{1}{2}\cosh^{-1}(2n_{\beta}+1)\right)_{B\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\,,

(434)

which is invariant under any symmetry representation
S_{A}(g)\oplus S_{B}(g)\oplus S_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}(g)
, provided that
S_{A}(g)=S_{B}(g)=ZS_{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muB\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}(g)Z
for all
g\in G
.

Any 1–mode phase-covariant channel
\mathcal{E}
is unitarily equivalent to the composition of a pure loss channel
\mathcal{E}^{\rm att}_{\alpha,\infty}:\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{A})\rightarrow\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{B})
and a quantum-limited amplifier
\mathcal{E}^{\rm amp}_{\alpha,\infty}:\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{B})\rightarrow\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{C})

Holevo 2007
, therefore one can use the composition rule in Eq.(
420
) to express the Stinespring dilation of
\mathcal{E}
in terms of the symplectic matrix

\displaystyle V^{U(1)}_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}=\lim_{\beta\rightarrow\infty}V^{\rm amp}_{\alpha,\beta}V^{\rm att}_{\alpha^{\prime},\beta}=V_{\rm 2sq}(\alpha)_{C\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muC\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}V_{\rm bs}\left(\frac{\pi}{2}\right)_{BC}V_{\rm bs}\left(-\alpha^{\prime}+\frac{\pi}{2}\right)_{AB}\,.

(435)
